measurementvaluewhere it comes from
the whole sweep30 (class, rung) cells; 26 worse, 4 better, 0 equal; mean +23.6%, worst +183.2%Six classes at rungs 1, 2, 3, 4 and 6; every class placed all 8 atoms of the cap and none reported improvement_exhausted, so every class contributes five sub-full rungs.
t2-straight-edge, the worst cell and the most quoted classn=1: prefix 1747.490731 against the one-atom fit's 617.022990, +183.2%; n=3: 402.939108 against 195.812376, +105.8%; n=2 +67.8%, n=4 +17.9%, n=6 +1.3%The monotone fall with n is the mechanism showing through: refine_tail = 3 re-tunes atom 0 at placements 2, 3 and 4, so it ends up tuned for a four-atom configuration and is then read as a one-atom answer.
the four cells that read bettert1-flat-fill n=6 -0.9%, t5-thin-stroke n=4 -6.9%, t5-thin-stroke n=6 -3.7%, t6-glyph-like n=6 -1.8%All at high n and all small. They refute a uniform-sign claim and they are why the criterion was written on the mean and the worst rather than on every cell.
the other four classest3-right-angle-corner worst +58.3% (n=1); t4-curve worst +68.7% (n=1); t5-thin-stroke worst +28.6% (n=2); t6-glyph-like worst +10.9% (n=4); t1-flat-fill worst +36.7% (n=3, on energies of 4e-6 where the ratio is large and the absolute difference is not)t1's cell is flagged deliberately: at a residual energy of four parts in a million a relative shortfall says little, and it is the one class where the number should not be quoted without its absolute.
what the harness does with these curvesharness.rs:583-589 sets first_pass to the SMALLEST rung whose prefix render meets the criterion, and that rung is the published atom cost of the classRead as an upper bound the published counts are safe; read as the fitter's actual cost they may be loose, because the n-atom fit renders better than the prefix that was judged. This experiment does not say by how many atoms: it measures residual energy, and the criterion is a different predicate.
the demo is not affectedsrc/bin/lights_demo.rs:1077-1092 sets request.max_atoms = count inside the count arm and renders outcome.atoms wholeSo the 12.99 / 16.05 / 20.99 dB at 1 / 2 / 4 atoms on t2-straight-edge quoted as the greedy baseline are refits, not prefixes, and they stand.
what it settled
  • THE SHORTFALL IS LARGEST WHERE THE ATOM COUNTS ARE READ. The published cost of a class is the first rung that passes, so it is decided at the low-n end of the curve, which is exactly where the prefix is worst: +183% on t2 at n=1, +68.7% on t4, +58.3% on t3.
  • THE SIGN IS NOT UNIFORM AND SAYING SO MATTERS. Four of thirty cells read better than their own fit, by under 7%. A claim that a prefix is always worse would have been refuted by this very sweep; the claim that survives is about the mean and the low-n end.
  • THE MECHANISM PREDICTS THE SHAPE. refine_tail = 3 re-tunes the three most recent earlier atoms after each placement, so atom 0 is tuned for a four-atom configuration and a one-atom read is the furthest from what it was tuned for. The measured shortfall falls monotonically with n on t2, t4 and t5, which is the prediction.
  • THE BLAST RADIUS IS THREE MODULES, NOT THE WHOLE CRATE. harness.rs (the quality sweep and the tiled field), nterm.rs (the rate-distortion ladder) and aspect2.rs (the N-term arm). harness.rs:697 takes the whole list and is fine, and the demo refits. Every figure quoted at the fitter's own stopping point is unaffected.
  • AND THE CORRECTION GOES ONE WAY. Since the prefix renders worse, a criterion crossing found on the prefix curve cannot be earlier than the true one. The published atom counts are therefore upper bounds — conservative rather than optimistic — which is the better direction to be wrong in and is not a reason to leave it.
  • LABELLED 2026-09-28 (L-142, PM ruling C): every reader now takes its slice through greedy::prefix, which returns the label with the slice; sweep.tsv gains a trailing reading column, NTermPoint and the aspect2 atom caption carry it, and PREFIX_SHORTFALL prints these numbers under any sweep with a prefix row. The previous finding's 'one way' holds in expectation only: 4 of the 30 cells above read BETTER, so a crossing read off a prefix curve is conservative on average, not a strict upper bound. No new measurement was run for the label; the numbers are this record's.
what it refuted
  • BURNDOWN L-132's premise that joint optimisation would DESTROY the prefix property: the property is not there to destroy, and was not before any optimiser was proposed.
  • The size implied by the first two-target probe, 15% to 41%. It understated the worst cell by 4.4x, because it ran t5 and t4 at depth 3 and the worst class is t2 at depth 8.
  • A uniform-sign claim. Four of thirty cells read better.
what it does NOT settle
  • Whether any published atom count actually moves. This measures residual energy; the harness picks a count by a criterion predicate (transition width, contrast, peak, FWHM), and a 183% energy shortfall need not move that predicate's crossing. Measuring it means re-running the sweep with per-rung refits, which is option (a) of the ruling L-142 is blocked on.
  • aspect2.rs:237 was not measured. It renders atoms[..terms.rendered] for the comparison against wavelets, and its arm and term accounting differ enough that the number here should not be carried over to it.
  • One kernel (gaussian), one settings profile (prefiltered), one tile size, each class at its own single viewport. Nothing says the shortfall is scale- or kernel-independent.
  • The fix. Three options are written into L-142 with their costs and it is BLOCKED on a PM scope call, because two of the three change every published figure.

Does a gradient fitter beat the coordinate ladder? Adam over the verified finite-difference gradient, against greedy, at equal atom count and equal render budget.

engineering2026-09-15copy-pasteable command3 refuted
the answer

No, and not narrowly: zero wins and six losses across all six content classes at four atoms and 1.06x the renders, from 2.4x behind on the right-angle corner to 8.7x behind on the straight edge. Two mechanisms, and only one of them is about tuning. The cost structure: one finite-difference gradient costs 18 renders against the ladder's 1 per trial, so at equal budget the ladder visits roughly eighteen times more candidate points and accepts each against the true loss rather than a model of it. The structural one: on t5-thin-stroke NO gain across a 40x sweep improves on the seed at all, while the ladder halves the energy, and the gradient there is not zero (|g| = 12.88) -- a non-zero gradient that leads immediately uphill, which is what L-131's support-edge straddle predicts. The rate sweep also refuses to choose: three classes, three different best gains, no plateau. Adam matches the ladder on exactly one class (flat fill, 0.19% behind), which is the guard that the implementation is not simply broken.

the question it was built to answer

BURNDOWN L-132. L-131 built and verified a finite-difference gradient and showed the ladder stops at points the gradient still descends from. Does an optimiser built on that gradient produce a better answer than the ladder at equal atom count and equal cost, on the classes P2 reported the fitter stopping early on -- which is 22 of 24 cells, every class except the right-angle corner under normalised blending?

criterion, and its convention

Written into L-132 before the build. The two fitters must be the SAME fitter with one part swapped, so descent::fit carries greedy's signature and FitOutcome, greedy's seeding (seed_pixel then seed_atom_scaled), greedy's densification, greedy's refine_tail pass and greedy's improvement_exhausted rule, with only the inner search replaced; it must bill its own Budget site. Comparison: four atoms, all six classes, gaussian, SharedSettings::prefiltered, tile 16, truth from exact_coverage at depth 10; a class whose two fitters place different atom counts is REPORTED and excluded rather than compared, and at least four must remain. The render-budget ratio must sit within [0.9, 1.25] or the comparison is about budgets and not searches. The bar L-132 set was that descent is not worse than greedy at equal atom count.

reproduce it
cargo test -p lights --all-features --test fit_descent -- --test-threads 1 --nocapture

Run 2026-09-15 on the M5 Max: 10 passed in 17.9 s. The comparison table is printed by descent_fit_matches_greedys_contract_and_is_compared_at_equal_atom_count; the rate sweep and the seed gradients by the_rate_sweep_finds_no_single_gain_and_one_class_where_no_gain_moves_at_all. All figures are residual energies and render counts, deterministic, not timings.
what it measured
measurementvaluewhere it comes from
the comparison, four atoms, equal budget0 wins, 6 losses. t1-flat-fill 0.000002 vs 0.000011; t2-straight-edge 97.744577 vs 848.947832; t3-right-angle-corner 268.776265 vs 636.289160; t4-curve 3.721167 vs 29.380185; t5-thin-stroke 4.875402 vs 24.964828; t6-glyph-like 11.090632 vs 29.201712 (greedy first)Renders 25968 greedy against 27408 descent over the six classes, a ratio of 1.06, asserted to stay inside [0.9, 1.25]. The bar L-132 set is NOT met and the entry stays open at state partial.
the cost structure, which is the part that is not about tuning18 renders per gradient against 1 render per ladder trialRENDERS_PER_GRADIENT = 2 * P, asserted in a_gradient_costs_eighteen_renders_and_bills_its_own_site. The ladder's ±delta trial is one evaluate() call. At equal budget the ladder therefore visits about eighteen times more candidate points, and each is accepted or rejected against the true loss.
t5-thin-stroke: no gain in a 40x range moves at allseed energy 43.679697 at every one of the six gains 0.05, 0.125, 0.25, 0.5, 1.0, 2.0; the ladder reaches 23.106238 from the same seedAnd |g| at that seed is 1.288236e1, the largest of the three classes swept, so this is not a flat spot. A non-zero gradient whose direction is uphill in the true loss is exactly what the support-edge straddle of experiment l131-finite-difference-gradient predicts for a 15 px long, third-of-a-pixel-thin atom.
the rate sweep chooses nothingbest gain 2.0 on t1-flat-fill (tied with 1.0), 0.5 on t2-straight-edge, and none on t5-thin-stroke; on t2 the gain of 1.0 is WORSE than 0.5 (1924.98 against 1568.29), so the sweep is not even monotoneADAM_RATE = 1.0 is set as the best row on the one class where Adam matches the ladder, and the test asserts the three classes disagree so that no future context reads a consensus into it.
the guard that the implementation is not simply brokent1-flat-fill: Adam reaches 4.311602 against the ladder's 4.303493, 0.19% behind, from a seed at 12280.27Asserted within 1% by the rate-sweep test. Adam can match the ladder on a class whose loss is smooth at the scale of the atom; it is where the loss is not that it cannot.
what the first version got wrong, and what fixed itwith no decay schedule Adam could not converge on the flat fill (0.015068 against the ladder's 0.000002, a factor of 7386); with the ladder's own eight-level halving schedule it reaches 0.000011Adam's step is normalised by the gradient magnitude, so it does not shrink near a minimum. AdamState::decay() walks ADAM_DECAY_LEVELS = 8 distinct rates over the step budget, opening at the full rate for reach and closing at 2^-7 for precision, and a mutation that removes the decay is CAUGHT.
what it settled
  • A GRADIENT FITTER DOES NOT AUTOMATICALLY BEAT A COORDINATE LADDER ON THIS LOSS, AND THE FIRST REASON IS ARITHMETIC. A finite difference costs two renders per parameter, so the gradient method buys one direction for the price of eighteen trials. The ladder spends the same budget on eighteen trials and keeps whichever actually lowered the true loss. Direction quality has to beat an 18:1 sampling disadvantage before it wins anything.
  • THE SECOND REASON IS THE LOSS, NOT THE OPTIMISER. On the thin stroke the gradient is large and useless: six gains over a 40x range all return the seed unchanged. The linearisation is valid over a radius smaller than any step worth taking, because the alpha cutoff makes the loss piecewise-smooth at pixel scale. This is the same mechanism that made eps = 1e-4 a spike in L-131's step sweep, arriving at the level of the search instead of the level of the derivative.
  • THE RATE SWEEP REFUSING TO CHOOSE IS ITSELF THE RESULT. Three classes chose three different gains and t2's response is not monotone in the gain. A method whose one hyperparameter has no cross-class plateau is not ready to replace a method that has none at all.
  • IT WORKS WHERE THE LOSS IS SMOOTH. Flat fill, 0.19% behind the ladder. That is what makes the other five losses a statement about the loss surface rather than about the code.
  • AND L-131's OWN RESULT SURVIVES INTACT AND MEANS LESS THAN IT LOOKED. The ladder really does stop at points the gradient descends from -- that was measured and still holds. What this experiment adds is that reaching those points the ladder's way is worth more than following the gradient from the start.
what it refuted
  • L-132's close bar as stated -- 'the descent answer is not worse than greedy's at equal atom count'. Measured: worse on six of six.
  • The implicit expectation carried from docs/plan/TWO_HORIZON_PLAN.md:482-501 that building a real fitter is the move that closes the gap between this crate's atom counts and the published ones. The naive real fitter opens a gap rather than closing one, so L-43's measurement cannot be run against descent::fit as it stands.
  • Adam as textbook Adam. Without a per-parameter rate it steps colour in pixel units; without a decay schedule it cannot converge (7386x behind on the flat fill). Both are CAUGHT mutations, so neither can quietly come back.
what it does NOT settle
  • An ANALYTIC gradient. Every cost figure here rests on the 18:1 finite-difference price, and a closed-form derivative of the splat loss with respect to the nine parameters would delete that term entirely. Nothing in this experiment says what happens then, and it is the obvious next move.
  • A smoothed loss. The thin-stroke failure is caused by the alpha cutoff's hard support edge; a cutoff-free or antialiased loss might make the gradient usable there. Not built, not measured.
  • Joint optimisation over all atoms. descent::fit optimises one atom at a time, exactly as greedy does, so this compares two inner searches and not two optimisation regimes. The four-atoms-that-are-best-together question is untouched.
  • More steps. ADAM_STEPS = 24 was chosen to land the render budget beside greedy's; whether Adam wins at 10x the budget is a different question and a different table.
  • Every figure is at four atoms on one viewport per class with one kernel. The comparison was not run at the atom counts the published curves use.

How many times is the render inner loop compiled into the tree? The monomorphisation budget the crate said to watch from the first build, measured for the first time.

engineering2026-09-16copy-pasteable command4 refuted
the answer

96, not 8. src/lib_rs_comment/seam_costs.md published 8 instantiations of each inner loop over the axes (T, K, Blend, Probe) = 2x1x2x2, and warned that four kernels and five methods would take it to 80. The real axes are (T, K, Probe) once per method -- Blend is K::Blend, an associated type, and multiplies nothing -- so today it is 2 x 8 x 3 = 48 per method and 96 across the two built methods. The 80 offered as the alarming future ceiling was already exceeded on the day it was written, by 20%, with a tree that has two methods and not five.

the question it was built to answer

BURNDOWN L-67. src/lib_rs_comment/seam_costs.md:44-50 names code size as 'the cost that IS real', projects 8 instantiations growing to 80, says the per-method cargo features exist so a developer can cut the tree, and ends 'Watch this from the first build rather than discovering it later.' No instrument ever did: there is no size assertion in tests/layering.rs, no CI, and lights-bench reports time and counters only. What is the number, and does the published projection hold?

criterion, and its convention

The script answers the question twice from independent places and must agree. MEASURED: built artefacts are scanned byte-for-byte for the mangled render_tile symbol (_ZN6lights6method5splat7execute11render_tile and the raycast equivalent), de-duplicated by full symbol name, maximum taken over shipping binaries; no external tool is used, because a sandboxed session can run neither nm nor c++filt. PREDICTED: impl .. Kernel&lt;T&gt; for and impl .. Probe&lt;T&gt; for counted from src/, times the length of Precision::ALL read out of src/dispatch.rs. Nothing is hardcoded on either side. Exit 0 only when they agree; exit 3 when no shipping binary carries the symbol at all, so an empty target directory refuses rather than reporting zero. CONVENTION: the verdict is taken over shipping binaries only -- two test binaries carry 49 because tests/splat_projection.rs:163 and tests/raycast_math.rs:179 each define a test-local ninth kernel by macro, which is correct on both sides and not a defect.

reproduce it
python3 crates/lights/scripts/monomorphisation_budget.py --target-dir <TARGET>

Run 2026-09-16 on an Apple M5 Max (18 cores, 128 GB, macOS 15) against a target directory built with:

  cargo build -p lights --release --all-features --bin lights-bench --target-dir <TARGET>
The RAM disk being 100% full (L-145) forced <TARGET> to a repo-local scratch path rather than /Volumes/RamBuild/cargo-target; the script's default is the RAM disk and the measurement does not depend on which is used.

RE-RUN 2026-09-29 (L-199), AND THE INSTRUMENT WAS WRONG, NOT THE FIGURE. Against the on-disk target/ the command first exited 1: MEASURED 100, MISMATCH 50 against 48 per method. Every debug shipping binary read 48; the 50 came from a release lights-demo, whose crate calls Renderer::<f32, Gaussian> itself, and an optimised build does not share generic instantiations across crates, so the bin carried copies of ones the library holds. Release counts move both ways (lights-bench reads 4 and lights-p2..p5 24 in release, because inlining removes symbols), so they cannot vote. Fixed in the same entry: the verdict is read off debug shipping binaries only, and a directory with none is refused. Re-run: 96 over 10 debug shipping binaries, agree. crates/lights/tests/monomorphisation_budget.rs holds the regression.

The published 8 was a correct measurement of the least representative artefact in the tree.

the factors, counted from source8 `Kernel<T> for` impls (CompactBump, Gaussian, GaussianNormalised, GaborColour, GaborDensity, SuperGaussian, GaussHermiteColour, GaussHermiteDensity); 3 `Probe<T> for` impls (NoProbe, CounterProbe, AuditProbe); Precision::ALL has 2. 8 x 3 x 2 = 48.KernelFamily::ALL is 18, but that enumerates kernel CONFIGURATIONS, not Rust types; the monomorphisation axis is the type count, 8.
artefact sizes, whole-file bytesrelease lights-bench 1,194,144 B; debug lights-bench 5,618,816 B; release liblights rlib 18,321,456 B; debug shipping binaries 42.4-43.5 MB each.Comparable only within one profile and one feature set. L-67's recorded baseline of 9,089,984 B for release liblights.rlib came from a target directory whose feature set is not recorded, so NO growth figure may be quoted from that pair.
what it settled
  • A PUBLISHED FIGURE CAN BE A CORRECT MEASUREMENT OF THE WRONG ARTEFACT, AND THAT IS HARDER TO CATCH THAN AN ARITHMETIC SLIP. The 8 in seam_costs.md is exactly what lights-bench contains; anyone re-running the check on the binary named in the evidence line would confirm it and move on. The error is that lights-bench instantiates one kernel family where the rest of the tree instantiates eight, so it under-reports by 12x. This is the same shape as L-42 (a test whose numbers looked right while measuring a quantity dominated by something else) and another instance of the crate's standing lesson that instruments lie.
  • THE PER-METHOD CARGO FEATURES ARE THE ONLY AXIS THAT CUTS THE TREE, AND THEY CUT IT EXACTLY IN HALF. T, K and Probe are shared by both methods, so --no-default-features --features method-splat removes precisely 48 instantiations and nothing else. The prunability argument seam_costs.md makes for those features is therefore correct, and it is worth more than the document claimed: it is 50% of the inner-loop code, not one method out of a projected five.
  • THE ALARMING PROJECTION WAS ALREADY EXCEEDED WHEN IT WAS WRITTEN. 80 was offered as where the crate would arrive after adding three kernels and three methods. The tree was at 96 with two methods. A ceiling that the tree is already above cannot function as a warning, which is why nothing was ever done about the advice in the same paragraph.
  • THE INSTRUMENT MUST BE ABLE TO FAIL ON BOTH SIDES OF A COMPARISON. This script compares a measurement against a prediction, and both halves were falsified before any figure was published: empty target directory refuses (exit 3); a ninth Kernel impl appended to a copy of src/ moves the prediction 48 to 54; a Probe impl renamed away moves it 48 to 32; a target directory holding only lights-bench moves the measurement 48 to 4. An instrument that can only fail on one side is half an instrument.
what it refuted
  • src/lib_rs_comment/seam_costs.md:46, 'Every render path is monomorphised over (T, K, Blend, Probe)'. Blend is K::Blend, an associated type at src/kernel/mod.rs:421; render_tile takes three type parameters.
  • src/lib_rs_comment/seam_costs.md:46-47, 'Today that is 2 x 1 x 2 x 2 = 8 instantiations of each inner loop.' Measured 48 per inner loop, 96 across both methods.
  • src/lib_rs_comment/seam_costs.md:47-48, 'Adding three kernels and three methods takes it to 2 x 4 x 5 x 2 = 80.' The tree was at 96 before any of that was added.
  • L-67's own evidence line, 'nm lights-bench | grep render_tile -> 8 symbols', as a statement about the tree. It is a true statement about lights-bench and a 12x under-count of the tree.
what it does NOT settle
  • WHETHER THE COUNT DESERVES A CEILING, which is the second half of L-67's next. The number now exists and moves with the tree, but no threshold has been argued for and this experiment does not supply one. A ceiling asserted without a cost model is a number nobody can lose against, which is the failure the entry itself names.
  • WHAT 96 INSTANTIATIONS COST IN BYTES. The script reports whole-artefact sizes, which in debug are dominated by debug info and in release are dominated by everything else linked in. Isolating the __TEXT contribution of the render_tile family needs a section-level tool this sandboxed session cannot run (nm and size both require approval), so the code-size half of 'the cost that IS real' is still unmeasured.
  • WHETHER THE RELEASE RLIB HAS GROWN. 9,089,984 B is on record and 18,321,456 B was measured today, but the recorded figure's feature set is unknown and --all-features turns on fit. The pair is not a time series and must not be read as one.
  • WHETHER THE MEASURED COUNT TRACKS THE DETACHED BUILDS. The prediction halves under --no-default-features --features method-splat by construction, and the script handles a one-method tree, but no detached build was scanned in this session -- a second feature set is the last thing a context does and this one spent its build budget recovering from a full RAM disk.

How wide is the shipped render inner loop? The vectorisation premise behind the crate's static-dispatch design, counted over all 99 render bodies instead of one.

engineering2026-09-16copy-pasteable command3 refuted
the answer

The loops ARE vectorised and it is the wrong width. Across all 99 release render bodies: splat 14 four-wide and 346 two-wide against 3144 scalar FP; raycast 6 four-wide and 1112 two-wide against 7104 scalar FP. Wide vector arithmetic is 10.3% / 13.6% of floating-point work, but PIXEL-PARALLEL width -- the thing the design argues static dispatch exists to preserve -- is 0.1%-0.4%: twenty 4-wide instructions out of 10,600. The 2-wide majority is the compiler pairing an (x, y) coordinate, which it does for free and which no dispatch decision protects. So the design's stated payoff is essentially unrealised, and L-68's own claim that the loops are SCALAR is also false: 1,458 wide vector arithmetic instructions exist and only 3 of 99 bodies have none.

the question it was built to answer

BURNDOWN L-68. src/lib_rs_comment/seam_costs.md:31-33 gives vectorisability as reason 2 of 3 for the crate's static-dispatch architecture -- 'an indirect call is an optimisation barrier, and the loss is the vectorisation, not the call'. Nobody had checked. L-68 then checked ONE monomorphisation by disassembly and reported zero vector arithmetic. What is the number over the whole tree, and does either claim survive?

criterion, and its convention

Written before the count. Instructions are classified from emitted assembly text into six buckets and the WIDTH buckets are kept apart, because collapsing them answers the wrong question: .2s/.2d is two lanes and is almost always (x, y) coordinate pairing the compiler does for free, while .4s is the pixel-parallel width the design clause is about. Moves (mov.16b, ld1.s), shuffles (ext.8b, zip1.4s) and single-lane forms are excluded from arithmetic. CONVENTION: 'wide share' is (4-wide + 2-wide) / (4-wide + 2-wide + scalar FP), over function bodies of at least 50 instructions. The script exits 3 rather than reporting zero when the needle matches no symbol, or matches symbols with no floating-point work of either kind.

reproduce it
cargo build -p lights --release --all-features --bin lights-demo --target-dir <DIR> --config 'build.rustflags=["--emit","asm"]'
python3 crates/lights/scripts/vector_mix.py <DIR>/release/deps/lights-<hash>.s --needle "splat..implementation..SplatRaster" --min-instructions 50
python3 crates/lights/scripts/vector_mix.py <DIR>/release/deps/lights-<hash>.s --needle "raycast..implementation..RayCast" --min-instructions 50

Run 2026-09-16 on an Apple M5 Max (18 cores, 128 GB, macOS 15), aarch64-apple-darwin, release profile. L-68's own recorded command (objdump --disassemble-symbols=...) could NOT be run: nm, objdump and size all require an approval a non-interactive session cannot obtain, and the binary it names had been evicted from the RAM disk (see L-145).

RE-RUN 2026-09-29 (L-199) into a fresh scratch target directory. Splat reproduces exactly: 14 four-wide, 346 two-wide, 3144 scalar. The ray caster has moved since this record: 6 four-wide as recorded, but 1088 two-wide against 1112 and 7279 scalar against 7104, because its bodies changed after it (the exact silhouette binning, the shading and the composite work). The pixel-parallel share this record's verdict rests on still reads 0.1%-0.4%; the raycast two-wide and scalar counts above are history.
what it measured
measurementvaluewhere it comes from
splat, all 51 render bodies4-wide 14, 2-wide 346, scalar FP 3144. Wide share 10.3%, 4-wide share 0.4%. Bodies with zero wide vector arithmetic: 3 of 51.The 4-wide count is 14 instructions in a body population totalling over 18,000 instructions.
raycast, all 48 render bodies4-wide 6, 2-wide 1112, scalar FP 7104. Wide share 13.6%, 4-wide share 0.1%. Bodies with zero wide vector arithmetic: 0 of 48.Not one raycast body is fully scalar, which is the direct refutation of L-68's evidence line.
what the vector instructions actually aresplat: fmul.2s 98, fmul.2d 79, fadd.2s 62, fadd.2d 51, fsub.2s 28, fsub.2d 28, fmul.4s 7, fadd.4s 5, fsub.4s 2. raycast: fmul.2s 492, fadd.2s 324, fsub.2s 192, fadd.2d 41, fmul.2d 39, fneg.2s 24, fmul.4s 3, fadd.4s 3.Real arithmetic, overwhelmingly 2-lane. This is the evidence for reading the 2-wide majority as coordinate pairing rather than pixel parallelism.
the control crates in the same buildpng 72.5% wide, miniz_oxide 77.1%, adler2 94.6%; syn, quote, rayon, bitflags, crossbeam_deque all 0. lights overall 18.9%.Instrument validation. A classifier that cannot separate checksum code from a macro crate is measuring nothing.
the bug the instrument had in its first versionsplat read 446 vector-compute where the true figure is 360 (18% inflation); raycast read 1342 against 1118.The lane regex listed only multi-lane specs, so single-element mov.s, mov.d, ld1.s, st1.s fell through to 'vector compute', and shuffles were counted as arithmetic. The first numbers looked entirely plausible.
what it settled
  • THE DESIGN CLAUSE AND ITS REBUTTAL WERE BOTH WRONG, AND FOR THE SAME REASON. seam_costs.md claimed static dispatch preserves vectorisation; L-68 claimed the loops are scalar with ZERO vector arithmetic. The truth is that the loops vectorise 2 lanes wide almost everywhere and 4 lanes wide essentially nowhere. Both errors came from reading a single monomorphisation as the tree -- the clause from none at all, the rebuttal from one of 99. This is the second entry in two sittings where that exact confound produced a published number; see L-67, which found the same shape in the paragraph directly below this one in the same file.
  • TWO-WIDE IS NOT A PAYOFF, IT IS THE COMPILER PAIRING (x, y). fmul.2s and fadd.2s dominate, on a loop whose data is 2D screen coordinates. Nothing about static dispatch is needed to obtain it and nothing about dynamic dispatch would obviously lose it. The clause is only meaningful about 4-wide pixel-parallel work, and that is 20 instructions in 10,600.
  • AN INSTRUMENT COUNTING 'VECTOR INSTRUCTIONS' ANSWERS THE WRONG QUESTION BY DEFAULT. Merging 2-wide and 4-wide gives 10-14%, which reads as 'partially vectorised' and would have supported the design clause. Separating them gives 0.1-0.4%, which refutes it. The bucket boundary WAS the result, and it had to be chosen before the count to avoid choosing it afterwards.
  • INLINING MOVES THE THING YOU WANT TO MEASURE. In release, render_tile has no body: it is inlined into the per-tile closure of SplatRaster::render and RayCast::render. A search for render_tile in release assembly returns nothing, and reading that absence as an error, or as 'the function was optimised away', would be wrong in a way that looks like a finding.
what it refuted
  • BURNDOWN L-68's title, 'the shipped inner loops are scalar'. 1,458 wide vector arithmetic instructions across 99 render bodies; 96 of 99 bodies contain at least one; every raycast body contains at least one.
  • L-68's evidence line, 'FOUR SIMD-suffixed instructions, all of them mov.16b register moves, and ZERO vector arithmetic'. True of the one symbol disassembled, false of the other 98 bodies.
  • src/lib_rs_comment/seam_costs.md:31-33 as a statement about realised benefit, 'the loss is the vectorisation, not the call'. The pixel-parallel vectorisation it protects is 0.1%-0.4% of floating-point work, so there is almost nothing there to lose.
what it does NOT settle
  • WHETHER AN ATOM-OUTER / 4-PIXELS-INNER RESTRUCTURING WOULD BE FASTER. This experiment counts instructions and says nothing about time. It gives that experiment a baseline it did not have, and nothing more. Raised as its own entry because it collides with sample_pixel_reproduces_the_tile_render_bit_for_bit, which pixel_loop.md says forbids the incremental whitening step.
  • WHETHER STATIC DISPATCH IS STILL JUSTIFIED. Reason 2 of 3 is now measured and weak, but reasons 1 (compile-time illegality via Kernel::Blend) and 3 (const folding of ORDER_DEPENDENT) are untouched by this and were never the ones in doubt. Nothing here argues for dyn dispatch.
  • WHERE THE TIME GOES. Static instruction counts are not a profile. A body that is 90% scalar by count may spend its time in the other 10%, and this crate's measurement rules require five reps and a spread before any speed claim.
  • ANY ARCHITECTURE BUT THIS ONE. aarch64-apple-darwin, release, one machine. NEON is 128-bit, so 4-wide is the f32 maximum here; on a target with wider vectors the same source could report a different shape entirely.
artefacts on disk

What does it cost to turn a rendered frame into bytes a GPU will accept? The CPU half of the never-measured CPU-to-screen handoff, at 1080p.

engineering2026-09-16copy-pasteable command2 refuted
the answer

7.444 ms median in RELEASE at 1920x1080 -- 45% of a 16.67 ms frame budget, spent before a single byte reaches the GPU. Against the crate's published render timings this is not a correction but a reversal of scale: P2 publishes 38.9 us and lights-bench's clear column is 0.58 ms, so the pack is roughly 13x the render column it would sit beside. And the handoff is worse than one term: Image::pixels is Vec<[u8; 3]> while every GPU surface format is four bytes per pixel, so an RGB-to-RGBA expansion over 2,073,600 pixels is also required and NOTHING IN THE CRATE PERFORMS IT.

the question it was built to answer

BURNDOWN L-69 asks what the CPU-to-screen handoff costs, and says no timing in this crate is yet an end-to-end frame time. The question has two halves: the GPU upload, which needs a window, and the CPU pack, which needs nothing but the crate. L-69 was SPLIT on 2026-09-16 and this is the half that could be measured. It also tests L-69's own description of the bridge as 'a few lines of ColorImage copy'.

criterion, and its convention

Five reps minimum with the spread reported and a 1.3x difference treated as noise, per this crate's standing measurement rules -- single-thread wall time on this machine has a 2.4x spread. The pack is timed with std::time::Instant around FloatImage<f32>::to_clamped_u8 at 1920x1080, and the RESULT IS VALIDATED BEFORE IT IS QUOTED: all three channels are checked against the ramp the fixture wrote, at four positions including two edges, tolerance one quantisation step. That check is not decoration -- see the numbers table.

reproduce it
cargo test -p lights --release --all-features --test handoff_pack -- --nocapture --test-threads 1

Run 2026-09-16 on an Apple M5 Max (18 cores, 128 GB, macOS 15). The same test under the DEFAULT test profile reads 30.4 ms median; always quote the profile.
what it measured
measurementvaluewhere it comes from
release, 1920x1080, five repsmin 6.750 ms, median 7.444 ms, max 10.158 ms. Spread 1.50x.45% of a 16.67 ms frame budget. Single-threaded: to_clamped_u8 is a serial iterator.
the same test, default test profilemin 30.318 ms, median 30.430 ms, max 36.253 ms. Spread 1.20x.4x the release figure and nearly twice the whole frame budget. Either number alone is defensible; the pair is what makes it honest.
against what this crate already publishespack 7.444 ms vs P2's 38.9 us render and lights-bench's 0.58 ms clear column.Roughly 13x the clear column and ~190x the P2 figure. No published timing in the register contains this term.
payload6,220,800 B per frame as RGB, 8,294,400 B as RGBA. At 60 Hz, 373 MB/s and 498 MB/s.The RGBA figure is what a GPU surface actually wants and is 33% more traffic than the crate's own format.
the structural gap`[u8; 4]` does not appear in src/image.rs at all; Image::pixels is Vec<[u8; 3]> at src/image.rs:352.So the RGB-to-RGBA expansion is a third term, unwritten and untimed. L-69's 'a few lines of ColorImage copy' understates it.
what it settled
  • THE FIRST PRESENTATION TERM EVER MEASURED IN THIS CRATE IS LARGER THAN EVERY RENDER TERM IT WOULD ACCOMPANY. 7.444 ms of pack against a 0.58 ms clear column and a 38.9 us P2 render. The CPU-renderer premise has been priced entirely on the cheap side of the joint, and the expensive side is not the GPU upload that L-69 worried about -- it is the byte conversion sitting in front of it.
  • A SHAPE-ONLY ASSERTION CANNOT GUARD A TIMING, AND THE ERROR FLATTERS THE RESULT. The first version of the test asserted length, width and height and then timed the pack. A mutation that stops quantising the blue channel PASSED it, at full speed, because a pack that drops a third of its work is still exactly the right shape and is faster. A timing test must assert the work happened, and it must assert every part of it: checking two channels would have missed this by the same mechanism. Compare L-42, where a test measured a quantity dominated by something else and passed beautifully.
  • A BREAK THAT DOES NOT COMPILE HAS TESTED NOTHING. The structural guard was first broken by adding a FIELD of type Vec<[u8; 4]> to Image, which failed with E0063 at three call sites -- a compile error, not a red test, and therefore no evidence at all. Re-broken with pub type RgbaBytes = Vec&lt;[u8; 4]&gt;;, which compiles and fails the test with its own message. This is the same trap the mutation manifest documents for rows that fail to apply.
  • ALWAYS QUOTE THE PROFILE ON A PACK. 30.4 ms debug against 7.444 ms release is a 4x gap on the same code and the same machine, and both are the kind of number that gets pasted into a table without its header. The debug figure alone would say the handoff is impossible; the release figure alone would hide how much of the result is the optimiser.
what it refuted
  • BURNDOWN L-69's why, 'The unmeasured quantity is not the bridge code, which is a few lines of ColorImage copy'. The bridge code is a per-pixel expansion over 2,073,600 pixels that does not exist in the crate, sitting on top of a 7.444 ms pack that does.
  • The implicit reading of every published timing in this register as a frame cost. P2's 38.9 us and lights-bench's 0.58 ms omit a term that is 13x to 190x their size.
what it does NOT settle
  • THE GPU UPLOAD, which is the other half of L-69 and stays there. It needs a session that can open a surface; a headless claude -p context cannot, and building eframe/wgpu cold costs gigabytes before the first frame.
  • WHETHER 7.444 ms IS IRREDUCIBLE, and this experiment explicitly does NOT claim it is. to_clamped_u8 is a serial iterator over 2M pixels and the crate already depends on rayon, so a par_iter would plausibly cut it several-fold. Nobody has tried. The pack also allocates a fresh 6.22 MB Vec per call, which a reusable buffer would avoid.
  • THE COST OF THE RGBA EXPANSION, which was not timed because it does not exist to time.
  • WHETHER THE PACK IS ON THE CRITICAL PATH AT ALL in a real host, which depends on whether presentation overlaps rendering -- a question about the host's frame structure, not about lights.
artefacts on disk

Can lights drive a game? The first frame rate ever measured in this crate, at 1920x1080 over an atom-count ladder, both methods, both precisions.

engineering2026-09-16copy-pasteable command3 refuted
the answer

SPLAT YES, RAY-CAST NO, and the atom count is the axis that decides it. At 1920x1080 on 18 cores, splat holds a 16.67 ms (60 fps) frame to somewhere between 10k and 100k atoms -- 4.858 ms at 10k, 23.923 ms at 100k -- while the ray caster is 15.590 ms at 10k and 183.551 ms at 100k, which is 5.4 fps. Adding L-150's 7.444 ms CPU pack term, which no figure here contains, moves the honest 60 fps ceiling for splat down to roughly 30k atoms and puts the ray caster outside 60 fps at every rung including 10k. THE SHARPEST RESULT IS NOT THE RATE: at 100k atoms splat's frame is 76% BIND (18.187 ms) against 4.248 ms of render, so the render column -- the only column this crate has ever published -- is the SMALL term at a game-like atom count.

the question it was built to answer

BURNDOWN L-123: every published timing in this crate is microseconds for one render of a 64x64 field, and a game engine is judged on a sustained rate at a real resolution with a real atom count. What is the frame time at 1920x1080 over 1k / 10k / 100k atoms, for both implemented methods and both precisions, and what content budget does it imply?

criterion, and its convention

The convention is a 16.67 ms budget = 60 frames per second, and 33.3 ms = 30 fps, applied to the bench's FRAME column -- which is measured by its own clock around the whole per-frame block, not summed from the phase columns beside it. Five reps minimum with the spread reported and a 1.3x difference treated as noise, per this crate's standing measurement rules; this run used 9. MIN is the robust latency statistic and is quoted beside every mean. THE FRAME COLUMN IS NOT AN END-TO-END FRAME: it contains build, bind, clear and render, and it does NOT contain the CPU pack term or any GPU upload, so every rate below is an UPPER BOUND on what a real pipeline would sustain.

reproduce it
cargo run -p lights --release --all-features --bin lights-bench --target-dir target -- --out-dir target/l123

Run 2026-09-16 on an Apple M5 Max (18 cores, 128 GB, macOS 15), release profile. The 1080p atom ladder and the resolution axis are BUILT INTO the binary and need no flags -- lights-bench has only --out-dir, --smoke and --quick, so L-123's own prescription of 'lights-bench at 1920x1080 over an atom-count ladder' is issued by passing nothing. 9 repetitions per cell, first discarded as warm-up, mean +- 95% CI with min reported alongside. Report committed at docs/lights/benchmarks/l123/report.tsv.
what it measured
measurementvaluewhere it comes from
splat-raster f32, 1920x1080, frame ms (mean +- 95% CI over 8 kept reps; min)1k 1.6347+-0.0919 (min 1.442); 10k 4.8583+-0.2343 (min 4.433); 100k 23.9234+-0.1555 (min 23.669)= 612 / 206 / 41.8 fps at the mean. Only the 100k rung misses 60 fps, and it still clears 30.
ray-cast f32, 1920x1080, frame ms1k 1.9189+-0.0636 (min 1.807); 10k 15.5900+-0.5383 (min 14.657); 100k 183.5509+-1.3907 (min 181.128)= 521 / 64.1 / 5.4 fps. 10k only just clears 60 fps and does not once the pack term is added.
splat-raster f64 and ray-cast f64, 1920x1080, frame mssplat 1k 1.4208+-0.0488, 10k 5.2723+-0.3453, 100k 24.7522+-0.4647; ray-cast 1k 2.0851+-0.1715, 10k 16.3596+-0.7450, 100k 196.2264+-1.2953DOUBLE PRECISION IS NEARLY FREE IN TIME AND NOT IN MEMORY. The bench calls f32-vs-f64 TOO CLOSE TO CALL at 1k and 10k for both methods; the only separated rungs are 100k, at 1.03x (splat) and 1.07x (ray-cast). Resident memory doubles: 58.59 -> 109.92 MiB (splat) and 58.15 -> 114.44 MiB (ray-cast) at 100k.
THE BIND TERM AT 100k ATOMS, splat-raster, 1920x1080bind 18.1867+-0.0627 ms against render 4.2482+-0.1661 ms, in a 23.9234 ms frame76% of the frame is projection and sorting. The bench flags this itself: 'bind EXCEEDS render - this row measures projection and sorting, not rasterisation'. Same at f64: bind 18.5688 vs render 4.6930.
the ray caster's gap widens with resolution, at 10k atoms f32 (render ms)256x256 0.8415 vs 2.5875 (3.07x); 960x540 1.6149 vs 7.9198 (4.90x); 1920x1080 2.4616 vs 13.1851 (5.36x)Deterministic and portable confirmation in the same run: keval/ms at 1080p is 1,245,308 for splat against 258,458 for ray-cast, a 4.8x marginal-work ratio that does not depend on this machine's clock.
END-TO-END ARITHMETIC, combining this run's frame column with L-150's measured 7.444 ms packsplat f32 10k 12.30 ms = 81 fps; splat f32 100k 31.37 ms = 32 fps; ray-cast f32 10k 23.03 ms = 43 fps; ray-cast f32 100k 191.0 ms = 5.2 fpsARITHMETIC, NOT A MEASUREMENT -- the two terms were timed in separate runs and no single clock has ever spanned both. The 60 fps ceiling for splat at 1080p interpolates to roughly 30k atoms on a bind term that is near-linear in atom count (0.18 us/atom between the 10k and 100k rungs); that interpolation is arithmetic too.
what it settled
  • THE FIRST FRAME RATE IN THIS CRATE'S HISTORY, AND IT SAYS THE UI TARGET WAS NEVER IN DOUBT AND THE GAME TARGET IS A BUDGET RATHER THAN A YES OR NO. 612 fps at 1k atoms and 206 fps at 10k means an IDE node graph redrawing on interaction is not close to a limit. 41.8 fps at 100k means a game scene is bounded by atom count, not by resolution, and L-120's ruling now has a number attached to it.
  • THE PUBLISHED COLUMN IS THE WRONG COLUMN AT GAME SCALE. Every timing this crate has published is a RENDER time, and at 100k atoms splat's render is 4.248 ms inside a 23.923 ms frame -- 18% of it. A reader extrapolating any P2 or P5 render figure to a frame budget would be out by 5.6x for splat at 100k. This is not a correction to a published number, which stands as a render time; it is a correction to what a render time MEANS.
  • BIND, NOT RASTERISATION, IS WHAT WOULD HAVE TO BE OPTIMISED FOR A DENSE SCENE. The 18.187 ms bind at 100k atoms is projection and sorting, it is 4.3x the render beside it, and no entry in this register is about it. It is also the term a real game would pay every frame for a moving camera, so it cannot be amortised away by the frames-per-build axis.
  • THE METHOD AXIS HAS A CHAMPION ON THIS TARGET, MEASURED RATHER THAN ASSUMED. Splat is 11.8x faster than the ray caster at 100k atoms at 1080p and 4.8x better per kernel evaluation by the portable counter. The three axes exist so a champion can be measured and the losers pruned; this is the first end-to-end evidence on the method axis, and it points one way. PRUNING A METHOD IS A PM RULING and is not taken here.
  • PRECISION IS NOT A TIME AXIS AT 1080p. f32 versus f64 is TOO CLOSE TO CALL at every rung but 100k, where it is 1.03x-1.07x -- inside the 1.3x this crate treats as noise. What double precision costs is memory: 2x resident, 114 MiB at 100k atoms. Anyone choosing f32 for speed at this resolution is choosing it for nothing.
what it refuted
  • BURNDOWN L-123's OWN EVIDENCE, on one line: "grep -rn &#x27;fps|frames_per_second&#x27; src/ docs/lights/ returns nothing". It does not any more -- src/bin/lights_demo.rs:1402 prints 'median {ms} = {n} fps', added when L-128 built the demo. The entry's CONCLUSION survived because the demo's rate is at its own frame size, f32 only, over demo_object()'s atom count, and never at 1080p; but the evidence line was stale and is withdrawn.
  • BURNDOWN L-123's PREREQUISITE, "with the sweep parallelism from L-96 landed first, since a frame that takes a second single-threaded may be fine across eighteen cores". This conflates two different parallelisms. L-96 is the FIT job sweep, which was lost with its worktree and is still not landed; the RENDERER's tile parallelism is independent of it and is already on -- SharedSettings::threads defaults to 0, and tiling.rs:127 takes the rayon branch for anything but 1. So these numbers are ALREADY across eighteen cores and the entry could have been measured at any time. The prerequisite was never real.
  • L-123's framing that lights-bench would need new flags to answer this. It needs none: the 1920x1080 ladder at 1k/10k/100k over both methods and Precision::ALL is compiled into the binary at src/bin/lights_bench.rs:652-674 and runs by default.
what it does NOT settle
  • ANY END-TO-END FRAME. The frame column stops before the CPU pack and before any GPU upload. L-150 measured the pack at 7.444 ms and the upload is still unmeasured (L-69), so no single clock in this crate has ever spanned a whole frame. The combined figures above are arithmetic over two runs.
  • WHETHER THE BIND TERM IS REDUCIBLE. 18.187 ms of projection and sorting at 100k atoms is measured; nothing here says whether it is inherent, a sort that could be incremental across frames, or an artefact of rebuilding per frame. It is the largest single term in a dense frame and it has no entry.
  • SCALING PAST 100k ATOMS, and any scene that is not the bench's gaussian. Every row here is kernel 'gaussian'; the modulated kernels this crate also ships are not on this ladder, and L-151 has just given them an out-of-plane carrier whose cost is unmeasured.
  • A SUSTAINED RATE. Every cell renders one frame per build and reports a per-frame clock; nothing here runs for a minute and reports the worst frame, which is what a game is actually judged on. Thermal behaviour over a long run is untouched.
  • THREAD COUNT AS AN AXIS. The run used rayon's default pool on an 18-core machine and did not sweep --threads, so nothing here says how the frame scales with cores or what it would do on a 4-core target.

docs/lights/benchmarks/l123/report.tsv (the full run: three axes, 29 columns, every cell) · crates/lights/src/bin/lights_bench.rs:652-674 (the 1080p atom ladder and the resolution axis, compiled in)

crates/lights/src/bin/lights_bench.rs:349-357 (the USAGE, which is why this needs no flags) · crates/lights/src/bin/lights_bench.rs:630 (repeats = 9 in full mode, 5 with --quick) · crates/lights/src/settings.rs:102 (threads defaults to 0) · crates/lights/src/tiling.rs:125-134 (for_each_tile: anything but 1 takes into_par_iter) · crates/lights/src/bin/lights_demo.rs:1402 (the fps line that refutes L-123's grep) · docs/lights/RESEARCH.json record l150-cpu-pack-term (the 7.444 ms pack this run does not contain) · crates/lights/BURNDOWN.toml L-123, L-120, L-96, L-69, L-150

Can the splat tile loop be given pixel-parallel width by turning it inside out? Atom-outer / pixels-inner, timed and disassembled against the shipped pixel-outer loop.

engineering2026-09-16copy-pasteable command2 refuted
the answer

NO, ON BOTH AXES, AND THE MECHANISM SAYS WHY. The inverted loop is 1.12x faster on min and 1.23x on median -- both INSIDE the 1.3x this crate treats as noise and inside the shipped loop's own 1.50x run-to-run spread, so there is no measured difference. And the disassembly says there was never going to be one: the variant emits ZERO 4-wide vector arithmetic instructions, exactly like the shipped loop it replaces. Loop inversion moves the blend's loop-carried, data-dependent accumulation around; it does not remove it, and that dependency is what blocks width. L-68 left open whether reason 2 of the static-dispatch argument COULD be filled; by this restructuring, it cannot.

the question it was built to answer

BURNDOWN L-149, split out of L-68. L-68 measured pixel-parallel 4-wide arithmetic at 0.4% of floating-point work in the shipped splat render bodies and 0.1% in raycast, so seam_costs.md's vectorisability argument is real but nearly empty. It counted static instructions and timed nothing. Does an atom-outer / pixels-inner restructuring of the splat tile loop go faster, does it create the 4-wide width the design clause exists to protect, and what does it cost in bit-exactness?

criterion, and its convention

The experiment was designed so it could LOSE, and the losing condition was stated before the run: a ratio inside 1.3x is NOISE by this crate's standing measurement rules, because single-thread wall time on this machine has a measured 2.4x P-core/E-core spread. Seven reps, first discarded, min and median and max all reported with the spread beside them. For the mechanism the bucket boundary is the one L-152 already paid for: 2-wide and 4-wide are counted SEPARATELY, because merging them reads as 'partially vectorised' and 2-wide is only the compiler pairing an (x, y) coordinate. Bit-exactness is asserted on to_bits(), never on a tolerance.

reproduce it
cargo test -p lights --release --all-features --target-dir target --test atom_outer -- --nocapture --test-threads 1

and for the mechanism, in a separate target dir so the main release cache is not invalidated:

cargo test -p lights --release --all-features --test atom_outer --no-run --target-dir target/asm --config 'build.rustflags=["--emit","asm"]'
python3 crates/lights/scripts/vector_mix.py target/asm/release/deps/atom_outer-<hash>.s --needle render_tile_atom_outer --min-instructions 50
python3 crates/lights/scripts/vector_mix.py target/asm/release/deps/lights_bench-<hash>.s --needle "splat..implementation..SplatRaster" --min-instructions 50

Run 2026-09-16 on an Apple M5 Max (18 cores, 128 GB, macOS 15). The shipped render_tile has NO BODY in the test binary's assembly because it is inlined at the call site, which is why the shipped figure is read from lights-bench in the same build rather than from the same file -- L-152 recorded that inlining and it is not an error.
what it measured
measurementvaluewhere it comes from
wall time, 480x270, 600 atoms, 7 reps first discarded, release, tiles walked seriallyshipped pixel-outer min 1.702 / median 1.873 / max 2.550 ms (spread 1.50x); atom-outer variant min 1.515 / median 1.527 / max 1.783 ms (spread 1.18x)variant / shipped = 0.891x on min, 0.815x on median -- i.e. nominally 1.12x and 1.23x FASTER, both inside the 1.3x noise band and inside the shipped loop's own 1.50x spread. NO MEASURED DIFFERENCE. This comparison is single-threaded by construction: the timing harness walks tiles serially so the two loop bodies are compared and not rayon's scheduler.
vector mix, same release build with --emit asm, bodies >= 50 instructionsrender_tile_atom_outer: 4-wide 0, 2-wide 11, scalar fp 42 -> wide 20.8%, 4-WIDE 0.0%. Shipped SplatRaster bodies (4 of them): 4-wide 0, 2-wide 36, scalar fp 323 -> wide 10.0%, 4-WIDE 0.0%.THE DECISIVE COLUMN IS 4-WIDE AND BOTH ARE ZERO. The variant's higher 2-wide share is not a win: 2-wide is the compiler pairing an (x, y) coordinate, which L-68 established costs the dispatch decision nothing.
bit-exactness of the inverted loop against the shipped oneEXACT. Every accumulator cell agrees on to_bits() for rgb.x, rgb.y, rgb.z and transmittance across 4 tiles of a 32x32 frame, and all five counters agree.Not obvious in advance. pixel_loop.md refuses to advance the whitened offset along a scanline because repeated subtraction accumulates rounding -- but that concern is about CURSOR-ADVANCING, and atom-outer does not require it. This variant still recomputes centre - sample and l_inv.apply(offset) per pixel, so per pixel the atoms are visited in the same order with the same arithmetic.
what it settled
  • REASON 2 OF THE STATIC-DISPATCH ARGUMENT CANNOT BE FILLED BY LOOP INVERSION, and that is the last open question from L-68's thread. The shipped loop is 0.0% four-wide and so is the inverted one. What blocks width is BlendMode::step: it writes a per-pixel accumulator, reads its own previous value, and can return Stop. Inverting the loop moves that dependency; it does not remove it.
  • THE RESTRUCTURING COSTS NO EXACTNESS, WHICH IS THE OPPOSITE OF WHAT THE ENTRY FEARED. L-149 raised bit-exactness as the collision that made this expensive, citing pixel_loop.md. The citation is about advancing a cursor along a scanline, which this restructuring does not do. So the trade the entry was worried about is not the trade on offer: the inverted loop is free in exactness and worthless in speed.
  • A NULL RESULT THAT WAS ALMOST A FALSE POSITIVE. Read on medians alone the variant is 1.23x faster and would have been reportable as a win. It is only the spread that kills it -- the shipped loop's own max/min is 1.50x, wider than the difference being claimed. This is the third time in this crate's record that reporting a single statistic would have inverted a conclusion.
what it refuted
  • BURNDOWN L-149's own framing that the experiment 'collides with a bit-exactness guarantee'. It does not collide with it at all -- see the EXACT row above. The guarantee pixel_loop.md protects is against cursor-advancing, not against reordering the two loops, and the entry inherited the wrong half of that note.
  • The implicit hope in seam_costs.md's reason 2 that the loop shape is what stands between this crate and pixel-parallel width. The loop shape is not the obstacle; the blend seam is. Changing the loop and holding the blend fixed moves the 4-wide count from zero to zero.
what it does NOT settle
  • A VARIANT THAT ALSO SPLITS THE BLEND. The restructuring tested here keeps BlendMode::step exactly as it is. Splitting the blend into a coverage pass and a colour pass WOULD remove the loop-carried dependency, and it would change the composite order and therefore break the bit-exactness that this variant preserves. That is a different experiment and needs its own ruling; nothing here says whether it would be fast.
  • ANY VIEWPORT BUT 480x270 AND ANY ATOM COUNT BUT 600, and the ray caster entirely -- this touched the splat loop only, as L-149 specified. The 1080p frame is dominated by BIND rather than render at 100k atoms (see l123-frame-rate-1080p), so the loop measured here is not where a dense frame spends its time anyway.
  • MULTI-THREADED BEHAVIOUR. The harness walks tiles serially on purpose, to compare the loop bodies rather than rayon. Whether the inverted loop's larger per-tile working set (three Vecs sized to the tile) changes cache behaviour under 18-way tile parallelism is untested.
  • ANY ARCHITECTURE BUT aarch64-apple-darwin, where NEON's 128 bits make 4-wide the f32 maximum and 2-wide the f64 maximum. On a 512-bit target the buckets would mean something different.

Does compositing two single-kind passes serve a scene that interpenetrates? A surfel floor with atoms resting on it, composited both ways against a per-pixel depth-interleaved truth.

engineering2026-09-28copy-pasteable command2 refuted
the answer

NO. Option (iii) of the L-73 ruling does not serve an interpenetrating scene in either order: atoms-over-floor disagrees with the interleaved truth on 3,001 pixels (15.6% of the frame, worst 0.391) and floor-over-atoms on 3,006 (15.7%, worst 0.400), against a pre-registered bar of zero pixels above 1/255. Every disagreeing pixel is one BOTH passes reach; wherever one kind is alone the composite is bit-exact. And the failure has a measured edge: one atom composites exactly over the floor once its centre is 3 sigma clear of it, and not at 2.5 sigma (36 pixels).

the question it was built to answer

BURNDOWN L-153, raised by the PM's primitive-set ruling on L-73 (2026-09-16), which chose (iii) - one scene holds one primitive kind, kinds mix by FloatImage::composite_over - and said that (ii), a heterogeneous Drawable scene costing 69 generic signatures, must be earned rather than assumed. composite_over carries no depth. Does a surfel floor with atoms resting on it, some partly below its plane, composite visibly differently from a render that sorts both kinds together by depth per pixel, and where?

criterion, and its convention

PRE-REGISTERED in the register entry before the first run: a pixel DISAGREES when the largest channel of |composited - interleaved|, both premultiplied colour over black, exceeds 1/255 - one code of the delivered 8-bit image. (iii) serves iff at least one composite order has ZERO disagreeing pixels. A lifted control (the same atoms 6 sigma higher) must agree under atoms-over-floor, so that a disagreement is attributed to interpenetration rather than to the instrument. The instrument itself is checked first: the interleaved arm restricted to one kind must reproduce each shipped pass to 1e-12.

reproduce it
cargo test -p lights --all-features --test interpenetration -- --nocapture --test-threads 1

Run 2026-09-28 on an Apple M5 Max (18 cores, 128 GB, macOS 15), debug profile, f64, 160x120, the ray caster under SharedSettings::contract() with OrderingModel::HitDepth and one DirectionalLight. Every number below is a deterministic pixel count or a colour difference, not a time; 26.6 s wall for the five tests.

L-156 follow-up re-run 2026-09-28, same machine and command; the depth-limited arm is the six tests added to the same file (12 tests, 15.8 s wall at --test-threads 4).
what it measured
measurementvaluewhere it comes from
instrument check: interleaved arm with one kind vs the shipped pass aloneatoms 0.0, floor 0.0 (largest channel gap over colour and coverage)Bit-exact, not merely inside 1e-12: the interleaved arm calls the shipped collect_hits, Over::step and intersect_surfel, so with one kind present it IS the shipped pass. Every disagreement below is therefore the composite. tests/interpenetration.rs::the_interleaved_instrument_reproduces_each_pass_alone.
resting scene: 5 atoms, sigma 0.35, centres at +1, +0.5, 0, -0.5, -1 sigma above an opaque surfel floor (841 discs, opacity 0.99)atoms over floor: 3,001 px disagree (15.630% of 19,200), 1,302 above 0.1, worst 0.390869. clearance sweep: ONE atom, centre h sigma above the floor, atoms over floorh=-1: 1,462 px (worst 0.386); 0: 1,045 (0.382); 1: 588 (0.212); 2: 184 (0.033); 2.5: 36 (0.008); 3: 0; 3.5: 0; 4: 0; 5: 0Monotone, and the edge sits in (2.5, 3] sigma for THIS camera (eye 2.5 above the floor, 5 back, 45 degree field). Even an atom whose centre is 2 sigma clear - one that visibly rests ON the floor - disagrees on 184 pixels. tests/interpenetration.rs::one_centred_atom_needs_clearance_before_atoms_over_floor_is_exact.
L-156 follow-up, the same resting scene with the floor's depth carried (option B, PM ruling 2026-09-28)atoms over floor, depth-limited: 0 px disagree of 6,678 overlap, gross 0, worst 3.879e-5The surfel pass hands out the ray t at which floor transmittance first falls to 1/1024 or below (12,332 of 19,200 px finite); dispatch::render_depth_limited narrows each ray's t_max to it and intersect_lit rejects any atom whose t* lies beyond. Seen RED first with the plumbing in place and the t_max test absent: 3,001 px, worst 0.391 - L-153's own number. tests/interpenetration.rs::a_depth_limited_atom_pass_composites_a_resting_scene_exactly.
L-156 follow-up, one atom swept from -1 to 5 sigma, depth-limited0 px disagree at all nine clearances; worst 4.124e-5 (-1 sigma), 1.641e-5 (0), 7.365e-6 (1), 1.091e-6 (2), 2.642e-7 (2.5), below 5e-16 from 3 sigmaThe residual is the light of the rejected atoms seen through the floor, bounded by the floor's transmittance (<= 1/1024) times their radiance; it vanishes where nothing is buried. tests/interpenetration.rs::the_depth_limit_holds_at_every_clearance_of_the_sweep.
what it settled
  • COMPOSITING IS EXACT EXCEPT WHERE THE TWO KINDS OVERLAP ON SCREEN, AND THERE IT IS WRONG ABOUT HALF THE TIME. The error is not a subtle blend drift: 1,302 pixels are wrong by more than 0.1, the whole-object occlusion error the entry predicted. No choice of order fixes it, because the resting scene needs atoms in front of the floor on some rays and behind it on others.
  • THE FAILURE HAS A CLEARANCE EDGE, WHICH IS A THIRD OPTION THE PM DID NOT HAVE BEFORE. An atom whose centre is 3 sigma or more above an opaque surface composites exactly over it at this view; at 2.5 sigma it does not. So (iii) is exact for scenes that keep that clearance - floating UI chrome, a lifted object - and inexact for anything that rests on or passes through a surface.
  • THE FLOOR IS A SINGLE OPAQUE LAYER, SO A DEPTH-CARRYING COMPOSITE WOULD REPRODUCE THE TRUTH HERE WITHOUT (ii). If the surfel pass exported its first-hit depth per pixel, deciding each atom hit against that depth is exactly what the interleaved arm does for this scene. That is cheaper than a heterogeneous scene by the 69 signatures L-73 counted; it is NOT built or measured, and it stops being exact the moment the surface in front is itself translucent or multi-layered.
  • THE DEPTH-CARRYING COMPOSITE (L-156, option B) CLOSES THE FAILURE WITHOUT A HETEROGENEOUS SCENE. One depth per pixel from the surfel pass, enforced as the atom rays' t_max, takes the resting scene from 3,001 disagreeing pixels to zero against the same pre-registered bar, at every clearance of the sweep. composite_over itself is unchanged. What remains unserved is an atom BEHIND A TRANSLUCENT surface (floor transmittance above 1/1024 keeps an infinite limit and composites as before) - the case option (ii) would have to earn.
what it refuted
  • That option (iii) - compose kinds with a depthless composite_over - serves the 3D game target's resting-object scene. It does not, in either order, by 3,001 and 3,006 pixels against a bar of zero.
  • That an object has to visibly sink into a surface before compositing breaks. An atom centred 2 sigma above the floor still disagrees on 184 pixels.
what it does NOT settle
  • Whether (ii) is ever needed. L-156 built (B), the depth-carrying composite, and it is exact for the resting scene; (ii) remains unearned until a measured scene with atoms behind a TRANSLUCENT surface fails. The clearance rule was not built.
  • The clearance edge's dependence on view angle, kernel and support mode. It was measured at one camera, the Gaussian kernel and SupportMode::CutoffSolved; 3 sigma is not a constant of the crate.
  • The splat method. The atom pass here is the ray caster, because only it hands out a per-ray hit depth to interleave against. Splat orders by centre depth and would need its own truth arm.
  • What the truth arm is true about. The ray caster places each atom at ONE depth per ray (its peak t*), so the interleaved arm moves a whole atom in front of or behind the floor per ray; it never splits one atom's density at the plane. A volumetric truth would differ inside the 'wrong' pixels, not outside them.

How many decibels behind image-gs is the greedy fitter at equal bits per pixel? Greedy fitted to six CLIC2020 crops at image-gs's two published bitrates, billed under image-gs's own float16 convention.

representation2026-09-28copy-pasteable command2 refuted
the answer

About 8 dB behind, and only as a CROSS-DATASET figure: greedy scores 21.24 dB at 0.122 bpp and 24.48 dB at 0.366 bpp on six 64x64 CLIC2020 crops, against image-gs's published 29.20 +- 4.57 and 32.99 +- 4.49 on its own 45-image 2K set - gaps of 7.96 and 8.51 dB. A same-image comparison cannot be produced: image-gs's images are behind an authenticated link, its CLIC run is a figure only, and it needs CUDA.

the question it was built to answer

BURNDOWN L-43, from docs/plan/TWO_HORIZON_PLAN.md section 8: fit a standard image and compare the rate-distortion curve against Image-GS's published points at equal bits per pixel; how many dB behind is our fitter? The prerequisite, a raster reference, landed as L-155 under the PM's ruling of 2026-09-16.

criterion, and its convention

PSNR = 10 log10(1 / mse), mse the mean over all pixels and all three channels of the squared difference between the re-rendered fit and the raster decoded as c/255 (peak 1.0; numerically identical to an 8-bit peak-255 PSNR). Equal bitrate means equal bits per pixel with every scalar billed at float16: 144 bits per lights atom (9 scalars, Atom2d::bits), 128 per image-gs Gaussian (5 + 3 parameters, paper section 4.1 'all quantized to float16'). Atom count = floor(bpp x pixels / bits). Image-gs's own PSNR convention (range, channel averaging, gamma) is not stated in the paper.

reproduce it
cargo test -p lights --all-features --test fit_imagegs -- --nocapture --test-threads 1

Run 2026-09-28 on an Apple M5 Max (18 cores, 128 GB, macOS 15), debug profile, f64, greedy with the Gaussian kernel under SharedSettings::prefiltered(), tile 16, render budget 40,000,000. 2 passed in 39.9 s. Deterministic: two runs in this context printed identical figures to four decimals.

what it settled
  • NO PUBLISHED IMAGE-GS NUMBER EXISTS ON AN IMAGE THIS CRATE CAN OBTAIN. The 45-image set is behind an authenticated OneDrive link (the share 301s to a 403; the OneDrive shares API answers unauthenticated, probed 2026-09-28); the CLIC2020 run is Figure 5 with no number in the text; image-gs runs on gsplat/CUDA and this machine has none.
  • THE BITRATE CONVENTION RECONSTRUCTS THE PAPER'S OWN DEFAULT. float16 x 8 parameters gives 9,994 Gaussians at 0.305 bpp, the shipped 10,000 within 0.1%, so the conversion from bpp to primitive count is the paper's and not a guess.
  • GREEDY SITS ABOUT 8 DB BELOW IMAGE-GS'S PUBLISHED MEAN AT BOTH BITRATES, and buys only 4.0 to 7.2 dB over a flat fill of the crop's mean colour.
  • THE CONFOUND LEANS TOWARD GREEDY. Image-gs reports lower scores on lower-resolution images at equal bitrate (its CLIC paragraph), so a 64x64 crop is its weak regime too; the gap measured across that regime is more likely an underestimate than an overestimate.
what it refuted
  • That the plan's recipe - compare against Image-GS's published points at equal bpp - can be run as a same-image comparison. It cannot, by any of three routes (private data, no CLIC numbers, no CUDA).
  • That greedy is within the published spread of image-gs at equal bitrate: the mean gap is 8 dB against a spread of 4.5 dB.
what it does NOT settle
  • The gap on the same images. What remains is internal: whether a better fitter (L-147, the analytic gradient) closes 8 dB on these six committed crops.
  • Any kernel but gaussian, any profile but prefiltered(), any crop size but 64x64, more than six crops.
  • Image-gs's PSNR convention beyond the word: range, channel averaging and gamma are not stated in the paper.
  • Greedy at image-gs's scale; extrapolated, not run.

What does one greedy candidate evaluation cost, and which of the three serial optimisations L-97 named is worth building? A per-candidate census of allocations and nanoseconds at the fitter's own viewport.

engineering2026-09-28copy-pasteable command3 refuted
the answer

Only the fused residual mattered: it takes about 1.9 us, roughly 10%, off an ~18 us candidate evaluation and is built. Framebuffer reuse would save about 1.2% of a render and the phase timers cost about 0.7%; both are dropped.

the question it was built to answer

BURNDOWN L-97: the fitter's inner step is one render_or_background plus one residual_energy per candidate, about 10^4 candidates per fit. Three serial savings were named: (1) reuse the framebuffer instead of allocating one per render, (2) fuse the residual so no difference image is built, (3) drop the unconditional Instant::now/elapsed per phase. Which of them is worth building, given the sweep is critical-path bound so only a cheaper fit helps?

criterion, and its convention

Nanoseconds per call as the median of 9 reps, with the lowest and highest rep, per the loop's rule that a single-run time is never published and a 1.3x difference is noise. A saving is worth building only if it moves a candidate evaluation by more than that noise floor or is free to build; allocation counts are deterministic and counted by a thread-local global allocator in the same test binary.

reproduce it
cargo test -p lights --all-features --release --test fit_residual -- --include-ignored --nocapture --test-threads 1

Run 2026-09-28 on an Apple M5 Max (18 cores, 128 GB, macOS 15), release profile, f64, Gaussian kernel, SharedSettings::contract() with threads = 1, tile 16, 64x48 viewport, 8 atoms. The census is the #[ignore]d test candidate_cost_census; 9 reps per row, each rep 400 (render) to 400,000 (timer) calls. Run three times in this context; ranges below span the three runs.
what it measured
measurementvaluewhere it comes from
one candidate render14.2-14.5 us median (13.1-17.6 us across all reps)render_or_background, 8 atoms, 64x48.
residual, old (difference then sum)3.61-3.98 us medianAn in-test copy of the old body, reference_energy.
residual, fused1.81-1.91 us medianThe built residual_energy; bit-identical to the old body (tests/fit_residual.rs).
saving per candidate evaluationabout 1.9 us of ~18 us, ~10%Derived from the two rows above; not a measured whole-fit time.
allocations per candidate render25 allocations, 180,288 bytesDeterministic, identical across runs.
of which >= 4 KiB2 allocations, 172,032 bytesThe 73,728-byte resolved image and a 98,304-byte framebuffer; the other 23 total 8,256 bytes.
allocations per residualold: 1 of 73,728 bytes; fused: 0residual_energy_allocates_nothing, red on the unfixed tree.
frame-sized allocation alone83-95 ns medianVec::<Rgb<f64>>::with_capacity(64*48).
frame-sized allocation plus fill687-768 ns medianFloatImage::filled; the fill is the clear a reused buffer still pays.
Instant::now + elapsed pair32-35 ns medianThree pairs per render (build, bind, render phases) = ~0.1 us, ~0.7% of a render.
what it settled
  • THE FUSED RESIDUAL IS THE ONLY ONE OF THE THREE THAT MOVES A CANDIDATE. Removing the difference image halves the residual (3.6-4.0 us to 1.8-1.9 us) and takes ~10% off each candidate evaluation, with every published residual_energy figure unmoved because the float operations and their order are unchanged.
  • FRAMEBUFFER REUSE IS WORTH ~1.2% OF A RENDER. Only 2 of the render's 25 allocations are large, and allocating one costs ~85 ns; the ~600 ns of writing it is a clear that a reused buffer would still pay for each candidate.
  • THE PHASE TIMERS COST ~0.7% OF A RENDER. Three Instant::now/elapsed pairs at 32-35 ns each.
  • THE FIRST ALLOCATION COUNTER LIED. Counted in process-wide atomics it read 3 allocations / 220,032 bytes for a residual that makes one of 73,728, because sibling tests allocate on their own threads while it is armed. Thread-local counters read the true figure.
what it refuted
  • That the framebuffer allocated per candidate render is a lever worth an ownership refactor of the fitter (~1.2%).
  • That the unconditional per-phase timers are a measurable cost of the fit (~0.7%).
  • The entry's premise that 'both allocate a full image per candidate render' weighs the same: the residual's image cost ~1.9 us, the render's own buffers ~0.17 us of allocation.
what it does NOT settle
  • Whole-fit wall time before and after: the ~10% is per candidate evaluation, derived from the census, and a fit also seeds, scans the structure tensor and renders outside evaluate.
  • Any viewport other than 64x48, any kernel but Gaussian, any atom count but 8.
  • The 23 small allocations of the render (8,256 bytes) were counted but not timed individually.

The patch-light-transport gate: does a path-traced 2.5D card canvas differ visibly from SSAO plus a contact-hardened drop shadow, and if it does, can a patch graph resolve the difference?

transport2026-09-28copy-pasteable command3 refuted
the answer

Rung 1 passes on a light canvas only: transport adds a coloured contact bleed (up to 17 CIEDE2000, a fifth of the visible canvas over the JND) that neither SSAO+drop shadow nor GTAO multi-bounce reproduces, and on a dark canvas it is invisible (max 1.17). Rung 2 then kills the patch line: the bleed halves within 2 px of the rim at 2-4 px elevation and 4 px at 8 px, so the patches that resolve it are the 3 px the ladder names as unaffordable.

the question it was built to answer

BURNDOWN L-107, docs/lights/TRANSPORT_RESEARCH.md section 8: render one card canvas twice, an offline path-traced reference and SSAO plus a contact-hardened drop shadow, at 100% and 200%. If they are indistinguishable the patch-transport line is dead. If not, name the phenomenon transport delivers that the screen-space pass cannot, and then measure the contact gradient in pixels to see whether the patch size that resolves it is affordable (kill at 3 px or below).

criterion, and its convention

CIEDE2000 between 8-bit sRGB renders, with 2.3 as the just-noticeable difference; counts are pixels over 2.3, split into canvas (ground) and card tops. The transport contribution is measured as reference minus direct-only, both from the same samples, so it carries no visibility noise. A bleed's spatial scale is the distance from the rim at which the traced bounce term falls to half its rim value, read on each card's outer side at 1 px steps; the ladder's rung-2 kill is a required patch of 3 px or less (TRANSPORT_RESEARCH.md F8).

reproduce it
python3 crates/lights/scripts/transport_gate.py --out docs/lights/transport_gate --spp 1024 --key-spp 256 --depth 6 --workers 16

Run 2026-09-28 on an Apple M5 Max (18 cores, 128 GB, macOS 15), python 3.9, numpy 2.0.2, Pillow 11.3.0; 24.7 s wall. Seeded (--seed 7 default) and deterministic: run twice in this context, identical numbers. The self-test runs first and refuses on any failure; `python3 crates/lights/scripts/transport_gate.py --selftest` runs it alone. Writes 32 PNGs and gate.json (every number below) into --out.

what it settled
  • ON A DARK CANVAS THE TRANSPORT TERM IS INVISIBLE. Max CIEDE2000 1.17 against direct-only at both scales, zero pixels over the JND; the canvas's 0.012 albedo leaves nothing to reflect. Every visible difference between the reference and the screen-space pass is AO approximation error, and GTAO multi-bounce takes the canvas pixels to zero over the JND.
  • ON A LIGHT CANVAS TRANSPORT DELIVERS A NAMED PHENOMENON: COLOURED CONTACT BLEED. The canvas beside and under a saturated card picks up that card's colour, reflected off its underside, at up to 0.19 linear. It is a fifth of the visible canvas over the JND at 100% and at 200%, and the GTAO multi-bounce correction, which tints by the RECEIVER's albedo, leaves it in place. This is the phenomenon the 2026-09-08 research said had never been named; rung 1 passes for that theme.
  • THE BLEED IS AS NARROW AS THE CONTACT SHADOW, SO RUNG 2 KILLS THE PATCH LINE. It halves within 2 px of the rim at 2-4 px elevation and 4 px at 8 px, the elevations node cards use; only a 16 px overlay reaches 8 px. Resolving that needs patches at or under 3 px, which is the ladder's own kill criterion and F8's 8e4-patch, 5e6-link, 12-25 ms regime; the 20 px patch F8 costed resolves none of them.
  • THE RESEARCH'S 'SECOND BOUNCE IS 1e-4' IS ABOUT CARD FRONTS AND HOLDS THERE. Card tops pick up no bounce here except under the overlay; the bleed lives entirely on the canvas, in the rim band the research already conceded survives. What changed is that it is now measured, and that on a light theme it is well over the JND rather than a tint one would not notice.
  • THE WAR MAP COMPLICATION IN SECTION 9 IS STALE. docs/war_map.toml:66 still marks lights Legacy/Deferred, but the PM ruled on 2026-09-09 that lights is the engine under optical and a 3D game engine, both required.
what it refuted
  • That the two images are indistinguishable (the original rung-1 expectation): true for a dark canvas, false for a light one.
  • That the only surviving difference is 'a rim tint you would not notice unpaired': on a light canvas it is 5.6-10.3 CIEDE2000 at the rim and runs 2-11 px out.
  • That patch transport is a candidate for the effect it names: the effect's gradient is 2-4 px wide at card elevations.
what it does NOT settle
  • The canvas is crafted, not captured: no real atrium layout exists on disk and the gate was run from a non-interactive context. Card sizes, gaps, inks and elevations are chosen, not measured from a session.
  • Whether a screen-space colour-bleed pass (SSDO, Ritschel et al. 2009, or a per-pixel variant of GTAO multi-bounce tinted by the OCCLUDER's albedo) reaches the reference within the JND. Not run; it is the cheap way to deliver the named effect and the next question if the PM wants the effect.
  • Whether the bleed is noticed UNPAIRED: CIEDE2000 is a paired measure. The pairs are in docs/lights/transport_gate/*_pair.png for a human eye (reference, SSAO, GTAO multi-bounce, left to right).
  • Cards are modelled as thin floating sheets. A solid card with side walls would block light under the rim and change the bleed; not rendered.
  • Anything about the 3D game target. The gate is a 2.5D UI canvas; radiosity or Enlighten-style transport for a game scene is a separate question this record does not touch.
