Does a per-atom tuned super-gaussian beta beat a tuned gaussian under the greedy fitter, and does it beat simply choosing one better fixed curve?

representation2026-09-28copy-pasteable command1 refuted
the answer

Against the tuned gaussian, yes and by a wide margin: the per-atom beta ladder wins 42 of 43 rungs over six targets at eight atoms, with residual energy 0.011x to 0.99x. Against an oracle that picks the best of the five fixed Beta::SWEEP families per target and per rung it is mixed: 23 of 43, strong on t3 and t4, and 2.64x worse on t6 at n=8.

the question it was built to answer

BURNDOWN L-135 (PM ruling B, 2026-09-16): a ShapedAtom2d carries a CurveShape per atom and render_shaped_atoms renders it. Before paying for more shape knobs, does a ladder with ONE shape knob (beta, the SuperGaussian family's parameter) fitted to the same targets and budget as the gaussian ladder produce a lower residual? If not, the per-atom-shape line is not worth carrying.

criterion, and its convention

The fitter's own objective, summed squared RGB residual energy against the exact-coverage truth (coverage_image_to_colour at quadrature depth 10), compared at equal atom count n = 1..8, for greedy::fit under SuperGaussian(Beta::One) against greedy::fit_shaped under the same family. A rung is a win when the shaped energy is strictly lower. This is the objective, not the edge criterion of fit::edge; see objective_is_not_the_criterion.md.

reproduce it
cargo test -p lights --all-features --test fit_shaped_beta -- --nocapture
cargo test -p lights --all-features --test fit_shaped_beta -- --ignored --nocapture a_tuned_beta_against_the_best_fixed_family

Run 2026-09-28 on an Apple M5 Max, debug build (the suite's own profile): the first prints the beta-4 control and the gaussian-vs-shaped count in about 36 s, the second prints the 43-row table with the fixed-family oracle in 139 s. Deterministic (single-threaded fits, no RNG); run twice in this context with identical numbers.

measurementvaluewhere it comes from
shaped beats tuned gaussian42 of 43 rungsa_tuned_beta_beats_a_tuned_gaussian_on_every_target; the one loss is t6-glyph-like n=2, 32.50 vs 22.09.
shaped beats best fixed family (oracle per target and rung)23 of 43 rungsa_tuned_beta_against_the_best_fixed_family, column shaped_over_fixed.

what it settled
  • A per-atom beta is worth carrying over a gaussian atom: the byte cost is one scalar and the render cost is 11%, and the residual falls by one to two orders of magnitude on edges and curves.
  • Recovered betas cluster at the 8.0 ceiling on edges and corners: the optimum there is flatter than the swept range allows.
  • The identity check holds bit for bit: under a kernel that never reads beta, fit_shaped returns fit's nine scalars exactly, so the gain is the knob and nothing else.
what it refuted
  • That a tuned curve might buy nothing over a tuned gaussian, which would have made the per-atom shape line (PM ruling B) not worth building further.
what it does NOT settle
  • Whether the win survives the edge criterion (fit::edge) rather than the fitter's own energy objective.
  • Whether widening MAX_BETA past 8 helps, given how many atoms sit on the ceiling.
  • Why t6 loses: the greedy commits atom 2 to beta 6.62 and never recovers; a search that is not greedy, or refine_tail over beta, might not.
  • Any shape knob other than beta (frequency, orientation, phase, order); none was tuned.
  • Wall time: only render counts were compared.

Does an exact gradient of the fit loss rescue the gradient fitter? A reverse pass over the splat forward model, proven against the central difference, priced in renders, and fed to Adam and to a line search at equal budget.

engineering2026-09-28copy-pasteable command2 refuted
the answer

The gradient is built and exact: it agrees with the verified central difference to 9.1e-10 of |g| on every one of nine partials in four cases (interior atom, fitted atom, an atom behind an occluder, the occluder), and its own forward image equals the tile renderer's bit for bit. It costs 0.89x-1.46x one render (debug build, five reps per class) against the difference's 18. But deleting the 18:1 changes NOTHING for Adam: at equal renders (228 steps instead of 24) analytic Adam reproduces the central-difference table to nine digits, 0 wins and 6 losses. Measured cause: Adam's first bias-corrected step is exactly sign(g)*rate, every improvement it made on all six seeds fell in steps 1-5, and on t4, t5 and t6 it never beat the seed. L-132's 0-6 was a verdict on that optimiser, not on gradients. A backtracking line search is not gradient-blind: fed the exact gradient it is never worse than fed the difference, and it beats the ladder on t4-curve and t6-glyph-like from the same seed at equal renders, losing on t1, t2, t3 and t5.

the question it was built to answer

BURNDOWN L-147, split from L-143. L-132 found Adam over the finite-difference gradient loses to the coordinate ladder 0-6 and named the 18-renders-per-gradient price as the term that changes the comparison's arithmetic. Build the analytic gradient for the gaussian under Over, prove it against descent::gradient, measure its price in renders, and re-run the six-class comparison at the new budget.

criterion, and its convention

Written into L-147 before the build. Agreement: every analytic partial within a stated tolerance of descent::gradient at eps = 1e-6 on at least the interior and fitted atoms of tests/fit_descent.rs; set at 1e-7 of |g| (the measured worst is 9.1e-10, so two orders of headroom and five below the difference's own 1e-3 identity tolerance), with every partial non-zero so a skipped parameter fails. Price: wall time of one analytic_gradient over one evaluate() on the same 4-atom fitted scene, five reps, spread reported, 1.3x treated as noise. Comparison: L-132's harness unchanged (four atoms, six classes, gaussian, prefiltered, tile 16, truth from exact_coverage depth 10) with Adam's step count scaled so the render budget is equal: 24*(18+1)/(1+1) = 228 steps.

reproduce it
cargo test -p lights --all-features --test fit_backward -- --test-threads 1 --nocapture

Run 2026-09-28 on the M5 Max (debug build, --all-features): 7 passed in 37.05 s. Agreement per partial: the_analytic_gradient_agrees_with_the_central_difference. Forward replica: the_reverse_pass_reproduces_the_renderers_image_and_loss. Price: the_price_of_an_analytic_gradient_is_measured_in_renders (wall clock, five reps per class). Adam table: the_analytic_descent_is_compared_with_the_ladder_at_equal_renders. Line-search table: a_line_search_fed_the_exact_gradient_is_never_worse_than_one_fed_the_difference. The central-difference Adam table for the same tree: cargo test -p lights --all-features --test fit_descent -- --test-threads 1 --nocapture descent_fit_matches

line search from the seed at the ladder's render count (seed, ladder, central-difference, analytic)t1 12280.27, 4.3035, 18.9815, 4.8803; t2 6099.58, 617.0230, 1015.4582, 989.6409; t3 3027.58, 571.6198, 734.3563, 733.8414; t4 63.59, 11.1083, 11.1054, 11.1054; t5 43.68, 23.1062, 29.9880, 28.6441; t6 47.41, 40.9717, 40.9602, 40.9219Ladder 865 renders (refine, refine_iterations default); central difference 24 steps, 865 renders; analytic 45 steps, 856 renders (t4 stopped after 23 steps at 457). Energies, deterministic.
what it settled
  • THE 18:1 PRICE WAS NOT WHY ADAM LOST. With the price at about one render, Adam's table is unchanged to nine digits. Bias-corrected Adam's first step is sign(g) times the rate, independent of |g|, and its trajectory continues from wherever it lands rather than from its best point; on these seeds every improvement it found was in the first five full-rate steps. An optimiser that reads only the sign of the gradient cannot be improved by a more accurate gradient.
  • THE EXACT GRADIENT DOES HELP A CONSUMER THAT READS ITS DIRECTION. The line search fed the analytic gradient is never worse than fed the difference, and it beats the coordinate ladder on two classes (t4-curve, t6-glyph-like) from the same seed at equal renders -- the first time a gradient method has beaten the ladder anywhere in this crate.
  • THE COST HAS MOVED. With the gradient at one render, the line search's 17 renders per step are now 17 of every 18 renders a descent step costs. The next price to delete is the line search, not the gradient.
  • THE FORWARD MODEL CAN BE REPLAYED EXACTLY OUTSIDE THE TILE LOOP. Using the method's own project_scene, depth_bits order, eval_screen and Over::step reproduces the renderer bit for bit, so a reverse pass need not be threaded through render_tile at all.
what it refuted
  • L-143 and L-147's premise that 'the 18:1 is the single term that changes the comparison's arithmetic' for Adam. Removing it changes Adam's result by nothing.
  • L-132's reading that direction quality has to beat an 18:1 sampling disadvantage before it wins anything: at a 1:1 price Adam still does not win, because it does not use the direction's quality.
what it does NOT settle
  • Whether an Adam that restarts from its best point at each decay level, or that is not bias-corrected into sign descent, would beat the ladder with this gradient. Not built.
  • Whether a line search cheaper than 17 renders (an Armijo test with one or two trials, or a closed-form step from the second derivative) turns the line search's 2-of-6 into more. Not built.
  • A release-build price. Every timing here is the debug build this loop is confined to; the ratio of two debug timings is not a release ratio.
  • Any kernel but the gaussian or any blend but Over. The reverse pass refuses them.
  • Joint optimisation of all atoms at once; the pass differentiates one atom per call.

How much of the canvas can optical's dirty counter name when one node changes? A headless bound on the damage-rect producer, against the rect that actually changed.

engineering2026-09-28copy-pasteable command1 refuted
the answer

A BOUND, NOT A SESSION MEASUREMENT: on a headless UnifiedMapV2 at 1200x800, collapsing one node, the shared payload-less dirty counter can name 24.3% of the canvas at 9 nodes in Manual and 72.4%-77.9% at 9-25 nodes in Manual and Grid, while the node rects that actually changed cover 0.6%-7.2% (node rects only; wires and drags not measured). So a sub-rect present fed by TODAY's producer is not worth building - it would redraw most of the canvas in three of four configurations - but the change itself is small, 10.7x to 120x smaller than what the counter names. The gap is the producer's missing payload, not anything in lights: L-41 already renders a sub-rect exactly.

the question it was built to answer

BURNDOWN L-70, ruled (A) 2026-09-28. L-41 built the consumer half of a damage rect in lights; this asks whether optical can PRODUCE one. DirtyFlag is Rc<Cell<u32>> with no payload. Drive the widget tree headless, and log the union of Positioner::bound_rect over the widgets whose dirty counter moved, as a fraction of the canvas. If it is routinely most of the canvas, sub-rect presentation is not worth building; if it is small, an interactive session (option B) has been earned.

criterion, and its convention

Written into L-70 by the ruling: 'if the union is routinely most of the canvas, that is enough to rule sub-rect presentation not-worth-building'. Convention: a fraction is area(union of rects, clipped to the 1200x800 canvas) / canvas area. 'What the counter names' is the union over every node, old and new, because every node holds the same Rc (unified_map/api.rs:107) and so every node's counter moved. 'What moved' is the union of old and new rects of the nodes whose bound_rect differs after the edit settles (<= 90 frames). Pinned in the test: counter_names >= 5 x actually_moved in every case.

reproduce it
cargo test -p optical --test damage_bound -- --nocapture --test-threads 1

Run 2026-09-28 on the M5 Max (debug build, default features): 2 passed in 0.15 s, deterministic (headless egui, no clock), identical on three runs. The table is printed by the_payloadless_counter_can_only_name_the_union_of_every_node. The guard test a_collapse_reaches_the_screen_only_through_the_shared_counter was seen RED with the consumer stuck: in crates/optical/src/unified/node_graph/unified_map/paint.rs replace `self.layout_dirty.get() > 0` in the dirty_consumed line with `self.layout_dirty.get() > u32::MAX - 1`, rerun the same command: both tests fail, at Manual n=9 and (Grid cases alone) at Grid n=9.
what it measured
measurementvaluewhere it comes from
Manual, 9 nodes (3x3, 220x160 world spacing), collapse node 0/4/8counter names 0.2432; moved 0.0120; 1 node movedPrinted table, damage_bound.rs.
Manual, 25 nodes (5x5), collapse node 0/12/24counter names 0.7237; moved 0.0120, 0.0120, 0.0060 (node 24 half off-canvas); 1 node movedPrinted table.
Grid (root-mlp), 9 nodes, collapse node 0/4/8counter names 0.7694; moved 0.0716; 1 node movedPrinted table. Grid zoom-fits, so screen rects are about 2.4x world.
Grid (root-mlp), 25 nodes, collapse node 0/12/24counter names 0.7785; moved 0.0252; 1 node movedPrinted table.
over-naming ratio, counter / moved10.7x (Grid 9) to 120x (Manual 25, node 24); minimum 10.7xDerived from the four rows above.
what it settled
  • THE COUNTER CANNOT NAME A WIDGET BECAUSE IT IS ONE NUMBER SHARED BY ALL OF THEM. add_node hands every node the map's own layout_dirty Rc and containers cascade it down; a bump anywhere is indistinguishable from a bump everywhere. So 'the widgets whose counter moved' is always every widget, and the producer's rect is the union of the whole graph.
  • A collapse does not repack its neighbours in either layout: exactly one node's rect changed in all twelve cases. The real change is local; only the reporting is global.
  • Several producers write the one counter. Sticking the bump inside NodeV2::set_collapsed left the collapse reaching the screen, through another bump on the same flag that was not identified. Only sticking the consumer turned the guard red.
  • Against L-150's 7.444 ms whole-frame pack: at today's producer a sub-rect saves at most 22%-28% of the canvas in three of four configurations, so the pack stays above 5 ms; a producer with a payload would bound it near 0.07x of the frame on these edits.
what it refuted
  • Nothing published is refuted. The entry's own guess that the union would be 'routinely most of the canvas' holds for the counter and fails for the change: those are two different quantities and the entry's prose did not separate them.
what it does NOT settle
  • Real editing. Nothing here is a session; typing in the text editor and dragging a node are still unmeasured (option B).
  • Wires. Node rects only; a collapse moves its pins and the incident wires' damage is not in 'moved', so the true change is larger than 0.6%-7.2%.
  • Drags. During a drag paint.rs rebinds every node every frame on drag_active, without the counter; the probe does not drive pointer input.
  • Which second producer carries a collapse when set_collapsed's own bump is removed.
  • Whether giving notify_dirty a payload (a node index or a rect) is worth its cost in optical. A PM scope call, recorded on L-70 and not filed.

Down to what size does ONE fine representation of a glyph, blurred analytically by covariance inflation, match the exact box-filtered glyph? The filter term alone, with the fitter taken out.

representation2026-09-28copy-pasteable command2 refuted
the answer

THE ANSWER DEPENDS ON THE THINNEST FEATURE IN PIXELS, NOT ON EM SIZE. With a perfect fine representation, so that only the filter differs, inflation holds against the box reference down to 10 px em for a monoline outline (stroke 1/12 em) and breaks at 8 px, where the stroke is 0.67 px. For a hairline outline (hairline 1/60 em) it holds at 96-128 px, is marginal at 48-64 px, and is BROKEN at every size from 10 to 32 px, 0.054-0.081 above the large-size floor (14-21 8-bit steps). Both breaks sit where the thinnest feature falls below about 0.7 px. The mechanism was measured on its own: a gaussian of the box's variance is not a partition of unity, so a 0.2 px horizontal hairline carries 0.638x to 1.356x its true ink depending on sub-pixel position (predicted 1 -/+ 0.361), where box coverage conserves ink exactly. So for monoline UI text there is no volume to build; for a face with hairlines a low-scale correction is real below about 48 px em. That correction is a FILTER correction, which one fit cannot supply, and a better fit would not remove it.

the question it was built to answer

BURNDOWN L-159. The text-as-atoms design (2026-09-28) says the correctly filtered glyph at every size follows from one fit at the finest scale by inflating covariances (S + tI), so only the deviation from that needs fitting. If the residual stays under the noise floor down to about 16 px there is no volume to build; if it breaks at 60 px the volume is real. Measure the residual after analytic inflation from 8 to 200 px.

criterion, and its convention

Reference: exact box coverage of the same outline (fit::coverage::exact_coverage, area of each pixel [px,px+1] covered), which is what a grayscale area rasteriser draws. Prediction: every covered cell of a 48 px-per-unit exact raster, carried as a gaussian of sigma 0.7 fine px and inflated so that the total variance is 1/12 px^2, summed at pixel centres px+0.5. Metric: worst |reference - prediction| over pixels where either is non-zero, the worst taken over four sub-pixel phases (0, 1/4, 1/2 and 3/4 px on both axes). Floor: that metric at the largest size whose fine cell is at most 1/4 px, which is 128 px. HOLDS if within 0.01 of the floor (one 8-bit step plus the 0.0037 that doubling the fine grid moves the floor row). BROKEN if more than 0.03 above it. MARGINAL between. 'Matches down to' is the smallest size such that no size between it and the floor is broken. The criterion was revised twice after the first run, and both revisions are recorded in not_settled.

cargo test -p lights --all-features --test glyph_scale -- --nocapture --test-threads 1

Run 2026-09-28 on the M5 Max, debug build, CARGO_TARGET_DIR = <repo>/target (target_dir.py fell back from the RAM disk, which had 4.25 GiB free against an 8 GiB floor): 5 passed, 1 ignored, in 117 s. It is deterministic: no clock, no threads, no random input. The curve and verdict lines are printed by the_glyph_over_scale_curve. The instrument-resolution figures come from the ignored test:

cargo test -p lights --all-features --test glyph_scale -- --ignored the_finest_row_does_not_move_when_the_fine_grid_doubles --nocapture

RED on a real defect: the first version subtracted the fine cell's variance h^2/12 instead of adding it back. inflation_meets_an_aligned_edge_at_the_box_against_gaussian_constant read 0.039224 against the analytic 0.041632 and failed. Corrected, it reads 0.041632.

what it settled
  • INFLATION WORKS WHERE THE THINNEST FEATURE IS AT LEAST ABOUT 0.8 PX, AND BREAKS BELOW ABOUT 0.7 PX. Monoline holds at 0.83 px and breaks at 0.67 px. Hairline is marginal at 0.80-1.07 px and broken from 0.53 px down. So the correction a format needs is indexed by feature size in pixels, and em size stands in for it only within one face.
  • THE BREAK IS A FILTER PROPERTY, NOT A FIT PROPERTY. The representation here is exact to 6e-5, so a better fitter cannot move these numbers. A gaussian of the box's variance matches the box's second moment and nothing else. On features narrower than a pixel the two differ in shape, and they differ in total ink, because the gaussian's grid sum ripples by 2 exp(-2 pi^2 sigma^2) = 0.386 per point.
  • THE HAIRLINE'S WEIGHT DEPENDS ON SUB-PIXEL POSITION. A 0.2 px hairline carries 64% to 136% of its ink depending on where it sits. This is not a small per-pixel error: it makes a hairline shimmer when text scrolls by a fraction of a pixel, which box coverage never does. Per pixel it is only 0.08, and that is why a worst-pixel metric understates it.
  • Large sizes have a floor of about 0.055, not zero. On every outline the worst pixel sits at 0.054-0.057 from 64 to 128 px, where the aligned-edge constant is 0.0416. Inflation never reproduces box coverage exactly at any size: it reproduces a gaussian prefilter, and the question it can answer is only whether the error grows as the glyph shrinks.
  • For the design, the scheme splits by face. For monoline UI text at 10 px em and above it is one fit and no volume. For hairline faces it needs a correction below about 48 px em, which is also where optical's body text lives. The design's 'only the deviation needs fitting' is right about the shape of the work, but the deviation is a filter mismatch, and a prefilter kernel that is a partition of unity might remove it without any stored volume (not measured).
what it refuted
  • Nothing already published is refuted. The design's premise, that inflation alone gives the correctly filtered glyph at every size, is refuted for any feature below about 0.7 px against a box reference, and it holds above that.
  • The first version of this harness is refuted by its own calibration. It subtracted the fine cell's variance h^2/12 where the point-mass representation needs it added back, and it read 0.039224 against 0.041632. It was corrected before any curve was read.
what it does NOT settle
  • REAL GLYPHS. Both outlines are drawn in the crate's own vocabulary (capsule segments, round caps). No font file was read, because the crate has no reader yet (L-161). Repeating this on a vendored corpus is filed as L-168, blocked on L-161. SETTLED 2026-09-29 by l168-real-glyph-inflation-over-scale: on nine real glyphs the 0.7 px rule is a bound only when the paper between strokes counts (鬱 breaks with its thinnest stroke at 0.798 px and its thinnest gap at 0.38 px), and it is not a predictor of the break.
  • THE FITTER TERM. The fine representation is exact, so this is the best case. How much a real fit of a few hundred Over-composited atoms adds on top, and whether Over's non-closure under blur matters where the atoms overlap, is not measured.
  • THE RENDERER. The prediction is a direct sum, not lights' splat path with screen_dilation. That the renderer implements the same S + tI arithmetic is asserted by the prefilter tests, not by this experiment.
  • APPEARANCE. There is no system-renderer comparison (the entry's second oracle). Below about 32 px CoreText and FreeType hint and stem-darken, so they are not box coverage either, and that comparison could only ever be read, not pass or fail.
  • A BETTER PREFILTER. Whether a partition-of-unity kernel (a tent, a quadratic B-spline, or the box itself analytically) removes the break without a stored volume. That is the obvious next question, and nothing here measures it.
  • THE CRITERION WAS REVISED TWICE AFTER THE FIRST RUN, AND BOTH REVISIONS ARE RECORDED HERE. First it was 'within 1/255 of the 200 px row', which the fine grid cannot resolve: doubling it moves that row by 0.0135. Then it was 'within 0.01 of the 200 px row', anchored on the least accurate row. Now it is the 128 px row, with holds, marginal and broken bands. Under the 200 px anchor the answers are 10 px (monoline) and 24 px (hairline): 32 and 24 px become marginal (0.029 and 0.026 above a floor of 0.0854) and 20 px is still broken. So the hairline's break point moves between 20 and 48 px with the choice of anchor, while 10-20 px is broken and monoline's 10 px is unchanged under every anchor tried.

Does L-159's rule, that inflation holds where the thinnest feature is at least about 0.7 px and breaks below it, hold on real glyphs from real fonts, and does it predict where each one breaks?

representation2026-09-29copy-pasteable command3 refuted
the answer

AS A BOUND YES, AS A PREDICTOR NO, AND ONLY ONCE THE PAPER COUNTS. On nine glyphs from three vendored faces (Inter n o g, Abril Fatface n o e, Noto Sans JP 永 書 鬱), 108 glyph-size rows, no rung broke while its thinnest ink stroke and its thinnest parallel-walled gap between strokes were both at least 0.7 px. Read on ink alone, as L-159 stated it, the rule fails once: 鬱 breaks at 24 px/em with its thinnest stroke at 0.798 px, because its paper is half as thin as its ink (a slot of 0.192 units, 0.38 px there), and its worst pixel sits on that slot. As a predictor the rule is exact for three glyphs and conservative by one to three rungs for six; Inter n, Inter g and 永 never break down to 8 px/em. So 0.7 px stays as a sufficient condition read on both sides of the outline, and "breaks below it" is withdrawn.

the question it was built to answer

BURNDOWN L-168. L-159 measured analytic covariance inflation against exact box coverage on two outlines drawn in the crate's own vocabulary and carried forward one rule: inflation breaks where the thinnest feature drops below about 0.7 px. Real faces have what that vocabulary cannot draw: stroke modulation along a curve, ink traps, overlapping contours, CJK contour density. Run L-159's curve unchanged on a licence-clean corpus chosen for difficulty, calibrations first, and check whether the rule predicts each glyph's break size.

criterion, and its convention

L-159's, unchanged. Reference: exact box coverage (fit::coverage::exact_coverage). Prediction: the glyph rasterised at 48 px per unit with exact coverage, every covered fine pixel carried as a gaussian of sigma 0.7 fine px and inflated to a total variance of 1/12 px^2, summed at pixel centres. Metric: worst |reference - prediction| over support pixels, the worst of four sub-pixel phases. Floor: the 128 px/em row. HOLDS within 0.01 of the floor, BROKEN more than 0.03 above it, MARGINAL between; matches_down_to is the smallest size with no broken size between it and the floor. A glyph is one Region read through ttf-parser at 1/4000 em, 12 units per em, one unit of margin, the crate's SUBPIXEL offset. THE RULE is scored by its predicted size, the smallest rung whose thinnest feature is at least 0.7 px: exact if it equals matches_down_to, conservative if larger, and a violation if smaller; equivalently it is a bound when no broken row has its feature at or above 0.7 px. Thinnest ink is thinnest_feature (2nd percentile of opposed-normal crossings, walls within about 45 degrees of antiparallel). Thinnest gap is thinnest_gap (the same on the paper side, walls within 14 degrees).

what it settled
  • THE RULE IS A BOUND ON ALL NINE GLYPHS, ONCE THE PAPER BETWEEN STROKES COUNTS. No broken rung of 108 has its thinnest ink stroke and its thinnest parallel-walled gap both at or above 0.7 px, and the largest feature at any broken rung is 0.629 px (Inter o at 8 px/em). The number L-159 carried forward survives as a sufficient condition.
  • ON INK ALONE IT FAILS ONCE, AND THE FAILURE IS THE PAPER. 鬱 is broken at 24 px/em (0.1068, 0.0322 above its floor) with its thinnest stroke at 0.798 px. Its thinnest gap is a slot of 16 font units between y = 136 and 152, 0.192 units, 0.38 px at that size. The worst pixel sits on it, and 7 of the 12 worst pixel-phase samples lie within 0.5 px of a gap under 0.7 px against 8.8% of the support, 1 of 12 near a thin stroke. Abril n, whose break is its hairline, shows the other side: 9 and 10 of 12 near thin ink at 24 and 32 px/em against 7.1% and 5.3%, and none near thin paper. A CJK glyph dense enough is thinner in its counters than in its strokes.
  • IT IS NOT A PREDICTOR. With both sides counted the predicted size is exact for Inter o (10), Abril n (48) and Abril o (48), and conservative for six glyphs by one to three rungs. Inter n, Inter g and 永 never break down to 8 px/em with thinnest features of 0.32-0.40 px there. A feature under 0.7 px is necessary for a break and not sufficient.
  • THE BREAK IS A BAND, NOT A FLOOR. Hairline glyphs break at middle sizes and recover at the smallest: Abril n is broken at 24-32 px/em (hairline 0.41-0.55 px) and holds at 8-12 and at 16 (0.14-0.28 px), and Abril o is broken from 14 to 32 and only marginal from 8 to 12. A hairline far below a pixel carries too little ink to be far wrong. L-159's didone-n had the same shape, marginal at 8 and broken 10-32. matches_down_to reports the first break from the top, which answers how small one fit can go and says nothing about the sizes under it.
  • REAL GLYPHS HAVE HIGHER AND MORE VARIED FLOORS. The 128 px/em worst pixel is 0.0495-0.0906 across the corpus against 0.054-0.057 for L-159's round-capped outlines, and every verdict is relative to it: Inter g's floor of 0.0874 lets all twelve of its rungs hold, 8 px/em included at 0.0899. Where each floor's worst pixel sits was not measured.
  • THE PAPER-SIDE INSTRUMENT WAS WRONG FIRST. It shared the ink side's gate and read Inter n's thinnest gap as 0.0898 units, Abril n's as 0.0797 and 永's as 0.1895. Each was one tight cluster at one acute notch, walls 21-29 degrees apart: a paper wedge whose width runs to zero at its apex at every scale, so it belongs to the floor and cannot cause a size-dependent break. The paper skeleton is short, so one notch owned the 2nd percentile. Requiring walls within 14 degrees of antiparallel removes them, and no glyph's predicted size moves anywhere from 0.93 to 0.99. The ink side keeps its 45-degree gate for the opposite reason: under 14 degrees Inter n's ink reads 0.7903 and loses its neck (0.4719, 0.4619 on the raw outline), because one wall of that neck is a vertex.
what it refuted
  • L-159's rule as it was carried forward, "inflation breaks where the thinnest feature drops below about 0.7 px", is refuted in both halves on real glyphs. Read on ink alone it is not a bound (鬱 breaks at 0.798 px), and as a statement about where the break happens six of nine glyphs hold one to three rungs below it. What survives is the bound with the paper counted.
  • No number is withdrawn. Every L-159 figure stands, and the thickness instrument still reads its outlines exactly (t6 1.0000, didone-n 0.2000).
  • The paper-side instrument's first version (the ink side's 0.7 gate on paper) is refuted by its own crossing dump and never fed a published number.
what it does NOT settle
  • APPEARANCE. No system renderer was compared (step 4 of L-168). CoreText and FreeType hint and stem-darken below about 32 px, so that comparison can only ever be read, never passed or failed. Filed as L-173. MEASURED 2026-09-29 by l173-system-rasteriser-oracle, and the premise was wrong in both halves: CoreText does not hint the calibration stems and its smoothing dilates at every size up to a 0.6 px cap, and FreeType 2.13.2 does not stem-darken unless asked. At the 21 rungs this record calls broken inflation's worst pixel is 20.5-40.1 steps from box, and the macOS default 39.3-174.6.
  • THE INK SIDE'S WEDGES. The 45-degree gate the ink side needs for necks also admits tapered stroke ends, which read too thin. Where it was checked on the raw outline (Inter n's neck, 0.4619 against 0.4719) the 2nd percentile lands on a real feature; nothing guarantees that for another glyph. Reading too thin makes the bound more conservative, never wrong.
  • WHY THREE GLYPHS NEVER BREAK. Inter n, Inter g and 永 hold at 8 px/em with features of 0.32-0.40 px. Whether that is the feature's length (Inter n's thinnest is a short neck, not a long hairline), its orientation, or a floor high enough to hide it is not separated.
  • THE CRITERION'S FLOOR is one row's worst pixel. With floors from 0.0495 to 0.0906 the same bands (0.01 and 0.03 above it) mean different absolute errors per glyph, and a high floor can hide a thin-feature error.
  • NINE GLYPHS FROM THREE FACES. Latin sans, one Didone, three CJK. No italic, no script with stacked diacritics or joining, no sweep of a variable axis.
  • THE FITTER, THE RENDERER AND A BETTER PREFILTER, all as in L-159. The fine representation is exact and the prediction is a direct sum. Every break measured here sits on a feature under 0.7 px, which is where a partition-of-unity prefilter would differ from the gaussian; that is still unmeasured.

Where does lights stop being insensitive to per-atom bytes? The 1080p atom ladder extended from 100k to 10M atoms, f32 against f64, both methods.

engineering2026-09-28copy-pasteable command2 refuted
the answer

NOWHERE ON THE LADDER. Doubling every atom's bytes (f32 -> f64) never moves the frame out of the 1.3x noise floor: splat's f64/f32 frame ratio is 0.985-1.019 from 100k to 10M atoms (4.56 GiB resident at f64), and the ray caster's is 1.095-1.174 from 100k to 300k before it REFUSES at 1M on its own 4096-hits-per-pixel budget. There is no crossover rung, so compressing atoms is a CAPACITY question here and not a speed question. BUT THE PREMISE THAT MADE THIS A QUESTION WAS WRONG IN ITS MECHANISM: splat's frame is 76-91% bind, and bind's dominant work is a single-threaded sort of (u32, u64, u32) tile keys whose width does not depend on precision at all, so f32-vs-f64 is blind to that term. The frame is not shown to be ALU-bound; it is shown to be insensitive to the bytes that compression would shrink.

the question it was built to answer

BURNDOWN L-163: record l123-frame-rate-1080p found f32 and f64 too close to call up to 100k atoms and inferred that lights is compute-bound, which is what makes DGF-style atom compression a capacity feature rather than a speed one. At what atom count, if any, does the f64-over-f32 frame-time ratio first leave the 1.3x noise floor -- the crossover at which halving per-atom bytes would start to buy time -- and does such a rung exist within memory?

criterion, and its convention

The f64/f32 ratio of the bench's FRAME column means, with the same ratio of mins beside it, read against this crate's standing noise floor: A DIFFERENCE UNDER 1.3x IS NOISE (single-thread wall time here has a 2.4x spread over identical calls; five reps minimum). The crossover is the first rung whose ratio is >= 1.3x. The bench's own verdict line (95% intervals overlap or not) is reported but is NOT the criterion: tight intervals separate 1.01x differences that the 1.3x rule calls noise. resident_bytes is reported at every rung so that a rung that does not fit is distinguishable from one that fits and is slow.

reproduce it
cargo run -p lights --release --no-default-features --features method-splat --bin lights-bench -- --atoms 100000,300000,1000000,3000000,10000000 --out-dir target/l163-splat
cargo run -p lights --release --no-default-features --features method-raycast --bin lights-bench -- --quick --atoms 100000,150000,200000,300000 --out-dir target/l163-ray
cargo run -p lights --release --no-default-features --features method-raycast --bin lights-bench -- --quick --atoms 1000000 --out-dir target/l163-ray1m

Run 2026-09-28 on an Apple M5 Max (18 cores, 128 GB, macOS 15), release, CARGO_TARGET_DIR=target (target_dir.py fell back from the RAM disk, 4.27 GiB free). --atoms is the one flag L-163 added: it runs ONLY the existing 1080p atom-count axis at the given counts, both precisions, and writes the same 29-column report. Each method is built ALONE because a combined --all-features run over the same ladder aborted the whole sweep at the ray caster's 1M cell (ERROR: budget exhausted at raycast::max_hits_per_pixel: limit 4096, exit 4) before any table was written. The third command reproduces that refusal. Splat: 9 kept reps per cell; ray-cast: 5 (--quick). First rep discarded as warm-up. Reports committed at docs/lights/benchmarks/l163/{splat,raycast}.tsv.

Resident at 300k: 110.58 / 215.70 MiB.
record 16 reproduced at 100k (same binary path, same scene)splat f32 frame 24.44 here vs 23.92 there; ray-cast f32 184.63 vs 183.55; ray-cast f64/f32 1.095 vs 1.07Inside the 1.3x floor in every cell, so the 100k anchor of this ladder is the same measurement as record l123-frame-rate-1080p.
what it settled
  • NO CROSSOVER EXISTS ON THE LADDER, AND THE LADDER ENDS AT A FRAME TIME RATHER THAN AT MEMORY. Splat is within 1.02x of f32 at f64 from 100k to 10M atoms; 10M atoms costs 4.56 GiB at f64 and fits 28 times over in this machine's 128 GB, while its frame is 2.2 s. Any rung where a crossover could still appear (memory would allow roughly 250M f64 atoms at 483 B/atom) is two orders of magnitude past an interactive frame. For every scene lights could draw at a usable rate, halving per-atom bytes buys no time.
  • THE CAUSAL STORY THAT MOTIVATED THE QUESTION IS WITHDRAWN. Record 16 and L-163 read f32~=f64 as 'the inner loop is ALU-bound'. The ladder shows the frame is BIND-bound (76% at 100k, 91% from 1M), and splat's bind is dominated by bin_and_sort's std sort_unstable over (u32, u64, u32) = 16-byte keys whose width does not depend on T. Precision cannot see that term at all, in either direction. What IS established is narrower and is the thing compression cares about: the frame is insensitive to the bytes of the atom arrays, which are the bytes compression would shrink.
  • THE ONE PRECISION-SENSITIVE PHASE IS RENDER, AND IT IS THE SMALL ONE. Splat's render ratio rises 1.12x -> 1.20x from 100k to 3M; the ray caster, whose frame IS its render, climbs 1.10x -> 1.17x and flattens. Neither reaches 1.3x. Note that f64 also halves SIMD lanes, so even these ratios are an UPPER bound on what bytes alone cost.
  • THE RAY CASTER HAS A CEILING THAT IS NOT MEMORY AND NOT TIME: its 4096-hits-per-pixel budget refuses the bench scene between 300k and 1M atoms. That refusal is a result -- a precondition, not a defect -- and it was not relaxed. It also means the ray caster's half of this question cannot be pushed further without a sparser scene.
what it refuted
  • BURNDOWN L-163's why, 'which means the inner loop is ALU-bound and not bandwidth-bound at these scales'. The dominant term at every rung measured is a sort whose data is precision-independent, so neither ALU nor bandwidth is established by an f32/f64 toggle; what survives is only that atom bytes do not move the frame. Withdrawn 2026-09-28; it stopped being a valid inference the moment bind was measured at 76% of the frame (record 16, 2026-09-16) and nobody connected the two.
  • lights_bench.rs's footer sentence 'SplatRaster reports none' for the candidates column. L-61 made splat charge CandidatesTested on 2026-09-15 and this run shows 20,932,574 at 100k; the sentence was corrected in the same change (a splat counts its own footprint's pixels, a ray every atom binned to its tile, 5.8x more at 100k).
what it does NOT settle
  • WHETHER BIND ITSELF IS BANDWIDTH-BOUND. 47M 16-byte keys at 10M atoms is ~750 MB through a single-threaded std sort; that is plausibly memory-bound, but compressing atoms would not touch it and this experiment cannot see it. A key-width or parallel-sort experiment would, and it is outside L-163.
  • ANY SCENE BUT THE BENCH'S. The stress field keeps atom size fixed while count grows, so depth complexity grows with the ladder (kernel evaluations 8.0M -> 15.8M, tile pairs 0.47M -> 47M); a game scene would add atoms by adding AREA or through LOD, not by stacking them. The ray caster's refusal at 1M is a property of that stacking.
  • ANY OTHER MACHINE. aarch64 with unified memory and very high bandwidth only; a narrow-bus x86 laptop could have a crossover this machine does not.
  • QUANTISED PRECISION. f32 is the floor here; a 16-bit or block-quantised arm adds decode arithmetic that neither measured arm contains. L-164 (record l164-dgf-atom-mapping, 2026-09-29) did not build one, by its own rule after this record found no crossover, and priced it instead: an atom block of 17.3-21.3 B would cut the per-atom footprint by 10-16%, because the render loops read the prepared arrays and never the atom.

Is an anisotropic atom closed under an arbitrary affine map in this crate's own parameterisation (mean, rotation quaternion, scale), so that a tetrahedral cage could deform atoms exactly instead of re-fitting them?

representation2026-09-28copy-pasteable command1 refuted
the answer

YES, TO ROUNDING, AND THE ONLY ERROR IS ONE THE UNDEFORMED ATOM ALREADY PAYS. Over 9,519 random atoms under 9,519 random sheared affine maps (2,986 of them reflections), re-extracting (rotation, scale) from A Sigma A^T with a new Jacobi eigensolver reproduces the mapped covariance to 1.98e-15 relative in f64. The density field of the deformed atom, sampled at A x + b, matches the static atom's field at x to 2.6e-10 absolute. Both paths miss the exact quadratic form u.u by the conditioning of inverting a covariance, 71 x kappa x eps for the static atom and 95 x kappa x eps for the deformed one, with kappa up to 1.6e7. Deforming adds nothing that is not already there. The eigensolver itself rebuilds 10,000 SPD matrices to 2.9e-15 (f64) and 1.5e-6 (f32) relative, always as a proper rotation.

the question it was built to answer

BURNDOWN L-165 step (1)+(2): AMD's tetrahedral cage deforms geometry by an affine map per tetrahedron. For a gaussian the family is closed under A Sigma A^T, but lights stores an atom as a Quat plus a Vec3 scale and Sym3 had no eigenvectors, so the map had no way back into the atom's own parameters. With an eigensolver added, does a re-extracted atom reproduce A Sigma A^T, and does its field equal the static field pulled back through the map (deform the atom versus transform the query, which is exactly the choice the cage makes)? And to what stated bound, following L-41's rule of stating a bound rather than assuming bit-identity?

criterion, and its convention

Covariance: max |mapped.covariance() - A Sigma A^T| over max |A Sigma A^T|, the reference computed as an explicit triple sum and not through Sym3::congruence. The bound is 1e-12. Field: the exact quadratic form of a point x = mu + R S u is u.u. Each path's error against it, divided by max(u.u, 1) and by kappa x eps with kappa = (max scale / min scale)^2 of that path's own atom, must be <= 256 for both paths. Eigensolver: reconstruction and orthonormality each <= 64 eps of the precision, and det(V) > 0 in every case. Convention: relative-to-max-entry for matrices, absolute for the field (peak 1).

reproduce it
cargo test -p lights --all-features --test affine_closure -- --nocapture

Run 2026-09-28 on an Apple M5 Max (18 cores, 128 GB, macOS 15), debug profile, CARGO_TARGET_DIR=/Volumes/RamBuild/cargo-target. Prints the EIGEN f64, EIGEN f32 and CLOSURE lines quoted below. Deterministic: a fixed xorshift seed per test, so the numbers reproduce exactly and no timing is involved.

measurementvaluewhere it comes from
CLOSURE f64: re-extracted covariance vs explicit A Sigma A^T, relative to max |entry|1.98e-15 worst over 9,519 atoms (all sheared, 2,986 reflections; maps with |det| < 0.05 skipped)tests/affine_closure.rs::an_affine_map_of_an_atom_is_an_atom_and_its_field_is_the_pulled_back_field, CLOSURE line. Scales log-uniform 1e-2..1; A = I (or -1 in xx for a quarter of cases) + 0.9 x uniform[-1,1] per entry.
CLOSURE f64: deformed field at A x + b vs static field at xquadratic form 1.10e-9 relative (floor 1); field 2.59e-10 absoluteSame line, 16 points per atom at u in [-3,3]^3 in the atom's own frame.
CLOSURE f64: each path against the exact u.u, in units of kappa x epsstatic 71.5; deformed 95.4; deformed kappa up to 1.64e7Same line. The static atom is the renderer's untouched path; the gap between 71 and 95 is the whole cost of deforming.
EIGEN f64, 10,000 SPD matrices: reconstruction / orthonormality / |det-1|2.90e-15 / 1.67e-15 / 1.55e-15tests/affine_closure.rs::the_eigensolver_rebuilds_every_symmetric_matrix_as_a_proper_rotation_and_a_diagonal. Five classes: isotropic, two equal eigenvalues, anisotropy to 1e6 in variance, general, axis-aligned.
EIGEN f32, same 10,000 matrices narrowed1.46e-6 / 7.59e-7 / 8.58e-7Same test. 12.3 x f32 eps; the 64 eps bound holds with 5x margin.
Seen REDmapping by A^T Sigma A (the deliberately transposed map) fails the closure testHand-applied before the fix was restored, and kept as mutation row 'Atom::mapped_affine maps the covariance by A^T Sigma A'.
what it settled
  • THE GAP L-165 NAMED IS CLOSED. Sym3::eigen (cyclic Jacobi, JACOBI_SWEEPS = 32; a product of Givens rotations from the identity, so always a proper rotation, and a reflection-flip branch written for it was deleted when its mutation escaped as unreachable), Quat::from_rotation_matrix (Shepperd), and Atom::mapped_affine(linear, offset) now take an atom through any non-singular affine map and back into (mean, rotation, scale). Similarity was never enough, and the sweep contains no similarity: every one of the 9,519 maps is non-symmetric.
  • DEFORMING THE ATOM AND TRANSFORMING THE QUERY ARE THE SAME FIELD. The pulled-back field differs by 2.6e-10 at worst. Against the exact form the deformed path misses by 95 kappa-eps, while the static path misses by 71. Sheared maps raise kappa, and kappa is what costs; the map itself adds no error class.
  • A SINGULAR MAP DOES NOT INVENT A VOLUME. A rank-2 map returns None or a scale below 1e-6 of the largest, which Atom::validate refuses at MIN_SCALE. The cage must therefore keep every tetrahedron's determinant away from zero; a collapsed tet is refused, not silently flattened.
  • WHY THE RENDERER NEEDS NOTHING ELSE: scene/prepare.rs:77 reads an atom through Atom::covariance(), and beside it only scale.max_component() and scale.min_component() (prepare.rs:71,102-103), which are the square roots of the covariance's extreme eigenvalues whatever parameterisation produced them. No method reads the rotation. So a re-extracted atom is indistinguishable, to rounding, from a fitted one with the same covariance, for every kernel family.
what it refuted
  • BURNDOWN L-165's 'mapping A Sigma A^T back into the atom's own parameterisation has no implementation'. True until 2026-09-28; Atom::mapped_affine is that implementation.
what it does NOT settle
  • THE CAGE ITSELF. No tetrahedral cage, no barycentric per-tet map, no seam treatment (AMD's 1.03 padding) exists. That is L-165's step (3), split to its own entry.
  • DEFORM VERSUS RE-FIT, IN ERROR AND IN TIME. The closure is exact per atom, but an atom straddling a seam between two tets gets one tet's map. How much error that costs, and what a deform costs per atom against a re-fit, is unmeasured. The debug-build test times nothing.
  • A RENDER-LEVEL COMPARISON UNDER SHEAR. CameraSpec has no skew or general 3x3, so 'the camera composed with A' cannot be rendered for a sheared A. The claim stands on the covariance (which is all any method reads) and on the field, not on two rendered images.
  • f32 CLOSURE. The closure sweep runs in f64 only; the f32 eigensolver is measured at 1.46e-6 but the f32 field is not.

Does stopping a hierarchy early at a moment-matched interior node buy frame time inside the error bound, as a principled version of AMD's early-stop, or does it pay AMD's darkening bias too?

representation2026-09-28copy-pasteable command3 refuted
the answer

NO THRESHOLD THAT SUMMARISES ANYTHING IS INSIDE ONE CODE VALUE, AND THE BIAS HAS TWO OPPOSITE SIGNS. The moment match is exact in its own terms: 500 random mixtures merge to their first and second moments to 2.4e-15 relative, with mass conserved to 3.4e-16. But the first merge in every scene already costs 48.6-223 code values of max error (rms 0.001-0.044; 223 is depth-pairs, whose first merge is at tau 16), and every scene is at 197-223 code values by tau 16 px. The summary is optimal for an ADDITIVE 3D density. The renderer projects (image mass is not 3D mass) and composites with over (sub-additive). The 3D cloud therefore DARKENS, with coverage -0.03% at tau 2 and -74% at tau 16, because mass leaks into depth the camera cannot see. The same atoms at one depth BRIGHTEN: +2% at tau 2 and +24% at tau 16. Time does fall, with splat 0.28-0.89x and ray-cast 0.025-0.99x of the uncut frame (debug build). Kernel evaluations RISE for the bimodal scenes (1.07-6.0x), because a merged blob is wider than the pair it replaces. Time saved is per-atom overhead, not per-pixel evaluation.

the question it was built to answer

BURNDOWN L-166: AMD stops BVH traversal before the leaves and treats an interior node as geometry, paying a small darkening bias that it hides with stochastic sampling. For a mixture of gaussians the moment-matched gaussian is the optimal single-gaussian summary, so the same early stop was claimed to be better founded here, with computable error. Build the hierarchy, cut it by screen footprint, and measure a CURVE of image error against time saved. Include a deliberately bimodal case, because moment matching says nothing about the third moment. At which threshold is the error still inside the bound?

criterion, and its convention

Error: the per-channel absolute difference between the cut render and the uncut render of the same tree order, both at 192x144, f64, Gaussian kernel, SharedSettings::contract() (no dilation), threads 1, black background. The bound is ONE 8-bit CODE VALUE (1/255) on the max, a convention chosen here and named as such. It is not the cross-method compose_tolerance, which bounds two methods on the SAME scene, whereas a cut is by construction a different scene. Also printed: rms over all channels, and the signed coverage bias (sum of cut coverage - sum of uncut) / sum of uncut, which is the analogue of AMD's darkening. Time: the median of 5 cold dispatch::render calls against the uncut median; the deterministic proxy is the counters' kernel_evaluations ratio. Threshold tau: the summary's one-sigma screen disc radius in px, from Camera::screen_disc_bound; a node stops when radius < tau.

reproduce it
cargo test -p lights --all-features --test moment_hierarchy -- --nocapture --test-threads 1

Run 2026-09-28 on an Apple M5 Max (18 cores, 128 GB, macOS 15), debug profile, CARGO_TARGET_DIR=/Volumes/RamBuild/cargo-target, 56.6 s. Prints the MERGE line, one CURVE line per (method, scene), and one tau line per threshold. Every count, error and coverage figure is deterministic (fixed xorshift seeds); the milliseconds are debug-build medians of 5 reps with the max/min spread printed beside them.

measurementvaluewhere it comes from

Seen RED4 mutation rows CAUGHT through all four gates: separation term dropped, mass weighted by opacity alone, merged peak from a two-axis area, cut emitting a child for its summarypython3 crates/lights/scripts/mutate_par.py dev 2 with MUT_MERGE=1 MUT_ONLY=two_equal_atoms_merge,a_random_mixture_merges,every_cut_conserves_mass; 116.6 s.
what it settled
  • THE PREMISE IS HALF RIGHT. The moment-matched gaussian IS the optimal single-gaussian summary, and lights now computes it to rounding (scene/hierarchy.rs moment_merge, MomentTree). But it is optimal for the wrong quantity. The image is a projection of an over-composite, not a sum of 3D densities. So the error is not 'computable from the moments'. It has to be measured, and it is 48+ code values at the first merge in every scene tried.
  • AMD'S DARKENING IS REPRODUCED AND DECOMPOSED. Depth spread darkens: 3D mass goes into a merged sz the camera discards, reaching -74% coverage at tau 16. Compositing alone brightens: additive mass fills what over would have saturated, reaching +24% on the flat cloud. They have OPPOSITE SIGNS. A scene mixing both can therefore show a small net bias while being wrong everywhere, and a coverage-only check would pass it.
  • THE LEVER IS PER-ATOM, NOT PER-PIXEL. Kernel evaluations fall only on the flat cloud (0.61x at best). They rise 1.07-6.0x on both bimodal scenes, because a summary is wider than the atoms it replaces. The frame time that falls (splat to 0.28x, ray-cast to 0.025x in debug) is projection, sort, bin and candidate overhead. L-166's 'removes atom evaluations per pixel' is withdrawn.
  • THE OBVIOUS REPAIR IS ALREADY REFUTED BY THE SAME RUN. A per-view 2D moment match (merging projected covariances) would remove the depth-spread term. The flat cloud has no depth spread and is still 48.6-205 code values out, so a 2D merge cannot bring a cut inside one code value either. This prunes that follow-up before it is filed.
  • The tree is an octree over atom means, not the tile binning the entry proposed. A tile holds atoms at all depths, so merging by tile is the depth-pair failure made systematic. A view-independent tree is also built once and cut per camera, in 0.07 ms or less here.
what it refuted
  • BURNDOWN L-166 'the interior node is a principled approximation whose error can be computed rather than dithered away': the moments are exact and the image error is not a function of them. It is 48-223 code values and changes sign with the scene.
  • BURNDOWN L-166 'this removes atom evaluations per pixel rather than bytes per atom': evaluations rise 1.07-6.0x on the bimodal scenes; what falls is per-atom overhead.
  • BURNDOWN L-166 'reuse the existing tile binning as the grouping': the tile binning is per-frame and depth-blind, and grouping by it merges occluder with occluded.
what it does NOT settle
  • A LOOSER CRITERION FOR THE GAME TARGET. At tau 2-3 px the rms is 0.004-0.047 and ray-cast time falls to 0.43-0.99x. Whether that is visible on distant game geometry needs a perceptual criterion the crate does not have. The one-code-value bound is right for the UI target and may be too strict for a far field.
  • RELEASE TIMING. Every millisecond here is a debug-build cold dispatch::render. The ratios are indicative, and the kernel_evaluations ratio is the only deterministic time proxy.
  • OPACITY-AWARE MERGING. A summary that merges in coverage space (1 - prod(1 - alpha)) rather than mass, or that refuses to merge across a depth gap, was not built. The flat-cloud number bounds what such a merge could gain, but it does not measure one.
  • Real scenes. Every scene here is synthetic. A fitted scene with the greedy fitter's confound was not cut.

crates/lights/BURNDOWN L-166 (AMD multi-resolution early stop: 317 / 240 / 218 average nodes per AO ray across three thresholds; darkening bias hidden by stochastic material sampling) · crates/lights/src/camera.rs Camera::screen_disc_bound (the footprint the threshold compares against) · crates/lights/src/blend.rs:135 (alpha = min(opacity * weight, alpha_cap): why a merged opacity above 1 is clipped rather than refused) · crates/lights/src/scene/mod.rs Atom::covariance, Sym3::eigen, Quat::from_rotation_matrix (L-165, the path from a merged covariance back into atom parameters) · Runnalls 2007, 'Kullback-Leibler approach to Gaussian mixture reduction' (moment-preserving merge as the KL-optimal single-gaussian summary)

Does deforming fitted atoms through a tetrahedral cage (one affine map per tet) beat re-fitting them to the deformed target, in the fitter's own error and in time per update?

representation2026-09-28copy-pasteable command2 refuted
the answer

DEFORM WINS AND RE-FIT IS PRUNED AS A PER-UPDATE PATH. A 4x4 cage costs 7.5-9.3 us per update in release against 0.107-0.283 s and 9,520-19,904 renders for one re-fit of the same 8-16 atoms, a ratio of roughly 12,000-33,000x. It keeps the original fit's quality: in the fitter's metric, the deformed fit scores 0.96-1.12x the original fit's own error against its own target. The re-fit scores lower against the deformed truth in all four cases, by 4-12% and by 3.2x once, because it fits the new target directly. The 3.2x case is the confound, not the deform: there the ORIGINAL fit stopped at 11 atoms and the re-fit found 16. A 1-cell cage is NOT enough for a glyph: it misses the pulled-back render by 22 (bend) and 38 (twist) where the per-atom Jacobian misses by 0.32 and 1.51. The cage converges on the Jacobian as cells are added (vs-jacobian falls 21.9 -> 0.016 bend, 39.3 -> 0.013 twist from k=1 to k=16). What remains is the warp's curvature INSIDE one atom's footprint, which no per-atom affine deform removes.

the question it was built to answer

BURNDOWN L-169 (split from L-165 step 3): L-165 proved an atom closed under any affine map. AMD's tetrahedral cage applies one affine map per tet, so an atom gets its containing tet's map. Two things were unmeasured. (a) How much error does that cost against a fresh re-fit to the deformed target, in the fitter's own metric, and against the original fit pulled back through the warp? (b) What does a deform cost per update against a re-fit? L-132 and L-143 price a re-fit at many renders.

criterion, and its convention

Every energy is lights::fit::greedy::residual_energy: the sum over pixels of the squared RGB difference, ink 1 on paper 0, 32x32 viewport. 'truth' is the target's own predicate, supersampled 8x8 per pixel through the exact inverse warp. 'pulled-back' is the ORIGINAL fit's render sampled at the inverse-warped pixel centre, which is what a perfect deform would draw. 'vs-jacobian' is the render with each atom mapped by the warp's own Jacobian at its mean (central difference, h = 1e-4), which is the limit of an infinitely fine cage. Warps act in the image plane under the fitter's orthographic camera, with the image-space width w = 32. The bend wraps onto a circle of radius 1.5w and is isometric along its centre line. The twist is a rotation by 0.6 exp(-r^2 / (2 (0.3w)^2)). Both have analytic inverses. Seam criterion: a caged mean equals the piecewise-affine (Kuhn) interpolation of the vertex images to 1e-12. Pass/fail criteria are stated before reading: the seam test, and the finest cage being closer to the Jacobian deform than one cell. The deform-versus-re-fit verdict is read off the numbers and not asserted.

reproduce it
cargo test -p lights --all-features --test cage -- --nocapture
cargo test --release -p lights --all-features --test cage_refit -- --nocapture --test-threads 1

Both run 2026-09-28 from the repo root with CARGO_TARGET_DIR=$PWD/target exported on an Apple M5 Max (18 cores, 128 GB, macOS 15). That is the SSD fallback that scripts/target_dir.py --explain chose, because /Volumes/RamBuild had 4.1 GiB free against its 8 GiB floor. The energies are deterministic and identical between the debug and release runs. The timings are release: five reps of min/median/max for the deform and for one render, and ONE run for each re-fit, whose render count is the deterministic figure. Each run prints CAGE seam / CAGE affine / CAGEFIT lines.

what it settled
  • THE CAGE IS BUILT AND ITS SEAM RULE IS EXACT FOR THE MEAN. src/scene/cage.rs is a regular grid of Freudenthal tets (six per cell, shared diagonals, so the piecewise-affine map is continuous). Each tet carries A = D' D^-1 and b = v'0 - A v0, and an atom takes the tet with the largest smallest-barycentric at its mean. The means agree with an independent Kuhn interpolation to 9e-16 across faces and diagonals. Mat3::inverse was added for it. Splitting straddling atoms (AMD's other option) was not built, and the next finding is why it is not the lever.
  • THE ERROR IS THE WARP'S CURVATURE INSIDE AN ATOM, NOT THE SEAM. Against the pulled-back render, the per-atom Jacobian (the best any per-atom affine deform can do) already misses by 0.32 (t6 bend) and 1.51 (t6 twist). The k=16 cage is within 0.3-4% of those figures. Its own gap to the Jacobian is 20x-740x smaller. Splitting an atom at a seam would buy only that gap. Splitting it where the warp curves across its length is what would buy the rest.
  • A 1-CELL CAGE IS WRONG FOR A GLYPH, AND 4 CELLS ARE ENOUGH ON THESE WARPS. One affine map for the whole glyph costs 22-38 against the pulled-back render, which is worse than not deforming at all under the bend (1.98). At k=4 the cage is within 1.2-1.4x of the Jacobian, at 7.6-9.3 us per update in release.
  • RE-FIT LOSES ON TIME BY FOUR ORDERS AND WINS ON ERROR ONLY BY FITTING THE NEW TARGET. The re-fit scores 4-12% below the deformed fit against the deformed truth, and 3.2x below where the original fit had stopped at 11 atoms. It is a different fit: against the pulled-back render it is 0.18-2.89 away, so an animated re-fit would also change the look from frame to frame. The deformed fit stays 0.96-1.12x of the original fit's error, so a deform carries the fit's quality with it. What bounds it is the fitter, not the deform: the greedy fitter's own run-to-run spread across targets (11 vs 16 atoms) is larger than anything the deform adds.
  • FOR THE GAME TARGET: 124 ns per atom single-threaded in release is 12.4 ms for 100,000 atoms, three quarters of a 60 Hz frame on one core. Deform is per-atom independent, so it parallelises with the job sweep; that is not measured here.
what it refuted
  • The re-fit-per-update path for animated geometry and for text on a curved surface (BURNDOWN L-169's why: 'Neither can re-fit per frame'). Measured, not assumed: 9,520-19,904 renders and 0.11-0.28 s per update for 8-16 atoms at 32x32, against 7.6-9.3 us for a 4x4 cage.
  • L-169's evidence line 'the only approximation a cage can introduce is at the SEAM'. The seam is the smaller term. The larger is the warp's curvature inside an atom, which the per-atom Jacobian pays too (0.32 and 1.51 against the pulled-back render on the glyph).
what it does NOT settle
  • ATOM SPLITTING where the warp curves across an atom's length. That is the remaining error term. Neither the rule (split when the Jacobian varies by more than X over one sigma) nor its cost is built.
  • A 3D WARP WITH DEPTH. The warps here leave z alone, so every per-tet map is block-diagonal and the fitter's 2D metric applies exactly. A bend out of the image plane needs a 3D reference and a perspective camera.
  • THE FITTER CONFOUND. The re-fit comparison uses the greedy fitter, whose early stop (11 of 16 atoms on t6) moves its error by 3.3x between two targets. A fitter with a stable atom count would give a cleaner re-fit baseline. It would not change the time verdict.
  • PARALLEL DEFORM and f32 deform. Only single-thread f64 is timed.
  • Only two targets, two warps, and a 32x32 viewport. A game scene of 100k atoms is timed but its error is not measured.

Converting a TrueType quadratic to circular arcs offline: does a stated em-space tolerance hold as a two-sided bound, and how does the edge count grow as the tolerance tightens?

representation2026-09-28copy-pasteable command1 refuted
the answer

THE TOLERANCE HOLDS TWO-SIDED, AND THE COUNT GROWS AS THE CUBE ROOT, NOT THE SQUARE ROOT. Over 48 seeded quadratics the worst dense two-sided distance between the converted line+arc edges and the curve is 0.9999x, 0.9979x and 0.9881x the tolerance at 1/100, 1/1000 and 1/4000 em. The edge count is 132, 273 and 419: 3.17x from 1/100 to 1/4000 em, exponent 0.313, where L-161's why predicted about 6x (tolerance^-1/2). The bound holds only because the fitter checks BOTH directions; forward-only acceptance let a near-full-circle arc through three hairpin points stray 18.98x the tolerance.

the question it was built to answer

BURNDOWN L-161 (Tier 0 of docs/lights/TEXT_LAYERS.md): a glyph's quadratics are converted OFFLINE to circular arcs so the approximation is a bounded property of a cached artefact. Two claims were unmeasured: that a tolerance stated in em is actually met, in both directions, by the emitted edges; and the why's scaling, 'biarc count scales roughly as tolerance to the minus one half, so 1/4000 em is about six times the arcs of 1/100 em'.

criterion, and its convention

Two-sided distance, per quadratic: FORWARD is the largest distance from any of 1,025 curve points (t = k/1024) to the nearest emitted edge; BACKWARD is the largest distance from any of 17 points per edge (s = k/16) to a 1,024-chord polyline of the curve. The reported figure is max(forward, backward) / tolerance over the 48 quadratics; pass is <= 1.0. The polyline's own sag at 1/1024 is below 1e-6 em. Edge count is the sum over the 48 quadratics of lines plus arcs. Battery: p0, p2 uniform in the unit em square, p1 uniform in [-0.2, 1.2]^2, LCG seed 0x6c69_6768_7473.

reproduce it
cargo test -p lights --all-features --test outline -- --nocapture

Run 2026-09-28 from the repo root with CARGO_TARGET_DIR=$PWD/target exported (the SSD fallback scripts/target_dir.py --explain chose; /Volumes/RamBuild had 4.1 GiB free) on an Apple M5 Max, macOS 15. The numbers are deterministic (seeded LCG), not timings.

forward-only acceptance, before the backward term (quadratic 23 of the battery)18.98x the tolerance at 1/100 em; accepted arc sweep 6.09 radfirst version of src/outline.rs, seen red in the same test; the scripts/mutations.json row 'outline: the fitter checks only curve-to-edge distance' reproduces it
source non-zero region vs converted winding, 61x61 grid, points farther than 2 tol from the boundary, 1/1000 emring 3,685 compared / 1,460 inside; two overlapping +1 3,671 / 1,053; +1 +1 -1 3,643 / 1,429; 0 disagreementstests/outline.rs::converted_contours_keep_the_source_nonzero_region_away_from_the_boundary
what it settled
  • A STATED EM TOLERANCE IS MET BY THREE-POINT ARCS WITH BISECTION, two-sided, over the battery, at 0.988-0.9999x of it. The fitter's own check is sampled (31 forward and 31 backward points per piece, the backward term to a 128-chord polyline plus that polyline's exact sag bound |p0 - 2p1 + p2| / 4 dt^2), so the bound is measured, not proved; the margin at 1/100 em is 1e-4 of the tolerance.
  • THE COUNT SCALES AS TOLERANCE^-1/3 for this fitter: a circle through three points of a smooth curve errs as h^3. 1/4000 em costs 3.2x the edges of 1/100 em, which makes the fine tolerance cheaper than L-161 assumed.
  • ONE-SIDED ACCEPTANCE IS WRONG, not merely loose: every curve sample can lie on an arc that goes the long way round its circle, so the arc is far from the curve while the curve is near the arc.
  • Winding over arcs must be counted on y-monotone arc pieces with each edge ending at its neighbour's EXACT start; a chord-plus-segment decomposition fails on points lying on a chord, and a recomputed endpoint double-counts a vertex on the ray.
what it refuted
  • BURNDOWN L-161's why: 'Biarc count scales roughly as tolerance to the minus one half, so 1/4000 em is visually exact past poster size for about six times the arcs of 1/100 em' - for the converter built, 3.17x, exponent 0.313. A G1 biarc fitter was not built, so the claim is refuted for this converter only.
what it does NOT settle
  • A REAL FONT. The battery is random quadratics and three synthetic blob glyphs; no glyph has been read from a font file (BURNDOWN L-171).
  • A PROOF of the bound. Both directions are sampled; a pathological quadratic between samples is not excluded.
  • G1 BIARCS: whether tangent continuity changes the count or the coverage error at text sizes.
  • Cubics (CFF curve_to) and the parts/holes partition (BURNDOWN L-170).

A real TrueType face through ttf-parser into Tier 0: does the converted glyph keep the font's non-zero region, and does exact coverage of it match an independent integral of the font's own quadratics?

representation2026-09-29copy-pasteable command1 refuted
the answer

THE ROUND TRIP HOLDS, AND IT FOUND A HOLE ONE LAYER DOWN. Nine glyphs of Ubuntu Light (two of them composites) convert at 1/4000 em to 32-75 edges with no winding disagreement anywhere farther than 2 tolerances from the outline, and L-159's exact_coverage of the converted glyph matches a scanline integral of the raw quadratics to 0.0075 worst pixel at 32 px/em (bound 0.032, i.e. 0.94x of the tolerance in pixels), ink ratio 0.99941-1.00015. Before the fix, the same comparison read 0.491: fit::target ignored PrimitiveSet::regions, so exact coverage of a region-only target decided every pixel at its centre.

the question it was built to answer

BURNDOWN L-171: no glyph from a font file had ever reached outline::ContourBuilder. L-161's close bar was a vendored glyph converted and its coverage checked against L-159's geometric oracle within a stated bound; the entry also asked for curve_to (cubics) and a paper check of CFF and COLR before Tier 0 is locked.

criterion, and its convention

Winding: on a 90x90 grid over each glyph's bounds, nonzero_winding of the converted contours equals the crossing-number winding of the raw segments (each quadratic flattened to 256 chords) at every grid point farther than 2x the tolerance from the converted outline. Coverage: the converted contours as one Region at 32 px/em plus 2 px padding; exact_coverage at max depth 6 against a scanline integral of the raw quadratics (64 sub-rows per pixel, each sub-row's non-zero intervals integrated exactly in x). Pass is worst |difference| <= 4 px of boundary x tolerance in px = 4 x 32/4000 = 0.032. Cubics: the same two-sided distance as l161-quad-to-arc-bound over 48 seeded cubics (control points in [-0.2, 1.2]^2, seed 0x6375_6269_6373).

what it settled
  • A REAL FACE ROUND-TRIPS INSIDE THE STATED BOUND. The coverage error of the converted glyph is below the tolerance in pixels (0.94x at worst), which is what a two-sided em tolerance predicts for a boundary of about one pixel per pixel.
  • L-170'S REGIONS WERE INVISIBLE TO THE FITTER'S TARGET. PrimitiveSet::combined read regions; Target::clearance, nearest_surface, nearest_feature, feature_ids, primitive_count and description_bytes_at did not, so exact_coverage treated a region's boundary as infinitely far and sampled at centres. Fixed in the same entry, with Region::gradient as a central difference.
  • TIER 0 IS NOT CLOSED UNDER AN AFFINE MAP (paper, COLR v1): an arc under skew or non-uniform scale is elliptical. A transformed paint or a synthetic oblique must be converted after the transform, so Tier 0 is keyed by the transform. Written into docs/lights/TEXT_LAYERS.md.
what it refuted
  • TEXT_LAYERS.md's row 'one dev-dependency, tempfile' is no longer true (ttf-parser added) and was amended in place with its date.
what it does NOT settle
  • A REAL CFF GLYPH. cubic_to is built and measured on seeded cubics only; no licence-clean CFF face was available offline.
  • A REAL OVERLAP OR HAIRLINE. Neither vendored-candidate face has one; the overlap cases rest on tests/outline.rs's synthetic inputs, and a variable font's unmerged masters are the likely real source.
  • COLR BUILT. v0 and v1 are checked on paper only.
  • Sizes other than 32 px/em, and the glyph-over-scale curve on this face (BURNDOWN L-168, now unblocked).

What do CoreText and FreeType actually draw for L-168's nine glyphs, placed exactly where the harness places them, and is analytic inflation's error larger or smaller than each platform's own departure from exact box coverage?

representation2026-09-29copy-pasteable command3 refuted
the answer

THE PLATFORMS DEPART FROM THE GEOMETRY FURTHER THAN INFLATION DOES, AND CORETEXT'S GLYPH RENDERER IS NOT A GEOMETRIC REFERENCE. With placement calibrated to 4 8-bit steps on square stems, L-168's nine glyphs at 8-32 px/em put inflation's worst pixel 13.8-40.1 steps from exact box coverage. The macOS default (font smoothing, dark ink on light) is 31.4-201.8 steps from it and FreeType's hintslight 21.9-234.9: further than inflation at 72 and 71 of 72 glyph-rungs, and at 21 and 20 of the 21 rungs L-168 calls broken. Only the geometric paths stay inside inflation's range, CoreText's path filler at 0.9-17.2 and unhinted FreeType at 4.2-24.3. It compares magnitudes, not visibility.

the question it was built to answer

BURNDOWN L-173, split from L-168's step (4). L-168 measured analytic inflation against exact box coverage on nine real glyphs and found the 0.7 px rule a bound once the paper between strokes counts. Box coverage is the geometric truth, not what a reader sees, and the entry held that below about 32 px CoreText and FreeType hint and stem-darken, so an error of 0.1 on a hairline may or may not be visible beside what the platform already draws. Nothing in the crate had rendered a glyph through a system rasteriser.

criterion, and its convention

Placement: the harness writes each row's pixel origin, where font unit (0, 0) lands, as offset*k + (phase, phase) with k = em_px/12, through the same placed() that builds every corpus target; the drivers draw at that origin and never compute one. Calibration: worst |system - box| (fit::glyph_scale::residual over support pixels, in 8-bit steps of 1/255) over 128 committed images of a vendored calibration font (a square stem, a hairline stem, a quadratic ring, an overlapping cross flagged OVERLAP_SIMPLE) at 8-32 px/em and four phases. The stems must sit within 4 steps in CoreText's glyph renderer, its path filler and FreeType unhinted. The comparison: for each corpus glyph and rung, the worst of four phases of three residuals: box to inflated (L-168's, recomputed), box to system, and system to inflated, for six system modes. It is a COMPARISON, never a pass or a fail.

what it settled
  • THE PLATFORM IS FURTHER FROM THE GEOMETRY THAN INFLATION IS, AT EVERY SIZE A READER SEES. At 8-32 px/em the macOS default sits 1.59-10.91 times inflation's worst pixel away from box coverage (median 3.98) and FreeType's hintslight 0.64-10.39 times (median 3.39). At the 21 rungs L-168 calls broken, where inflation's worst pixel is 20.5-40.1 steps, the macOS default is 39.3-174.6. By the only yardstick measured here, the worst pixel, the bound's failures are smaller than the departures readers already live with. The two differ in kind: dilation and grid-fitting are systematic, inflation's error is a blur. Nor does inflation move the image toward what a platform draws: for the smoothed and hinted modes the |x - inflated| matrix is of the same size as |x - box|.
  • CORETEXT'S GLYPH RENDERER IS NOT A GEOMETRIC ORACLE, EVEN WITH SMOOTHING OFF. It draws square stems within 2.6 steps of box at every rung, and it approximates the rest. Curves are chord-flattened: on the ring 23.5-60.2 steps over 8-128 px/em, an inscribed polygon, a tolerance roughly constant in pixels. Overlaps behave as if summed instead of unioned: the flagged cross sits 43.6-65.0 steps off at every size, with up to 6.2% extra ink at 8-32 px/em, where the path filler is exact to 1.0 and FreeType, which honours OVERLAP_SIMPLE, to 6.5. On the corpus that makes 書, which has no curves, 58.8-67.2 steps from box while the path filler is at 0.9-1.0. Any oracle for lights's text must be the path filler or FreeType, never CTFontDrawGlyphs.
  • FONT SMOOTHING IS A DILATION THAT GROWS WITH SIZE, NOT A SMALL-SIZE EFFECT. About 0.0117 px of stem width per px/em for dark ink on light paper, reaching a 0.6 px cap at 64 px/em and holding it to 200; about 0.032 per px/em for light ink on dark, capped from 20 px/em. It adds 0.4-35% ink to the corpus at 8-32 px/em for dark text and 16-75% for light, and on the CJK glyphs at 48 px/em and above it departs from box by 214-255 steps.
  • FREETYPE DARKENS NOTHING BY DEFAULT AND HINTS ONLY WHEN ASKED. no-stem-darkening is 1 in all four of 2.13.2's drivers. Unhinted it sits 4.2-24.3 steps from box at 8-32 px/em: conics are flattened to 1/16 px, and flagged overlaps are oversampled. Hintslight changes every one of 624 images and sits up to 245.7 steps from box.
  • THE PLACEMENT IS THE HARNESS'S AND IT HOLDS. The stems sit within 2.6 (glyph renderer), 1.7 (path filler) and 3.6 (FreeType) steps at every rung from 8 to 128 px/em. The floor is each rasteriser's own coordinate grid: FreeType places to 1/64 px, 0.98 steps on that grid and 3.62 off it over 240 origins. Every harness defect that could matter is 60 steps or more, and three of them are mutation rows that are caught.
what it refuted
  • "Below about 32 px CoreText and FreeType hint and stem-darken" (L-173's why and l168's APPEARANCE item) is wrong in both halves for the systems measured. CoreText applies no hinting to the calibration stems, which it draws within 2.6 steps of box at every rung from 8 to 128 px/em with smoothing off, and its smoothing darkens at every size up to a 0.6 px cap. FreeType 2.13.2 does not stem-darken unless asked and hints only under a hinting load flag. Corrected in place in l168 with its date.
  • The entry's calibration bar, "about one 8-bit step", is below what either platform reaches off its own grid. FreeType places an origin to 1/64 px, worst 3.62 steps off-grid, and CoreText's glyph renderer reaches 2.6 on stems. The bar used is 4 steps, with its terms stated.
  • The working assumption that a system rasteriser with smoothing and hinting off draws the geometry. CoreText's glyph renderer does not: it chord-flattens curves, adds overlapping contours whether or not they are flagged, and at 10 and 16 px/em in the calibration font copies a bottom edge into the row below when the edge lies within 0.008 px above a row boundary.
what it does NOT settle
  • VISIBILITY. The worst pixel is not a perceptual measure. The platforms' departures are systematic and their readers are adapted to them, while inflation's error is a blur. No observer and no perceptual model was used, so the magnitudes say only that the bound's failures are smaller than what each platform already does.
  • OTHER PLATFORMS AND PIPELINES. Only CoreText on macOS 26.5 and FreeType 2.13.2 were measured: no DirectWrite, no Skia or Chrome, no LCD sub-pixel antialiasing, no display gamma. Every image is 8-bit linear coverage.
  • THE FILES, NOT THE FACES. Inter and Noto Sans JP are instanced variable fonts whose glyphs keep their overlaps, so CoreText's coverage summing belongs to these files; a build with overlaps removed would not show it. The hintslight column depends on which reference glyphs the three-glyph subsets contain.
  • THE EDGE BAND'S CAUSE. A bottom edge less than 0.008 px above a row boundary is copied into the row below at 10 and 16 px/em in the calibration font, and not in a throwaway stem-only font. Why the rest of the font matters is unknown.
  • THE MECHANISMS. Chord flattening and coverage summing are inferred from error maps; Apple documents neither.
  • WHETHER CORETEXT RUNS A HINTED FACE'S BYTECODE. Abril Fatface carries fpgm and prep, and its glyph-renderer departures (22.6-76.4 steps) are not separated here from curve flattening.
  • THE FITTER AND LIGHTS'S OWN RENDERER, as in L-159 and L-168: the prediction is a direct sum of exact fine atoms.
artefacts on disk
