What does AMD's Dense Geometry Format map onto in a lights atom, what would a DGF-style atom block cost, and what would compressing the atom buy on this machine?

prior-art2026-09-29copy-pasteable command6 refuted
the answer

ONE FIELD MAPS, AND COMPRESSING THE ATOM WOULD SHRINK THE ONE ARRAY THE RENDERER NEVER READS. DGF encodes positions, strip topology and IDs; of an atom's 14 scalars only the three of mean take its encoding verbatim, and rotation, scale, opacity and colour are what the paper calls sideband data. The render loops read PreparedScene (108 B per atom at f32, 216 at f64) and per-frame screen atoms and sort keys, never the 56- or 112-byte Atom, so an atom block budgeted at 17.3-21.3 B would cut the per-atom footprint L-163 measured by 10-16% and move no frame. Not built, by L-164's own rule once L-163 found no crossover. Six sentences of L-164's why are withdrawn: two about the tree, one about L-163 and three about the paper.

the question it was built to answer

BURNDOWN L-164: AMD's Dense Geometry Format (Barczak, Benthin and McAllister, HPG 2024) compresses triangle meshes into 128-byte blocks for hardware ray tracing. Which of its fields map onto a lights Atom, what would a DGF-style atom block cost per atom, and, with L-163 having found no compute-to-bandwidth crossover, what would compressing the atom buy on this machine?

criterion, and its convention

A DGF field MAPS when an atom field can take its encoding verbatim; an encoding the paper does not contain is recorded as the codec's own choice and not as a mapping. A claim about the tree counts only if a test in the tree pins it AND has been seen to fail under the opposite claim. The capacity case is read by record l163's rule, under which the atom is a capacity feature only, so its worth is the fraction of the per-atom resident footprint it removes, in bytes. Every paper figure names its table or section, and no figure is carried from L-164's why unless it was found in the paper.

reproduce it
cargo test -p lights --all-features --test scene -- --exact atom_covariance_is_r_s_and_not_s_r push_normalises_the_quaternion resident_bytes_charges_each_stored_vector_exactly_once

Run 2026-09-29 on an Apple M5 Max, debug, CARGO_TARGET_DIR=/Volumes/RamBuild/cargo-target: 3 passed. The three tests predate this record and pin the only facts about the tree the mapping rests on: an Atom's scale is a linear sigma, a stored rotation is a unit quaternion, and PreparedScene holds nine arrays at 108 / 216 B per atom. L-164 added two mutation rows that make the first two fail under the exact claims withdrawn here, scale read as a log-sigma and the quaternion kept unnormalised: MUT_ONLY='L-164:' MUT_MERGE=1 python3 crates/lights/scripts/mutate_par.py dev 2, both CAUGHT through all four gates in 139.9 s (docs/lights/mutations/results.tsv rows 572-573; the third test's row, 240, was already CAUGHT). Every DGF figure is read from the paper (sources); every other number is either record l163-bandwidth-crossover's measurement or arithmetic whose terms are in its note. No encoder was built, so no image error was measured.
what it measured
measurementvaluewhere it comes from
DGF block and vertex code (paper)128-byte blocks of at most 64 triangles and 64 vertices; a 20-byte header; a 24-bit signed anchor per axis, an unsigned 1-16-bit offset per vertex per axis and an 8-bit IEEE exponent, decoded as V = (A + O) * 2^(E-127); the x, y and z offset widths sum to a multiple of 4 'to minimize muxing cost in a possible hardware decoder'Barczak, Benthin and McAllister 2024, section 3.2 and 3.2.1, pp. 3-4.
DGF bytes per triangle against uncompressed (paper)DGF 2.74-3.30 at b = 11 up to 3.68-6.95 at b = 24 (lowest-highest over the model set); 2.87-3.29 at b = 14 on eight scanned models; DGF blocks 3.47-5.36 at b = 16. Uncompressed: 18 for a vertex and index buffer; 32.00-35.78 for the DXR BVH's triangle leavesTable 1 (p. 9), Table 3 (p. 11), Table 4 (p. 11). Nanite is quoted at roughly 9 in memory and 5.6 on disk (section 5.2).
DGF error (paper)max error roughly 1.8x the mean, proportional to the grid step: mean 0.0314 / 0.0157 / 0.0078 % of the AABB diagonal at b = 11 / 12 / 13 on the lowest modelSection 5.1, Table 1.
DGF decoded in software (paper, Radeon RX 7900 XT, 1080p)frame time 1.89x (Telegraph) and 2.38x (Crytek Sponza) the uncompressed reference; VALU utilisation 89% and 94% against 50% and 68%; L2 misses 0.65x and 0.63xTable 5 (p. 12). Positions only, decoded in intersection shaders; the paper's case rests on future fixed-function hardware (abstract, section 1).
lights Atom, the source14 scalars: 56 B at f32, 112 B at f64src/scene/mod.rs:15-21; Vec3, Rgb and Quat hold 3, 3 and 4 scalars of T and nothing else (src/numerics/vector.rs:10-16, src/numerics/quaternion.rs:6-11), so no padding is possible.
PreparedScene per atom, the arrays the render loops read108 B at f32, 216 B at f64 over nine arrays: mean 12 / 24, sigma 24 / 48, lambda 24 / 48, whiten 24 / 48, opacity 4 / 8, colour 12 / 24, params 0 (Gaussian), scale_max 4 / 8, scale_min 4 / 8tests/scene.rs::resident_bytes_charges_each_stored_vector_exactly_once, run by the command above; src/scene/prepare.rs:17-28.
renderer resident per atom, marginal from 1M to 10M atoms279 B at f32, 483 B at f64Record l163-bandwidth-crossover, splat at 1920x1080. It counts the framebuffer, the strategy and the prepared scene (src/renderer.rs:119-123) and not the source Scene.
per-frame state per atom (derived)about 171 B at f32 and 267 B at f64The row above minus PreparedScene's 108 / 216; not measured separately. It is the screen atoms plus 16-byte (tile, depth, slot) sort keys (src/method/splat/bin.rs:11), 4.71 keys per atom at 10M atoms (47,111,253 tile pairs, record l163).
atom block budget (arithmetic; no encoder was built)136 bits = 17 B per atom: mean 3 x 16 bits, rotation 32 (smallest-three, 2 + 3 x 10), scale 3 x 8 on log2 sigma, opacity 8, colour 3 x 8. With DGF's 20-byte header, 6 atoms fit a 128-byte block (21.3 B per atom) and 64 atoms fill 1,108 bytes (17.3 B per atom)Mean takes DGF's widest offset; the other widths are common game-engine choices and were not judged against image error.
what the atom block would savef32: 56 B of a 335 B per-atom footprint becomes 17.3-21.3, 10.4-11.6% smaller, 1.12-1.13x atoms in the same memory. f64: 112 of 595 becomes 17.3-21.3, 15.2-15.9% smaller, 1.18-1.19x. Replacing PreparedScene as well: 1.74-1.78x at f32 and 2.06-2.09x at f64, with preparation moved into every bindArithmetic from the four rows above (footprint = source Atom + L-163's marginal). The frame moves by nothing (record l163).
what it settled
  • ONE FIELD MAPS VERBATIM, AND IT IS THE POSITION. mean takes DGF's vertex code as it stands: a 24-bit signed anchor per axis, unsigned offsets of 1-16 bits per axis, and an 8-bit IEEE exponent, decoded as V = (A + O) * 2^(E-127) (section 3.2.1). Its worst error is half a grid step per axis, and DGF's measured max error is roughly 1.8x its mean and halves with every added bit (section 5.1, Table 1). The primitive ID maps too: DGF stores a 29-bit base in the header and adds the slot (section 3.2.3), which is exactly an atom index.
  • EVERYTHING ELSE IN AN ATOM IS WHAT DGF CALLS SIDEBAND DATA. DGF carries no per-vertex attributes; it hands them back to the application through its encoder's reordering tables (section 3.3). Rotation, scale, opacity and colour, 11 of an atom's 14 scalars, have no DGF counterpart, and the encodings a codec would use for them are its own. DGF's 'opacity' is not the atom's either: it is a 1-bit any-hit flag concatenated with a 24-bit geometry ID (section 3.2.3). Its whole topology section, control bits, first-index bits and a re-use buffer (section 3.2.2), exists because triangles share vertices; atoms share nothing, so that has no counterpart.
  • SCALE IS LINEAR ON THE ATOM L-164 NAMED, AND LOGARITHMIC ON A DIFFERENT ONE. Atom::covariance multiplies R diag(scale) by its own transpose (src/scene/mod.rs:50-54), so scale is a standard deviation. atom_covariance_is_r_s_and_not_s_r pins it (an isotropic sigma of 0.05 gives Sigma_xx = 0.0025), and it has been so since the crate's first commit (84311742, 2026-09-08). The fitter's Atom2d is the one that stores log_scale (src/fit/atom2d.rs:17, 33, 41). The coordinate ladder steps it additively (src/fit/greedy.rs:458-461), a saved fit writes it (src/fit/atoms_io.rs:12-13), and fit::atom2d::to_atom (atom2d.rs:137) exponentiates it into an Atom. So a log grid comes free for a saved 2D fit and is the codec's own work for a scene. A DGF-verbatim shared exponent over a linear sigma gives a relative error proportional to 1/sigma inside a block; only a grid on log2 sigma gives a constant one.
  • ROTATION TAKES SMALLEST-THREE, WHICH IS NOT IN DGF. Scene::ingest normalises every quaternion it stores (src/scene/mod.rs:163-187, pinned by push_normalises_the_quaternion), so a stored rotation is unit and q and -q are one rotation. Two bits name the largest component and three 10-bit fields hold the others, 32 bits in all. It is a standard game-engine encoding with an error of its own, which this record did not measure.
  • THE BLOCK IS NOT THE TILE. DGF's blocks come from binned-SAH clusters in object space, at most 128 triangles each (section 4.1), packed greedily with Morton-code tie-breaks (section 4.3). The binning L-164 named is rebuilt at every bind. Splat clears and re-sorts one (tile, depth, slot) key per atom-tile pair (src/method/splat/bin.rs:11, 52, 80), 4.71 keys per atom at 10M atoms (record l163), and the ray caster rebuilds its TileBinIndex per camera (src/method/raycast/implementation.rs:164). That is a view-dependent set of references, not a partition of storage, which is what L-166 found when it tried to reuse it. The object-space grouping the crate does have is L-166's MomentTree (src/scene/hierarchy.rs:81-163). It is an octree over atom means that sorts its own copy of the atoms into octant order down to single atoms, so the atoms under any node form one contiguous, spatially compact run. That run is the analogue of DGF's clusters and Morton packing; nothing yet cuts it into bounded blocks.
  • THE 128-BYTE BLOCK DOES NOT TRANSFER AS A COUNT. It holds 64 triangles because triangles share vertices and carry only positions (DGF still duplicates vertices across blocks by 1.49-2.16x, Table 1). An atom carries 14 scalars and shares none. At the 17 B budget in the numbers, a 20-byte header leaves room for 6 atoms: 21.3 B per atom, against 17.3 B for a 64-atom block of 1,108 bytes. DGF sizes its block to the cache line (section 3.1), so the choice between the two is a fetch-granularity question this record did not measure.
  • COMPRESSING THE ATOM SHRINKS THE ONE ARRAY THE RENDERER NEVER READS. The render loops read PreparedScene's nine arrays (src/scene/prepare.rs:17-28) and the strategy's per-frame screen atoms and sort keys. Renderer::resident_bytes counts exactly those plus the framebuffer (src/renderer.rs:119-123), and the source Scene sits outside it. Against L-163's measured marginal of 279 / 483 B per atom (f32 / f64) plus the 56 / 112-byte source, a 17.3-21.3 B atom block saves 10.4-11.6% of the per-atom footprint at f32 and 15.2-15.9% at f64. That is 1.12-1.19x more atoms in the same memory, where L-163 already found memory is not what ends the ladder: 10M atoms at f64 is 4.56 GiB of 128 GB, and the frame is 2.2 s.
  • THE CEILING OF THE CAPACITY CASE COSTS THE FRAME ITS PREPARATION. Only replacing PreparedScene with blocks reaches further: the per-atom footprint would fall to the block plus the per-frame state (about 171 / 267 B), 1.74-2.09x more atoms per GB. But the 108 / 216 prepared bytes exist so that no render loop recomputes a covariance, its Cholesky factor and its inverse (src/scene/prepare.rs:70-104), and a block in their place moves a decode and all three into every bind. The paper measured the simpler case, decoding positions alone in software, and it cost 1.89x and 2.38x the reference frame time at 89-94% VALU utilisation while cutting L2 misses to 0.65x and 0.63x (Table 5). Its case rests on fixed-function hardware (section 1), which a CPU does not have, and L-163 shows there is no bandwidth here for the extra arithmetic to buy back.
  • A DEFORMED BLOCK RE-ENCODES TEN OF FOURTEEN SCALARS. DGF requires vertex positions to update without re-blocking (section 3.1). Under L-169's cage an atom's rotation and scale change with its mean, because Atom::mapped_affine rebuilds both from an eigendecomposition of the mapped covariance (src/scene/mod.rs:56-70). So every deformed atom re-encodes 10 of its 14 scalars where a deformed triangle re-encodes only its positions.
  • ATOMS HAVE NO CRACKS, SO EACH BLOCK MAY CHOOSE ITS OWN EXPONENT. DGF's encoder snaps the whole model to one global grid, 'thus preventing cracks' (section 4.2), even though the header carries an exponent per block. Atoms share no vertices, so a per-block exponent costs nothing in watertightness, and a block of small atoms can take a finer grid than a block of large ones.
what it refuted
  • BURNDOWN L-164's why, 'scale is ALREADY LOGARITHMIC, so a linear grid on it is a geometric grid on true scale ... and a shared per-block exponent is precisely what a log scale already encodes'. False for the Atom its where names (src/scene/mod.rs:15-21, 50-54), which has held a linear sigma since the crate's first commit (84311742, 2026-09-08); true of the fitter's Atom2d, which the entry did not name. It was never true of the object it was said about. Withdrawn 2026-09-29.
  • BURNDOWN L-164's why, 'the BLOCK is free: L-20's silhouette-conic tile binning already groups atoms spatially, which is the property that makes an anchor tight'. Both methods rebuild their binning at every bind, and splat's duplicates each atom into every tile it touches (4.71 keys per atom at 10M); it groups references per view, not atoms in storage. L-166 reached the same conclusion on 2026-09-28, the day L-164 was raised, and built MomentTree instead, so the sentence was false when written. Withdrawn 2026-09-29.
  • BURNDOWN L-164's why, 'It reaches about 5.6 to 6.9 bytes per triangle against 18 to 21 uncompressed'. The paper reports 2.74-6.95 B per triangle across b = 11-24 (Table 1) and 2.87-3.29 at b = 14 (Table 3), against 18 B per triangle for a vertex and index buffer (Table 3) and 32.00-35.78 for the DXR BVH's leaves (Table 4). 5.6 B per triangle is Nanite's on-disk rate, quoted in section 5.2, and 21 does not appear. Withdrawn 2026-09-29.
  • BURNDOWN L-164's why, 'the paper states its error bound in exactly the form this crate states its own'. DGF reports mean and max vertex displacement as a percentage of the AABB diagonal and observes max ~= 1.8x mean (section 5.1, Table 1); this crate states a worst case against a named convention and a stated bound (L-41, L-165). What transfers is that the error is proportional to the grid step, which is what a stated bound needs, and not the form. Withdrawn 2026-09-29.
  • BURNDOWN L-164's title and why, 'maps onto an atom almost field for field' and 'the mapping onto Atom is unusually clean'. One field of five maps (mean, 3 of 14 scalars), plus the primitive ID as an atom index; smallest-three and a log grid are codec choices the paper does not contain. Withdrawn 2026-09-29; the title is kept as the entry's name.
  • BURNDOWN L-164's why, 'L-163 says ALU is the scarce resource'. L-163 withdrew that inference itself (record l163-bandwidth-crossover, refuted): the frame is 76-91% bind, dominated by a precision-independent key sort, and what is established is only that the frame is insensitive to the atom's bytes. Withdrawn 2026-09-29.
what it does NOT settle
  • THE IMAGE ERROR OF ANY QUANTISED ATOM. The widths in the budget (16-bit offsets, 10-bit smallest-three, 8-bit log2 sigma, 8-bit opacity and colour) are choices, not measurements. Judging them is the quantised precision arm L-164 described, to be built only if a crossover appears, and L-163 found none.
  • THE COST OF DECODE AND PREPARATION IN BIND, if blocks ever replaced PreparedScene. Not measured; the paper's software decode of positions alone is the only figure, and it was taken on a GPU.
  • SIZE ON DISK AND LOAD TIME. A saved scene is text, 14 Debug-formatted f64 columns per atom (lights-scene v1, src/scene/io.rs:9-64), and a saved fit carries log_scale (src/fit/atoms_io.rs). A binary block format would shrink both, and that is the one place bytes matter independently of the frame. Neither size was measured.
  • HOW TIGHT AN ANCHOR IS ON A MOMENTTREE RUN: the offset bits a run of 64 atoms in octant order needs for a given position tolerance. Not measured.
  • ANY OTHER MACHINE. The capacity arithmetic inherits L-163's M5 Max measurement, and a narrow-bus x86 laptop could have the crossover this machine does not.

What does optical cost TODAY on eframe + egui + wgpu? The incumbent's baseline the lights port will be diffed against: eleven real showcase frames at 1920x1080 points, at 1x and 2x.

engineering2026-09-29copy-pasteable command2 refuted
the answer

UNDER ONE MILLISECOND OF CPU AND UNDER 0.7 ms OF GPU FOR EVERY FRAME OPTICAL DRAWS TODAY. Over all eleven showcase tabs at 1920x1080 points, the whole CPU frame (layout, tessellation, upload calls, pass encoding) is 0.1654-0.9011 ms at 1x and 0.1679-0.9792 ms at 2x, and the render pass is 0.1285-0.2482 ms and 0.3875-0.6685 ms on the M5 Max GPU. So the bar a lights port must clear is about one millisecond, not 16.67: L-150's 7.444 ms CPU pack alone is 8.3x the dearest egui CPU frame measured here. And the scale factor splits the two stacks: egui's CPU frame does not move from 1x to 2x (0.99-1.09x) while a CPU rasteriser's pixel count quadruples.

the question it was built to answer

BURNDOWN L-174 (PM, 2026-09-29): a baseline must be recorded BEFORE any port, so that the ported optical is judged against the incumbent rather than against an argument. What does one frame of the REAL optical showcase cost on eframe 0.31.1 + egui 0.31.1 + egui-wgpu 0.31.1 + wgpu 24.0.5 on Metal - CPU per phase, GPU pass time, vertex, index and draw counts, font atlas, texture and resident bytes - for three scenes that rank differently, at 1x and 2x? No number in the repository said, and the 35 records before this one time atom renders, one fit, a CPU pack and a monomorphisation count, never a UI frame.

criterion, and its convention

Five reps per scene and scale, each its own child process with an empty private HOME, in rep-major order; per rep the MEDIAN over 60 measured frames after 30 warm-up frames, with the pointer swept across the frame and time advanced 1/60 s per frame; the published figure is the median of the five rep medians with the min..max of the five beside it, and a 1.3x difference is noise on this machine. CPU = egui layout (App::update inside Context::run) + epaint tessellation + the texture and buffer upload calls + render pass encoding. GPU = the render pass's own timestamp pair, period 1 ns, checked against the wall clock. The submit-to-fence time is reported beside it and never added to either. A frame is validated before it is quoted: egui must report the requested pixels per point, the pass must ink pixels, the end timestamp must follow the begin one, and all five reps must hash alike on their deterministic counters.

reproduce it
cargo run -p optical --release --bin unified_showcase -- --frame-bench --out docs/lights/benchmarks/l174

Run 2026-09-29 on an Apple M5 Max (18 cores, 128 GB, macOS 26.5), release profile: 110 child processes (11 tabs x 2 scales x 5 reps) in about 40 s of wall time. The instruments are tested in optical by cargo test -p optical --bin unified_showcase -- bench:: (5 tests) and cargo test -p optical --test frame_bench (2 tests), and the artefact by cargo test -p lights --all-features --test optical_baseline (4 tests).

RE-RUN 2026-09-29 (L-199), AND PINNED TO A COMMIT. This command no longer runs on the tree: d9ea50fe ('optical: revert the lights-support changes', the same day, under the ruling that optical's source is off limits) deleted the frame bench, and unified_showcase ignores a flag it does not parse, so at HEAD the command exits 0 having written nothing - it did exactly that, after 409.9 s. It reproduces at 8e9a15ba, the revert's parent: `git archive 8e9a15ba -- . ':!docs' | tar -x -C <DIR>` and the command above from <DIR>, with the output moved to scratch. Run that way, baseline.tsv matched docs/lights/benchmarks/l174 in every census column of all 22 rows, counters_hash included; only timings and rss moved. The report's ledger in this file holds the run.
what it measured
measurementvaluewhere it comes from
the three named scenes at 1x (1920x1080 px): CPU median [min..max of five rep medians], GPU pass mediantext-heavy tree: cpu 0.1654 [0.1606..0.1666] ms, gpu 0.1290 ms; fill-heavy canvas: cpu 0.9011 [0.5090..1.0155] ms, gpu 0.2482 ms; op-heavy overview: cpu 0.8092 [0.6501..0.8099] ms, gpu 0.1909 msdocs/lights/benchmarks/l174/baseline.tsv, rows ppp=1. canvas's 2.00x spread across reps is above the 1.3x noise line and is the P/E-core spread, not the scene: its five reps drew identical frames.

the GPU clock is not fixedhit_test @2x rep 4: pass median 0.1682 ms against 0.3903-0.3904 ms in the other four reps, with identical frames (hash) and identical inkdocs/lights/benchmarks/l174/reps.tsv. One child in 110 ran the same pass 2.3x faster, so the GPU was in a higher clock state; the five-rep median absorbs it and a single run could have reported either.
what it settled
  • THE INCUMBENT'S WHOLE FRAME IS UNDER A MILLISECOND. Every one of the eleven real showcase frames costs 0.17-0.98 ms of CPU and 0.13-0.67 ms of GPU pass at 1920x1080 points, at both scales. Every earlier statement about lights as a UI renderer was implicitly compared against a 16.67 ms budget; the incumbent spends about 6% of it. L-176's comparison has to be read against this row, not against the budget.
  • EGUI'S CPU COST IGNORES THE SCALE FACTOR; A CPU RASTERISER'S CANNOT. From 1x to 2x egui's CPU frame moves 0.99-1.09x while its GPU pass grows 2.64-3.06x for 4.00x the pixels. A renderer that rasterises on the CPU pays that pixel factor on the CPU, so on a Retina display the gap between the stacks widens by up to four times before any other term is counted.
  • WHAT A PORT REPLACES IS NOT WHAT THE FRAME COSTS. Layout (App::update inside Context::run) is optical's own code and stays in any port; tessellation, upload calls, encoding and the GPU pass are what lights would replace. In the op-heavy overview the replaceable part is 78% of the CPU frame (tessellation 0.3305 + upload 0.2986 of 0.8092 ms); in the fill-heavy canvas 55% is layout that no renderer removes. L-176 must hold the layout term fixed or it will credit or charge lights for optical's widget code.
  • THE CANVAS IS NOT FEW HUGE OPS. The entry expected a canvas frame to be few huge ops. Optical's canvas tab has 581 visible ops (second of eleven), draws 2.58 screens of coverage (the most) and holds 47 MB of textures, and it is the dearest frame on every column. The frame that IS few huge ops is an empty one: every tab's largest op is the central panel's background, so mean area per op is about one screen over the op count and crowns the emptiest developer tabs (the first draft of the role rule did exactly that).
  • A FENCE AROUND SUBMIT MEASURES LATENCY. Submit to completion is a flat 1.53-1.54 ms in all 22 rows whatever the pass costs (0.13-0.67 ms). Any GPU figure taken with a stopwatch around queue.submit on this machine reports mostly command-buffer scheduling.
  • ON THIS GPU THE TEXTBOOK TIMESTAMP RESOLVE READS ZERO. Resolving the pass's timestamps in the same command buffer as the pass returned 0, or a value before the begin sample, for the end-of-fragment sample on every frame; the harness refused (end <= begin) instead of publishing. A probe showed the pair is valid when resolved in a later command buffer after the fence (0.076-0.113 ms for 200 rects), that each index is written only when requested, and that encoder-level write_timestamp writes nothing at all on wgpu 24 Metal here.
  • THE SHOWCASE FRAME READ THE WALL CLOCK. The frame-time readout in the showcase's bottom panel came from Instant::elapsed, so a frame's text depended on how fast the previous frame ran: the first smoke run had canvas @2x non-deterministic by one glyph in one frame of one rep. It now reads egui's input clock (the same interval in the real app, the scripted 1/60 s in the bench), and all 22 scene-scale pairs hash alike over five reps and three runs.
what it refuted
  • L-174's next, 'a canvas frame (few huge ops)'. Optical's canvas tab is 581 visible ops, the second most of eleven tabs, with the largest covered area (5,350,446 px at 1x, 2.58 screens). The three scenes are named instead on the three axes a CPU rasteriser is judged on: text share, covered area and op count.
  • L-174's where, 'the Painter::paint(&mut self, ui: &mut egui::Ui) contract, 105 impls'. base.rs:73 is right and the count is not: 122 lines in crates/optical/src match impl ... Painter for, 116 in the library and 6 in the showcase binary.
what it does NOT settle
  • PRESENTATION. The target is an offscreen texture: surface acquire, present, the compositor and vsync are in no number here. Neither is winit's event pump.
  • REACTIVE REPAINT. egui produces a frame only on input or a repaint request; these are costs per produced frame, not per second of use.
  • INTERACTION. The script sweeps the pointer and nothing else: no drag, scroll, typing, animation-heavy frame, or frame in which the font atlas grows (texture upload is 0 B in the steady state measured).
  • THE GPU CLOCK. One rep in 110 ran a pass 2.3x faster than its siblings. The medians are at whatever clock a light load leaves the GPU in; a GPU under sustained load may run these passes faster.
  • OTHER MACHINES, OSES AND BACKENDS. One Apple M5 Max on Metal. Linux and Windows are L-187's; no Vulkan or DX12 number exists.
  • THE DEBUG BUILD. Release only, as the close bar asks; the entry's 4x debug factor was measured on lights (l150), not on optical.
  • WHOSE RESIDENT BYTES. RSS is the whole child process; the Metal driver, egui's state and the showcase's own state are not separated.
  • COVERAGE VERSUS BOUNDING BOXES. op_area_px sums each visible op's bounding box clipped to its clip rect. It is an upper bound on the pixels a rasteriser touches, not their count.
  • OTHER SCALE FACTORS. Only 1 and 2 pixels per point; 1.5 and 3 are common and unmeasured.

What does lights' DrawList cost on a real optical frame? The eleven showcase frames L-174 measured, replayed op for op through DrawList::render, serially and over rayon row bands, at 1x and 2x, f32 and f64.

engineering2026-09-29copy-pasteable command2 refuted
the answer

THE FRAME IS PAID IN PIXELS: 5.0-6.4 ns PER BOUNDING-BOX PIXEL, AND UNDER 0.02 ms FOR ALL THE OPS. Replayed op for op, every real optical frame costs the serial DrawList 11.0252-28.4196 ms at 1x (f32) and 44.1008-108.1929 ms at 2x, so at chrome scale the serial number is outside 16.67 ms for three of eleven tabs at 1x and for all eleven at 2x. Over 16-row bands on 18 threads, bit-identical to serial, it is 1.3679-2.9135 ms at 1x and 4.4938-10.1802 ms at 2x: inside the budget everywhere, and still 2.4-8.3x the incumbent's WHOLE CPU frame (L-174) at 1x while it occupies every core. The parallel version is therefore the first arm L-176 measures.

the question it was built to answer

BURNDOWN L-175 (2026-09-29): the display list is the arm P2 says should win on crisp UI content, and it had only ever been timed on a 64x64 P2 target (10.2-38.9 us). What does DrawList::render cost on the frames optical actually draws - the eleven showcase tabs whose incumbent cost L-174 recorded - at 1920x1080 points, 1x and 2x, f32 and f64, serially and with rayon over tile rows? Is that cost a function of covered area or of op count? L-176 is gated on the answer: if the serial number is already outside a 16.67 ms budget at chrome scale, the parallel version is the first arm L-176 measures rather than an afterthought.

criterion, and its convention

Seven reps per job after one discarded warm-up, rep-major (every job once per rep); each figure is the median over reps with min..max beside it, and a 1.3x difference is noise on this machine. A job is one call: DrawList::render_through (serial, on the calling thread) or DrawList::render_through_parallel (16-row bands on rayon's global pool of 18 threads), timed with the returned RasterLayer dropped inside the clock, so allocation and free are in. Four arms per list separate the terms: fixed (an empty list at the same viewport), touch (the same two planes allocated, every pixel written once, freed), opsx (the list repeated 32 times through a view that moves every footprint off the frame: every per-op step and no pixel) and full. per_op_ns = (opsx - fixed) / (32 x ops); px_ms = full - fixed - ops_ms; per_px_ns = px_ms / visited_px, where visited_px sums DrawOp::footprint, the exact rectangle the render iterates. Two lists per frame: native (the ops DrawList has a shape for) and framed (native plus proxies: a glyph as its quad, a mesh or text triangle as a flat polygon, a blurred or textured rect as a flat rect of its reach). Gate: before any time is taken every list is rendered in both modes and the bits of both planes hashed, and one differing hash refuses the run.

reproduce it
cargo run -p optical --release --bin unified_showcase -- --frame-bench --reps 1 --out /tmp/l175_frame_scratch --ops-out docs/lights/benchmarks/l175/ops
cargo run -p lights --release --all-features --bin lights-ui-frame -- --ops docs/lights/benchmarks/l175/ops --out docs/lights/benchmarks/l175/drawlist.tsv

Run 2026-09-29 on an Apple M5 Max (18 cores, 128 GB, macOS 26.5), release profile. The first command is L-174's harness with one rep and the new --ops-out: it writes the LAST measured frame of every tab at both scales as a capture, 22 files (its own timing table goes to /tmp and is not this record's). The second replays the captures: 176 rows, 7 reps after one discarded warm-up, about 50 s. The instruments are tested by cargo test -p optical --bin unified_showcase -- bench:: and cargo test -p optical --test frame_bench (the capture), and by cargo test -p lights --all-features --test draw_list --test ui_capture --test ui_frame (the parallel render, the footprint, the parser, the replay and this record).

RE-RUN 2026-09-29 (L-199). The capture half (the first command) is pinned to 8e9a15ba for the reason the l174 record gives - d9ea50fe deleted optical's frame bench - and run there it rewrote all 22 op files byte-identical to docs/lights/benchmarks/l175/ops. The replay half runs on the tree: every deterministic column of drawlist.tsv (ops, native, proxy, absent, refused, visited_px, inked_px, frame_hash) identical in all 176 rows. lights-ui-frame writes --out without creating its parent directory, so a scratch path must exist first; it refuses with exit 4 after the reps otherwise.

the least-squares cross-check over the 44 f32 serial rows (ARITHMETIC on the committed table)full_ms = -1.113 ms + 964.5 ns per op + 5.431 ns per visited pixel, R2 0.98479, largest residual 13.049 msOrdinary least squares of full_ms on ops and visited_px over drawlist.tsv's f32 serial rows. The pixel coefficient agrees with the direct 5.0-6.4 ns; the op coefficient is 150-700x the direct 1.4-6.2 ns, because across frames op count is confounded with shape mix. It is why the opsx arm exists.

It is the evidence that the committed serial figure is today's renderer rather than a faster rewrite of it; at f64 the rewrite IS about 10% faster, and the f64 serial figures above are the new path's.
the captures against L-174's censusten tabs match L-174's per-frame means to within the larger of 2 ops and 1% on every counted kind at both scales; the timeline's last frame has 675 ops against a mean of 557docs/lights/benchmarks/l175/ops/*.tsv #census against docs/lights/benchmarks/l174/baseline.tsv. what it settled
  • THE FRAME IS PAID IN PIXELS, NOT OPS. The per-op term is 1.4-6.2 ns and at most 0.0168 ms per serial frame; the pixel term is 97.8-99.2% of every serial frame at 5.0-6.4 ns per visited pixel (f32). At optical's op counts (13 to 2,695 per list) the question 'how many ops' does not move the frame, and 'how many pixels do the ops' bounding boxes cover' decides it.
  • EVERY FRAME PAYS FOR AT LEAST ONE WHOLE SCREEN. The emptiest tab (tree: 13 native ops) visits 2,105,772 pixels at 1x, more than the screen's 2,073,600, because the central panel's background covers it; so the serial floor for any optical frame is about 11 ms at 1x and 44 ms at 2x before a single widget is drawn. The incumbent fills the same background inside a GPU pass of 0.13 ms (L-174).
  • SERIAL IS OUTSIDE THE BUDGET AT CHROME SCALE, SO L-176 MEASURES THE PARALLEL VERSION FIRST. Three of eleven tabs exceed 16.67 ms at 1x (canvas 28.4196, graph 24.2293, overview 17.8188) and all eleven at 2x (44.1008-108.1929 ms), the scale this machine's display runs at. That is the condition L-175's next wrote down in advance for making the parallel list L-176's first arm.
  • ROW BANDS ARE EXACT, BUY 7.9-10.8x ON 18 THREADS, AND THEN THE FRAME BUFFER IS A FIFTH TO TWO FIFTHS OF THE COST. The serial render is now the one-band case of the banded kernel, so the parallel render is bit-identical by construction and by test (eight band heights through three views in tests/draw_list.rs; the hash gate over all 88 real lists). Once the pixels are split 18 ways, the allocation, rayon's dispatch and the first touch of two fresh planes (0.518-0.5917 ms at 1x) are 18.7-42.4% of the parallel frame against 2.6-6.7% of the serial one: L-178's reusable target is worth far more to the parallel path.
  • AN UNTOUCHED ALLOCATION IS FREE AND THE BILL ARRIVES AT FIRST TOUCH. Allocating and freeing two zeroed 1080p f32 planes (49,766,400 B) costs 0.2111-0.2665 ms because the zero pages are mapped lazily; writing every pixel once costs a further 0.4155-0.5319 ms serially (0.9849-1.0687 ms at f64, 2.0136-2.2179 ms at 2x f32). A render pays that inside its pixel term, which is why the touch arm exists.
  • EVEN IN PARALLEL THE LIST IS DEARER THAN EGUI'S WHOLE CPU FRAME. At 1x the parallel DrawList is 2.4-8.3x egui's entire CPU frame (layout, tessellation, upload calls and encoding, 0.1654-0.9011 ms) while occupying 18 threads to egui's one, and it has not yet paid the pack to RGBA8 (l150: 7.444 ms serial). The lights half of L-176's kill condition holds for the serial list at 2x (it loses by 43.932-107.2137 ms, more than a frame) and does not hold for the parallel one (it loses by 1.1227-2.0309 ms at 1x and 4.3259-9.201 ms at 2x); the condition now turns on the tiny-skia arm.
  • A FIT ACROSS FRAMES CANNOT SEPARATE THE TERMS. Least squares over the 44 f32 serial rows returns 964.5 ns per op, 150-700x the directly measured cost, with R2 0.98479: the frames with many ops are the frames with polygons, whose SDF is linear in their vertex count (canvas pays 6.094-6.374 ns per pixel against 4.999-5.373 in every other tab). Only an arm that holds the pixels at zero, like opsx, measures the op term.
  • WHAT A PIXEL PAYS FOR. Every pixel of every op's clipped bounding box evaluates the whole DrawOp::coverage_through: the inverse similarity (Similarity2::invert computes the rotation's sin_cos on every call), the shape's SDF, the clip fraction and the premultiplied over, whether the pixel lies on an edge or deep inside a rect. This record measures the constant and does not measure what a span fill for interiors, a hoisted transform, SIMD or a reused target would remove; it claims none of them.
what it refuted
  • L-175's where, 'one sdf per covered pixel': one SDF per VISITED pixel. The render evaluates coverage at every pixel of the op's clipped bounding box and skips only the composite where coverage is zero; across the 176 rows it visits 1.008-1.381x the pixels it inks.
  • The census prose in optical (census_rs_comment/the_counters.md) called op_area_px 'the term a CPU rasteriser's cost is proportional to' without a measurement. It is the right term and a fair proxy: L-174's op_area_px is 1.03x the list's visited pixels in tree and 0.97x in overview, but 1.21x in canvas (4,423,698 visited against 5,350,446), where egui's visual bounds include text and images the native list does not draw.
what it does NOT settle
  • TEXT. DrawList cannot draw a glyph. The framed list's glyph boxes are a floor on text's fill term; the cost of text through PrimitiveSet::render, and of putting it in the same list as the rect behind it, is L-183's.
  • IMAGES, GRADIENTS, MESHES. The canvas's 18 textured triangles (its images) and 1,014 vertex-coloured ones are flat polygons here (L-181, L-182).
  • CLIP STACKS. Each op carries egui's final clip rect, already intersected by egui; how optical's nested painter_at clips reach a lights list is L-180's.
  • GEOMETRY DETAIL. Inside and outside rect strokes (352 and 31 of the 394 at 1x) are drawn by moving the centred outline half a width, which the capture parser does; three rects with unequal corners take the largest; two inverted rects in the overview (-0.33 and -8.3 pt wide, which egui feathers into slivers) are refused; path strokes ignore their stroke kind; segments have round caps where egui's are butt. None of these moves more than a few hundred pixels and none was measured.
  • THE PRESENT. The frame ends as two float planes. The pack to RGBA8 (l150), any upload and any present are in no number here.
  • THREADS AND BANDS. One band height (16 rows) and one pool (rayon's global, 18 threads) were measured. Neither was swept, and the parallel figures occupy every core, which a UI thread shares with the rest of the application.
  • THE TIMELINE GROWS. The timeline tab adds about four line ops per frame of the script (439 ops at frame 30, 799 at frame 120), so its cost here and in L-174 is a function of how long the tab has run. The mechanism was not traced: the demo's own recording buttons are never clicked by the harness.
  • OTHER FRAMES AND MACHINES. One frame per tab, the last of L-174's script; a frame that scrolls, animates or opens a popup is not here. One Apple M5 Max.

Is lights justified as the UI renderer? L-175's eleven optical frames replayed op for op through lights' DrawList and through tiny-skia, serially and over rayon row bands, at 1x and 2x, with the registered edge criterion re-measured on each renderer's own render.

engineering2026-09-29copy-pasteable command2 refuted
the answer

NOT ON FILL RATE AND NOT ON MEMORY. On the identical op sets tiny-skia draws every frame 2.22-10.16x faster than lights' DrawList (in parallel at 1x, 2.48-4.83x on the framed lists and 3.14-6.55x once lights pays its pack), from a 4 B/px buffer against lights' 24 B/px, with no pack at all. Lights keeps two things: the registered quality gate at 1x, which tiny-skia fails on the glyph-like target because it draws a 1 px stroke as a hairline (peak 0.6157 against the 0.90 bar, where lights reads 0.9369 and the exact area 0.9226), and bands that are bit-exact, where tiny-skia's depart by up to 191 levels. KILL CONDITION DOES NOT HOLD: its first clause needs lights and tiny-skia within noise of each other, and they are 2.2-10.2x apart in tiny-skia's favour; and in parallel neither CPU arm loses to epaint's renderer CPU by more than the 16.67 ms budget.

the question it was built to answer

BURNDOWN L-176 (2026-09-29): using lights must be justified against the alternative in speed AND memory, not assumed (PM). L-174 measured the incumbent (egui/epaint on wgpu) and L-175 the display list on the same eleven real showcase frames. The arm that decides whether lights earns its place or merely reimplements a solved problem is tiny-skia, a mature CPU rasteriser: on the SAME op sets, at 1x and 2x, serially and in parallel, with allocation, first touch, ops, pixels and the handoff to an RGBA8 texture kept separate, and with quality re-measured from each renderer's own fresh render against Criterion::registered, how does it compare with lights, and does the kill condition written into L-176 before the run hold?

criterion, and its convention

Timing as L-175: seven reps a job after one discarded warm-up, rep-major, each figure the median with min..max beside it, and a 1.3x difference is noise on this machine. Both renderers draw the SAME DrawOps, the lists Capture::draw_list builds, lights at f32 and tiny-skia from a device-space conversion made once outside the clock. Serial is one band the height of the frame on the calling thread; parallel is 16-row bands on rayon's global pool of 18 threads, for tiny-skia each band a PixmapMut over its own rows of the one buffer. Arms fixed, touch, opsx and full are L-175's; pack is lights' handoff from its two float planes to RGBA8 (serial: FloatImage::to_clamped_u8 then an RGBA expansion; parallel: one fused banded pass), and tiny-skia's is 0 because its buffer already is premultiplied RGBA8. Gates in the harness, before any clock: lights' two modes hash alike, and tiny-skia's bands paint within 1% of its serial frame. Checked on the table by tests/renderer_arms.rs: the lights rows hash as L-175's f32 rows did, and tiny-skia paints within 1% of lights on every frame. Quality: Criterion::registered via measure_target_mapped on each arm's fresh render of the six canonical targets at 1x and 2x, with fit::exact_coverage as the calibration row. The kill condition, as L-176 wrote it before the run and as tests/renderer_arms.rs evaluates it: it holds if lights with its pack and tiny-skia are within 1.3x of each other AND both exceed epaint's renderer CPU (L-174 tess + prep + encode, layout held fixed) by more than 16.67 ms, on the three named scenes' framed lists in parallel, at 1x and 2x.

reproduce it
cargo run -p lights --release --all-features --example renderer_arms -- --ops docs/lights/benchmarks/l175/ops --out docs/lights/benchmarks/l176

Run 2026-09-29 on an Apple M5 Max (18 cores, 128 GB, macOS 26.5), release profile, 30 s of wall time: 176 timing rows (11 tabs x 2 scales x 2 lists x 2 renderers x 2 modes, 7 reps after one discarded warm-up) and 30 quality rows. The captures are L-175's, unchanged. The instruments are tested by cargo test -p lights --all-features --test renderer_arms (10 tests: the converter shape by shape, clips, bands, the pack, the six targets, and the two tables and this record).

what it settled
  • TINY-SKIA DRAWS THE SAME OPS 2.2-10.2x FASTER. On every one of the 88 lists, in both modes and at both scales, tiny-skia's frame is 2.22-10.16x cheaper than the display list's, and the gap widens with pixels (serially over both lists: 3.34-8.22x at 1x, 4.24-10.16x at 2x). The pixel term explains it: lights evaluates a whole signed distance and a clip fraction at every pixel of every bounding box (4.11-10.84 ns per frame pixel serially), where tiny-skia fills an axis-aligned rect by exact area and a path by a 4-sub-scanline supersampled span blitter (0.41-1.88 ns). Fill rate does not justify lights as a chrome renderer.
  • THE BUFFER IS SIX TIMES SMALLER AND THE HANDOFF IS FREE. tiny-skia writes 4 B/px of premultiplied RGBA8, which is the texture's format; lights writes 24 B/px of float planes and then owes a pack, 5.7430-6.0190 ms serially at 1x (0.3872-0.4969 ms as a fused parallel pass). Allocation and first touch scale with the buffer: 0.0953-0.0996 ms for tiny-skia's allocation plus first touch at 1x against lights' 0.5184-0.5308.
  • LIGHTS HOLDS THE QUALITY GATE AT 1x WHERE TINY-SKIA DOES NOT. tiny-skia draws any stroke 1 device pixel wide or thinner as a hairline whose alpha is scaled by its width, so the glyph-like target's 1 px arc and stems read peak 0.6157 against the 0.90 bar; lights reads 0.9369 and the exact area 0.9226, so the failure is the renderer's and not the criterion's. At 2x the same strokes are 2 px, stroked as paths, and pass (peak 1.0000). Edges are otherwise a wash: tiny-skia's 10-90 transition is within 0.029 px of the exact area's (0.7524, 0.7915, 0.8084 against 0.7806, 0.7834, 0.8365 on the edge, the corner and the curve) and lights' is 0.017-0.024 px wider (0.8000, 0.8000, 0.8605).
  • TINY-SKIA IS NOT EXACT UNDER A BAND CUT, AND LIGHTS IS. The parallel tiny-skia is the harness's construction (tiny-skia has no threads) and costs exactness: 16-row bands depart from the serial frame by 25-191 levels at 8-7,075 pixels, and in one-row bands every hairline vanishes. Lights' bands are its serial frame bit for bit (L-175). What that buys is determinism across thread counts, the property L-121 keeps lights headless for; it is not speed.
  • KILL CONDITION DOES NOT HOLD, AND IT FAILS IN A DIRECTION IT DID NOT ANTICIPATE. It was written for lights and tiny-skia landing within noise of each other and both losing to epaint by more than a frame; instead tiny-skia beats lights by more than the noise everywhere (3.14-8.82x with lights' pack on the named scenes in parallel), and in parallel neither loses to epaint's renderer CPU by more than 10.71 ms. So the pre-written outcome (a raster path for chrome, lights for canvas content, text and zoom) is not triggered by the letter of the condition, and the question it was guarding is answered anyway: a raster path already exists that is faster and smaller than lights on chrome. Whether chrome moves to it is a scope ruling, L-196.
  • AGAINST THE INCUMBENT, TINY-SKIA IS CLOSE AND LIGHTS IS NOT. On the named scenes in parallel at 1x, tiny-skia's frame (0.2537-0.7928 ms) runs from 0.0274 ms under epaint's renderer CPU (overview) to 0.4468 ms over it (canvas) (epaint: 0.1074-0.6654 ms, which also pays a 0.1290-0.2482 ms GPU pass), while lights with its pack costs 1.5705-3.0180 ms. Both CPU arms occupy 18 threads to epaint's one, and at 2x both grow with pixels where epaint's CPU does not (L-174).
  • THE CONVERTER DRAWS THE SAME FRAMES, WHICH IS WHAT MAKES THE SPEED COMPARABLE. tiny-skia departs from lights' packed frame by at most 0.0905 levels per channel on average, paints within 0.026% on every frame, and matches it shape by shape within 1% of ink on nine shapes at two scales (tests/renderer_arms.rs). The lights arm hashes exactly as L-175's did, so it IS L-175's renderer, re-timed in the same process as its competitor.
what it refuted
  • L-176's why: tiny-skia is 'a mature CPU rasteriser with analytic coverage, which is the SAME bet lights makes'. tiny-skia 0.11.4 fills a path by supersampling (scan/path_aa.rs:21, SUPERSAMPLE_SHIFT = 2, four sub-scanlines a pixel), fills an axis-aligned rect by exact area (scan::fill_rect_aa, hairline_aa::fill_rect), and draws a stroke of 1 device pixel or less as an alpha-scaled hairline (painter.rs treat_as_hairline). Only the rect path is analytic; the bet is scan conversion, and the hairline is where the quality table separates the two.
  • L-176's next, 'the condition now turns on the tiny-skia arm': it did, but not as framed. The condition's first clause presumed the two CPU renderers land within noise of each other; they are 2.2-10.2x apart.
what it does NOT settle
  • EPAINT ON THESE OP SETS. epaint cannot be an arm of this harness: lights may not take a wgpu or egui dependency even for a test (tests/layering.rs, PM ruling L-121). The incumbent here is L-174's measured frame, which draws real text through its atlas where these lists draw glyph boxes. epaint replayed on the identical lists, and its quality from a GPU readback of the six targets, is L-194.
  • THE GPU COMPUTE RASTERISER. L-176 named it optional and it was not run: L-195.
  • TEXT. Neither CPU renderer draws a glyph here: the framed lists carry each glyph as its box. tiny-skia's text path and lights' (L-183) are not compared.
  • UPLOAD AND PRESENT. Both CPU arms end in an RGBA8 buffer (lights after its pack). The upload of 7.91 MiB a frame at 1x (31.64 MiB at 2x) and the present are in no number.
  • THREADS. tiny-skia is single-threaded; its parallel rows are the harness's 16-row bands on rayon's 18 threads, which occupy every core as lights' do. One band height and one pool were measured.
  • OTHER TINY-SKIA SETTINGS. Anti-aliased SourceOver solid colours on the default pipeline. force_hq_pipeline, and a converter that strokes 1 px lines as paths instead of hairlines (which should trade some of tiny-skia's speed for the 1x quality gate), were not measured.
  • WHAT LIGHTS COULD RECOVER. The per-pixel gap is measured, not decomposed: what an interior span fill, a hoisted transform, SIMD or render_into (L-190) would take off lights' pixel term is not claimed.
  • RUN TO RUN. The lights rows reproduce L-175's bits exactly and their times differ from L-175's by up to 1.25x (canvas native serial at 1x: 22.6745 ms here, 28.4196 there). Compare numbers within one run, never across runs.
  • OTHER MACHINES. One Apple M5 Max, macOS 26.5.

tiny-skia 0.11.4 src/painter.rs (fill_rect, fill_path, stroke_path, treat_as_hairline), src/scan/path_aa.rs (SUPERSAMPLE_SHIFT), src/scan/mod.rs (fill_rect_aa), src/mask.rs (Mask::from_vec), src/pixmap.rs (PixmapMut::from_bytes); tiny-skia-path 0.11.4 src/path_builder.rs, src/rect.rs · crates/lights/src/draw.rs (render_through, render_through_parallel, DrawOp::coverage_through), src/capture.rs (Capture::draw_list), src/image.rs (to_clamped_u8 and its truncating quantise), src/fit/edge.rs (Criterion::registered, measure_target_mapped), src/fit/target.rs (Target::all), src/fit/coverage.rs (exact_coverage) · docs/lights/RESEARCH.json l174-optical-egui-baseline, l175-drawlist-ui-frame and l150-cpu-pack-term · crates/lights BURNDOWN L-176, L-177, L-178, L-190, L-192, L-194, L-195, L-196

Should the macOS host take its window and events from AppKit directly or from winit? Both pointer and key paths were written and driven by one synthesized input, and the host's present path was timed at 1920x1080.

engineering2026-09-29copy-pasteable command2 refuted
the answer

APPKIT DIRECTLY. Under one scripted input the AppKit arm delivered every event the contract names, exactly and in the pump that followed the input, in 5 of 5 runs: press (80, 100) px, motion and release (240, 180) px at scale 2, both keys, the resize as 800x520 at scale 2, and the close request with the window left open. winit 0.30.13 in pump mode dropped every window event raised outside its own callback (0 of 3 pointer events and 0 of 1 close requests, 3 of 3 runs). With the same events injected from inside about_to_wait, it delivered them one pump late in 4 of 5 runs, positioned against the next resize's height (y +80 px), and on time in the fifth, where the resize that followed never arrived in its pump. A winit host would add 18 crates to the 7 that the Metal present path needs either way, to save 103 lines of event code. The present path the host keeps costs 0.61-0.86 ms back to back at 1920x1080 and 2.22-2.28 ms (run medians) after an idle, against a 15.4-19.8 ms render of the same frame.

the question it was built to answer

BURNDOWN L-186 (2026-09-29): 'Window and events: AppKit through objc2-app-kit, or winit if its event model fits - decide by writing the pointer and key paths both ways, not on paper.' The platform contract (docs/lights/PLATFORM_CONTRACT.md, L-187) asks for pointer press, motion and release each with a position, a key press naming the key, close and resize as events, a pixel size and a scale factor, and a present path. On macOS that present path is a CAMetalLayer in both arms, because winit exposes no Metal layer.

criterion, and its convention

An arm fits when, under the same synthesized input, it delivers each event the contract names, at the position the input was made at, in the pump that follows the input, in every run of five. Positions are pixels from the top left, compared exactly against points times the screen's own backingScaleFactor. Cost is the crates added to the build graph by NAME on top of lights (cargo tree -e normal), and the lines of event-path code. Present timing is five runs of 20 presents at 1920x1080 px in release, each figure a median with min and max, and a 1.3x difference is noise on this machine.

reproduce it
cargo run -p lights-mac --release --offline --bin lights-mac-probe -- /tmp/l186_probe
cargo run --offline --manifest-path docs/lights/benchmarks/l186/winit_arm/Cargo.toml
L186_INJECT=between cargo run --offline --manifest-path docs/lights/benchmarks/l186/winit_arm/Cargo.toml

Run 2026-09-29 on an Apple M5 Max (18 cores, 128 GB, macOS 26.5) at backingScaleFactor 2: the AppKit probe five times in release, the winit arm five times and three times between pumps in dev. The AppKit arm's contract rows are also asserted by cargo test -p lights-mac (six tests, ten mutation rows), which run_tests.sh now runs.
what it measured
measurementvaluewhere it comes from

what it settled
  • WINIT DOES NOT FIT A LOOP THE CALLER OWNS. In pump mode winit 0.30 has an event handler only while pump_app_events runs. An AppKit callback raised between pumps (a click dispatched, a close button pressed, a size set) finds no handler and its event is dropped with a log line. The AppKit host queues every event in its view's ivars wherever it was raised, and the next pump drains the queue. The demo's loop (render, present, pump) is caller-owned, so under winit it would have to move inside winit's callbacks.
  • THE PRESENT PATH COSTS NO WINIT SAVING. winit exposes a window handle and no CAMetalLayer, so a winit host still needs objc2-app-kit, objc2-foundation, objc2-metal and objc2-quartz-core to attach the layer, which is the 7 crates the AppKit host uses in total. winit is therefore purely additive: 18 crates, including core-graphics and tracing, in exchange for 103 lines of event code.
  • WHAT WINIT DOES BETTER, AND NOTHING NEEDS YET: key naming. winit reports Code(KeyA) with Character("a") and Code(Escape) with Named(Escape). The AppKit host reports the virtual key code and the characters ignoring modifiers (0 and "a", 53 and ESC). No scene of L-197 needs named keys.
  • WINIT'S PRESS CARRIES NO POSITION. MouseInput has none; winit emits CursorMoved immediately before each press (view.rs mouse_down calls mouse_motion then mouse_click), so a consumer must track the last motion. The contract asks for a press with a position, and the AppKit host's PointerPressed carries one.
  • THE HOST'S PRESENT IS SMALL AGAINST THE RENDER. At 1920x1080 in release, back-to-back presents cost 0.61-0.86 ms (run medians) and a present after an idle 2.22-2.28 ms, while rendering the frame costs 15.4-19.8 ms. The idle case is about 3x the warm one. That is consistent with caches and clocks going cold in 40 ms, and not proven to be that. Either way the present is 4-15 percent of the render, so it is not where a frame budget is lost.
  • A SYNTHESIZED CLICK MUST NOT GO THROUGH APPKIT'S QUEUE IN AN INSTRUMENT. Posted and dequeued, its location came back rewritten. Passed straight to NSApp.sendEvent, the call the host's pump makes for each event it dequeues, it arrived exactly. Keys survive the queue unchanged, so the probe keeps them there and the pump's dequeue stays under test.
what it refuted
  • L-186's 'or winit if its event model fits': measured, it does not fit a caller-owned loop. Events raised outside winit's callback are dropped, and events injected inside it arrive at a pump boundary the caller does not choose (4 of 5 runs a pump late).
  • The debug-build reading that back-to-back presents are paced by the display (16.550 ms median, one frame at 60 Hz). In release the same 20 presents cost 0.61-0.86 ms each. The 16.55 ms was the unoptimised expansion loop over 2,073,600 pixels, and the equality with a 60 Hz frame was a coincidence.
what it does NOT settle
  • THE IMAGE ON SCREEN. The probe reads the drawable's texture back after the blit, in the same command buffer. That proves the bytes submitted for display, not the pixels the compositor shows: no screen capture was taken (it needs the Screen Recording permission). Row 0 at the top rests on Metal's top-left texture origin, which is platform knowledge and was not observed here.
  • A HAND. Nobody has driven lights-view with a real pointer. Every event in this record was synthesized.
  • REDRAW DURING A LIVE RESIZE. While the window edge is dragged, AppKit tracks inside sendEvent and the pump does not return, so the last frame is stretched until release. Not measured.
  • WHY BACK-TO-BACK PRESENTS ARE NOT PACED BY THE DISPLAY. Twenty presents in about 15 ms is faster than any refresh rate. Whether the probe window was occluded, or drawables are released before display at this size, was not measured.
  • THE DISPLAY TRANSFER. The drawable is BGRA8Unorm and the bytes are lights' linear-coded ones, so the screen shows them as the demo page's PNGs do. The encode pass L-187's audit proposed is still unmeasured.
  • WINIT UNDER run_app. Only pump mode was measured, because the probe must interleave its own actions. Under run_app a winit host owns the loop and events arrive in order, and that arm was not written.
  • WINDOWS AND LINUX. Deferred by the PM, 2026-09-29.

What does the CPU handoff cost when lights resolves straight into the host's BGRA8 buffer instead of resolving, packing and expanding, and what does that do to the frame on screen?

engineering2026-09-29copy-pasteable command4 refuted
the answer

The fused resolve takes 2.574-2.649 ms median at 1920x1080 in release. The three passes it replaces take 6.843-7.092 ms: resolve_float 1.800-1.833, to_clamped_u8 4.766-4.958, and the host's expansion 0.279-0.287. That is 2.66-2.68x per run, byte-identical, with no allocation. Free-running on the built-in 120 Hz panel, the viewer's 1080p frame now keeps pace with the display at 8.144-8.235 ms between presents. The three-pass frame fell behind at 9.056-9.524 ms, because the CPU part of a frame dropped from 8.156-8.566 to 4.517-4.664 ms.

the question it was built to answer

L-190 asks whether the pack L-150 priced can be removed rather than optimised. The viewer's frame is resolved to float planes, packed to 3-byte RGB, then expanded to 4-byte BGRA by the host: three passes over 2,073,600 pixels after the render, plus two fresh allocations (a 24,883,200 B FloatImage at f32 and a 6,220,800 B Image). On unified memory the host's shared buffer is CPU-writable, so the resolve could write final bytes into it directly. Is the fused path bit-identical, and what does it save, in the handoff and on screen?

criterion, and its convention

Five probe runs of 20 reps each, and three headless runs of 5 reps; spreads are reported and a 1.3x difference is treated as noise, per this crate's measurement rules. Nothing is quoted before it is validated. The probe reads both arms back from the drawable and refuses to write a time unless they are byte-identical. The headless test asserts its two arms' bytes equal before it prints. The bit-identity itself is a test: every pixel, in all three blend modes, at both precisions, for every built method, at three strides, with padding untouched. Timings are medians in ms under the release profile.

reproduce it
cargo build -p lights-mac --release --bin lights-mac-probe
for i in 1 2 3 4 5; do cargo run -p lights-mac --release --bin lights-mac-probe -- /tmp/l190_probe/run$i; done
for i in 1 2 3; do cargo test -p lights --release --all-features --test handoff_pack -- --nocapture --test-threads 1; done

Run 2026-09-29 on an Apple M5 Max (18 cores: 6 P + 12 E, 128 GB, macOS 26.5), on the built-in Liquid Retina XDR at backingScaleFactor 2, with CARGO_TARGET_DIR set to the on-disk target/ that crates/lights/scripts/target_dir.py chose. The probe needs a logged-in session with a window server, and the cargo test needs none. The bit-identity and the contract rows are also asserted by cargo test -p lights --all-features --test handoff_pack --test platform_contract and by cargo test -p lights-mac, which run_tests.sh runs.

bit-identity0 departures over 14,538 synthetic and 54,136 rendered pixels, at three strides eachtests/handoff_pack.rs: 3 blend modes x 2 precisions x 4 sizes, and 2 methods x 4 scene, kernel and precision cases
what it settled
  • THE PACK IS REMOVED, NOT OPTIMISED. The frame's bytes are written once, straight from the accumulators into the buffer the GPU reads, and the float image, the 3-byte image and the expansion are gone from the viewer's frame. The fused pass is 2.66-2.68x faster than the three it replaces. It is serial, the same as they were.
  • BIT-IDENTITY HOLDS BY CONSTRUCTION, AND THE TEST PROVES THE CONSTRUCTION. The WATCH on L-190 was that writing 8-bit in the inner loop changes when rounding happens. For the atom renderer it does not: accumulation stays in float, and the fused pass applies the same quantise to the same B::resolve value the pack would have. What the test catches is every way of doing that wrongly: a swap, rounding, a missing clamp, a sheared stride, a padding write, a short band. Those are nine mutation rows, all CAUGHT.
  • ON THIS PANEL THE FUSED FRAME MAKES THE DISPLAY RATE AND THE THREE-PASS FRAME MISSES IT. With a 2.1 ms render, the CPU part of a frame drops from 8.2-8.6 ms to 4.5-4.7 ms, under the panel's 8.33 ms interval. Free-running, the fused frames arrive at the display's pace (8.144-8.235 ms) and the three-pass frames at 9.056-9.524 ms. The handoff, not the render, decided which side of the display interval the frame fell on.
  • A PRESENT TIMED BACK TO BACK MEASURES THE DISPLAY. The first version of the probe quoted each arm's present separately. The arm whose CPU part was shorter absorbed the wait for a free drawable: fused 4.66-4.95 ms median against three-pass 0.95-1.01 ms, with overlapping minima. The fused arm was paying for being faster. The probe now times only CPU terms per arm, and it measures presentation as the interval between frames.
  • THE EXPANSION L-150 FOUND MISSING IS THE CHEAPEST TERM. The host's RGB-to-BGRA expansion costs 0.279-0.287 ms at 1080p. to_clamped_u8 costs 4.766-4.958 ms and resolve_float 1.800-1.833 ms. The pack's cost was the float-to-byte conversion into a fresh allocation, not the fourth byte.
what it refuted
  • BURNDOWN L-190's why, 'the RGBA expansion every GPU format needs does not exist at all'. It stopped being true with L-186 (25f4bdaf), which wrote it into the host, and it is timed here at 0.279-0.287 ms.
  • L-190's premise that the conversion is 'a SECOND pass over pixels the render loop already visited'. For the atom renderer it was three passes after the render: resolve_float, to_clamped_u8, and the host's expansion. They came with two fresh allocations per frame.
  • l150's 7.444 ms as a figure for this machine today. Its own unchanged command reads 4.643-4.658 ms, so the pack is 28% of a 16.67 ms budget, not 45%. The published figure stands as what was measured on 2026-09-16, and the audit note carries the pair.
  • L-190's proposal that lights own a page-aligned allocation for the host to wrap with a no-copy buffer. The host's own shared buffer is already written in place, so a wrapped allocation would save no copy.
what it does NOT settle
  • WHETHER THE FUSED PASS SHOULD BE PARALLEL, and on which cores. It is serial at 2.574-2.649 ms, now the largest CPU term in the viewer's frame beside a 2.1 ms render on 18 threads. Its bands are disjoint by construction. The experiment is stated in L-190's audit and was not run.
  • WHY THE BACK-TO-BACK PRESENT IS PACED TODAY. L-186 read it at 0.63-0.86 ms and these runs read 7.931-8.238 ms, with the same step and the same host. Neither run recorded the display's state (refresh mode, occlusion, whether it was awake).
  • WHY l150's 7.444 ms DOES NOT REPRODUCE. The code, the test and the profile are unchanged. The 1.50x spread that day against 1.02x today points at a shared machine, and nothing proves it.
  • A TRANSLUCENT TARGET (coverage in A, colour premultiplied), and any byte order but BGRA8. Neither is built, because nothing asks for them. Other hosts are deferred by the platform contract.
  • Any machine but this M5 Max, any panel but its built-in XDR at 2x, the ray-cast method in the probe (the probe renders splat), f64, and frames that change between presents. The probe re-presents a fixed camera.

read from

docs/lights/RESEARCH.json l150-cpu-pack-term (the pack term and its not-settled list) · docs/lights/RESEARCH.json l186-event-path-arms and docs/lights/benchmarks/l186/present.tsv (the host and its present figures) · crates/lights/src/image.rs (resolve_float, to_clamped_u8, quantise, the tile-major Framebuffer) · crates/lights/src/image_rs_comment/framebuffer_layout.md (why a linear walk of the accumulators is tile by tile) · crates/lights-mac/src/lib.rs encode (the shared MTLBuffer, blit, present and wait) · git show 7a4eb243 (the l150 tree; handoff_pack.rs and to_clamped_u8 diffed against HEAD, identical) · system_profiler SPDisplaysDataType (Built-in Liquid Retina XDR Display, 3456 x 2234) · BURNDOWN L-190, L-186, L-187 and L-197

What does a frame cost in the interactive scene gallery at the size it opens at, scene by scene, and which parts of lights can a hand drive?

engineering2026-09-29copy-pasteable command2 refuted
the answer

At the gallery's own render area, 1920x1016 px in release, splat draws the 41-atom demo object in 4.910-5.063 ms of CPU per warm frame (render plus the fused fill into the host's buffer), shown 6.903-7.165 ms after the render starts. The ray caster on the same view takes 21.609-22.290 ms of CPU, 3.2x splat, so it stays off until asked for. The surfel pass is not interactive at any window size: 2.939-3.091 ns per pixel and surfel on one thread, 91.0-96.1 ms for a 255x135 frame of the 900-surfel shell. So that scene renders 135 rows and the window scales them.

the question it was built to answer

L-197 builds the first window a hand can drive: three scenes (orbit, method comparison, surfel lighting), a selector, a caption and a frame time on screen. The close bar's audit asks for the cost per frame at the size a feature is used at. What does each scene cost at the gallery's opening size, and which parts of lights are fast enough to drive by hand?

criterion, and its convention

Five process runs, one frame per step in each. The value is the range of the five, per this crate's measurement rules: five reps at least, the spread reported, and 1.3x treated as noise. The gallery renders only when something changes, so the five reps are five processes, not five frames of one. cpu is the scene's render plus its fill of the host's buffer. shown runs from the start of the render to the return of the present, which waits for the command buffer. The probe spaces its steps with 50 ms pumps, so no present here is back to back, and no figure is an interval paced by the display. Times are in ms, under the release profile.

reproduce it
cargo build -p lights-mac --release --bin lights-gallery
for i in 1 2 3 4 5; do cargo run -q -p lights-mac --release --bin lights-gallery -- --probe /tmp/l197_gallery/run$i 960x600; done
for i in 1 2 3 4 5; do awk -F'\t' -v run=$i '$1 ~ /^(scale|window_points)$/ || $1 ~ /\.(scene|frame_pixels|render_pixels|render_ms|fill_ms|cpu_ms|shown_ms)$/ {print run"\t"$1"\t"$2}' /tmp/l197_gallery/run$i/report.tsv; done > docs/lights/benchmarks/l197/frames.tsv

Run 2026-09-29 on an Apple M5 Max (18 cores: 6 P + 12 E, 128 GB, macOS 26.5), on the built-in display at backingScaleFactor 2, with CARGO_TARGET_DIR set to the on-disk target/ that crates/lights/scripts/target_dir.py chose. It needs a logged-in session with a window server. The probe's steps are the ones cargo test -p lights-mac --test gallery drives, at 480x320 pt in debug, and run_tests.sh runs that.
what it measured
measurementvaluewhere it comes from

what the surfel pass would cost at the render areaabout 5.3 s per frame1920 x 1016 px x 900 surfels x 3.0 ns, from the per-pixel-and-surfel rate above; not run
what it settled
  • THE ATOM SCENES CAN BE DRIVEN BY HAND, AND THE FILL IS HALF THEIR CPU. A warm orbit frame is 4.91-5.06 ms of CPU at 1920x1016. The render is 2.19-2.33 ms on 18 threads, and the serial fused fill is 2.66-2.74 ms. For a small scene the fill that L-190 made one pass is now the larger term. Whether to run it in parallel is the experiment L-190 stated and did not run, and nothing here runs it.
  • A FRESH RENDERER PAYS FOR ITS FIRST FRAME. A renderer's first frame renders in 4.18-4.71 ms, against 2.19-2.33 ms once it is warm, and a process's first present shows at 14.2-15.5 ms. The gallery keeps one renderer per atom scene. It rebuilds it only for a resize or a change of method, which is the cost the platform contract gives for a resize.
  • THE RAY CASTER IS THE ORACLE AT 3.2x. On the same 41-atom view it takes 21.6-22.3 ms of CPU against splat's 6.7-7.0 ms. A hand could still drive it here, at a third of splat's rate, and l123's 10k-atom figures put it further behind. The caption and the status line both say it is off until asked for.
  • THE SURFEL PASS IS NOT INTERACTIVE AT ANY WINDOW SIZE. It tests every surfel against every pixel's ray on one thread, with no binning, at 2.94-3.09 ns per pixel and surfel. At the render area that is about 5.3 s a frame. At 135 rows it is 91-96 ms, about ten frames a second, and the window scales the small frame up. This is recorded for L-198's audit, not fixed: whether the surfel pass is load-bearing enough to earn binning or threads is the audit's call.
  • THE CHROME COSTS NO RENDER TIME. The caption, the selector and the status line are AppKit views that the window server draws. The status line is written after the present, so it falls outside every figure above.
what it refuted
  • L-197's 'NO NEW LIGHTS API is needed for the scenes themselves - only orbit_camera moves out of the demo binary'. orbit_camera circles the demo's ring object and nothing else, and the method view and the surfel shell were private to lights-demo as well. Nine items moved, unchanged, into a new module, lights::scene::showcase: demo_object, RING_ATOMS, orbit_camera, still_camera, shell_camera, surfel_shell, material_shell and SURFEL_SHELL_COUNT are public, and hue stays private. The library gains 122 lines, a 121-line module and one line in scene/mod.rs, and the demo binary loses 109 net. They could not go into scene::demo, the obvious home: tests/layering.rs refused that, because the demo binary is excepted from the distortion-metric ban only while it names no carrier of the path-traced reference, and ::scene::demo is one.
  • PLATFORM_CONTRACT.md's smallest branch for the caption, the window title. A caption of three lines does not fit in a title bar, so the host shows a label above the render area instead.
what it does NOT settle
  • WHAT A CONTINUOUS DRAG REACHES ON THE 120 Hz PANEL. The probe spaces its steps, so nothing here is a back-to-back interval. l190 found back-to-back presents paced at 7.931-8.238 ms, and a warm orbit frame (shown 6.9-7.2 ms) sits just under that.
  • A REAL CLICK ON THE SELECTOR, AND THE LABELS' PIXELS. A background test process cannot make AppKit track a click on a control: its window is never key and its app is never active, and activation was declined. So the probe sets the segment the way a click leaves it. The labels' strings are read, the screen is not.
  • THE GALLERY'S RESIDENT MEMORY. It holds one renderer per atom scene at the window's size. Not recorded.
  • WHETHER THE FILL OR THE SURFEL PASS EARNS THREADS. Both are serial. That is for L-198 and the PM, and it is stated rather than run under the close bar's audit rule.

Verified in the file. docs/lights/P5_RESULTS.md:140-143 still reads, unhedged, "The apparent minification failure of atoms is the missing prefilter, not the representation." The amendment sits BELOW it at :145-166 under "AMENDED 2026-09-09, AFTER THE VERDICT COLUMNS WERE FIXED", with the table that kills half of it: frozen t2, prefiltered() 10-90 goes 0.629 / 0.642 / 0.711 / **1.419** / **1.816** / **2.944** scene units across 8x..0.25x and meets goes yes/yes/yes/**no**/**no**/**no**, against contract() at 0.383-0.573 and yes at every rung.
